## 2026-09-21: PR #34 automatic ODK Validate gate (ADR 0024) at `5e9dfce`; finding 54, run 35619801600 on hold

Preconditions: head `5e9dfce57be48d1d521760060ea3219c545a1a92` is exactly one commit on accepted main `92ec5d3`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti. Main CI 35385815996 passed and main has not moved. Exactly the seven declared files change; the `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` trees and all four package and lock files are byte-identical to main; the pin 1d0edc1 appears twice.

Local, in a detached worktree: root 252 of 252, service unit 206 of 206, integration 1 passed and 1 skipped, lint, typecheck, format, scaffold, production audit with zero results, `git diff --check`, 8 interchange fixtures and the XLSX fixture digest. Public CI 35619801599 passed.

Finding 54, low severity: `decodeValidationResult` accepts an ordinary `Proxy`. Both `new Proxy({ ok: true }, {})` and a proxy whose `getPrototypeOf` trap returns `Object.prototype` over a null-prototype target produce a full package. The trap case defeats the decoder's own prototype check, and `Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptors` runs traps before that check. No security impact was found: values are snapshotted into descriptors and read once, so there is no time-of-check gap, and `code` and `location` must still match the fixed allowlist, so a proxy conveys nothing a plain object could not. It is reported because the brief requires refusing proxies and because `types.isProxy` is already the house rule at four other input boundaries, in `compiler/definition.ts` twice, `interchange/bundle.ts` and `interchange/xlsform-tables.ts` twice. Bounded fix: import `types` from `node:util`, add `types.isProxy(value)` to the first guard in `decodeValidationResult` and `types.isProxy(diagnosticDescriptor.value)` to the diagnostic guard, with two unit cases.

Everything else in the brief holds, across 173 probe checks plus mutation and genuine-tool runs. The string handed to the validator is the exact string embedded in package JSON and covered by `xformSha256`, and the validator runs once. Forty hostile result shapes are refused without running a getter or trap and without leaking a supplied value, while the five documented code and location pairs pass through as fresh frozen records. Every refusal returns only `ok`, `diagnostics` and `requestCount` with exact counts. Authoring, mapping, compile, workbook, provider and target refusals all precede validation; as ADR 0024 documents, an invalid XForm is refused after Describe and therefore costs one request per target.

Adapter: relative Java or JAR paths refuse before any process starts; a missing JAR, a directory, an empty file, one flipped byte, a truncated copy and a 65 MiB file all fail closed without disclosing paths or XML; configuration mutated after construction is ignored. Overwriting the configured JAR while validation was in flight did not affect the run, which completed from the verified copy, and the next call refused the replaced file with `PUBLICATION_VALIDATOR_PIN`. Exit 0 is valid, exits 1, 2, 3, 42 and 137 are invalid, and a missing executable, timeout, overflow and output split across both streams are infrastructure. With the cap lowered to 200 ms against the real JVM the adapter refused in 233 ms, removed its workspace and left no orphan Java process. A directory name full of shell metacharacters ran as one literal argument with no side effect. No temporary directory survived any path, and with `rm` forced to throw an otherwise valid form returned `PUBLICATION_VALIDATOR_CLEANUP`. Package bytes and digests are identical to those from accepted main, inputs are unmutated, and omitting the validator argument fails closed.

Genuine ODK Validate 1.20.0: the JAR available on this machine hashes to the pinned digest exactly. It accepted four compiled fixtures and the largest form the compiler will emit at 157,383 characters, in about half a second under the 256 MiB heap, and refused malformed XML, a non-XForm document, an empty string, an invalid XPath bind, a duplicate instance id and a truncated form.

Note 55, informational: on Windows an abnormally terminated JVM reports exit code 1 with no signal, so the classifier records an invalid XForm rather than an infrastructure failure. On Linux, where the service runs, the signal is reported and the classification is correct. Worth one sentence in the ADR.

Note 56, informational: on Windows libuv adds `HOMEDRIVE`, `HOMEPATH`, `LOGONSERVER`, `SYSTEMDRIVE`, `USERDOMAIN`, `USERNAME` and `USERPROFILE` to any child environment regardless of the code's allowlist, confirmed by a direct spawn carrying one variable. No credential-shaped variable reaches the child; the only exposure is the local account name to the pinned JAR.

Note 57, informational: the JAR on this machine matches the pin exactly, but both the file and the pin originate from the builder's download, and nothing outside the repository was fetched during this review. The digest should be confirmed once against ODK's published release checksum.

Note 58, informational: ODK Validate 1.20.0 accepts a bind that points at a node absent from the instance, so this gate proves JavaRosa parsing and XPath validity rather than structural completeness, consistent with what ADR 0024 already disclaims about runtime behaviour.

Documentation matches the code, and note 53 is correctly untouched.

Finding 54 closed. The `types.isProxy` guard is placed before any descriptor read, so a proxy is rejected before its traps can run, and the nested guard runs before the prototype check. Thirteen proxy shapes at both levels were refused as `PUBLICATION_VALIDATOR_FAILURE` at `validator`, with no package, exact request counts and no leakage: ordinary proxies over valid true and false results, the prototype-lying proxy that previously passed, a descriptor-forging proxy fabricating `ok` through `ownKeys` and `getOwnPropertyDescriptor`, revoked and trap-throwing proxies at the top level, ordinary, prototype-lying, revoked and trap-throwing proxies in the nested diagnostic, and proxies wrapping proxies at both levels. With every trap instrumented, the only trap that fired was a single `get` of `then`, which is the thenable lookup `await` performs on any value; the decoder touched nothing.

Plain results are unaffected: a valid result still succeeds with the `odk-validate-1.20.0` label, documented refusals still surface their own codes, a frozen plain object still succeeds and a null-prototype plain object is still refused. The full package suite passes 112 of 112 on this head.

Observation, not a defect: a validator may return a thenable, including a proxy acting as one, and `await` resolves it before decoding, so a proxy can only enter by resolving to a genuine plain object, which conveys nothing extra. A throwing `then` accessor is caught as `PUBLICATION_VALIDATOR_FAILURE`.

ADR 0024 now records note 55 accurately, including that adapter-initiated timeout and output-limit terminations remain infrastructure failures because the adapter records that it initiated them, which matches the `forcedFailure` flag. Preconditions: head `7997324d200ee60b42d768a626b33a885d5e5d79` is exactly one commit on accepted main `aa80c5c`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti. Local, in a detached worktree: root 252 of 252, service unit 216 of 216, integration 1 passed and 1 skipped, lint, typecheck, format, scaffold, production audit with zero results and `git diff --check`. Public CI 35646852421 passed.

Probes, 149 checks plus targeted follow-ups:

- Configuration: fifteen hostile shapes refused at construction, including ordinary, prototype-lying and revoked proxies, null prototypes, class instances, extra and symbol keys, each missing key and an accessor-valued key whose getter never ran.
- Origins: three realistic origins accepted; twenty-four refused, including HTTP, trailing slash, explicit `:443`, path, query, fragment, credentials, `evilsalesforce.com`, `salesforce.com.evil.test`, a trailing-dot FQDN, loopback IPv4 and IPv6, `file:` and `javascript:`, whitespace padding, uppercase, a non-443 port, a punycode lookalike and a 2,100-character host.
- Timeouts: zero, negative, fractional, NaN, Infinity, string, unsafe-integer and over-cap values refused on both timers, an overall shorter than per-request refused, and the exact maxima and an equal pair accepted.
- Requests: the URL is exactly `/services/data/v64.0/sobjects/<name>/describe` and 64.0 matches `salesforce/sfdx-project.json` and the Apex metadata. GET with exactly `accept` and `authorization`, `cache: no-store`, `credentials: omit`, `redirect: manual`, an abort signal, no body and no retry. Seventeen malformed names, including traversal, slash, query suffix, leading digit, leading underscore, 256 characters and non-strings, are refused before token acquisition or network I/O; 255 characters is accepted; each call takes a fresh token and makes exactly one fetch.
- Tokens: empty, whitespace, newline, carriage return, tab, control character, non-ASCII, over-length, null, numeric and object values refused before the fetch, so Bearer header injection is unreachable; 4,096 characters accepted; a throwing provider gives a bounded silent failure.
- Responses: 201, 204, 301, 302, 401, 403, 500, missing content type, `text/html`, `application/json-patch+json`, `text/json`, `application/ld+json`, over-cap or malformed declared length, empty body, malformed JSON, trailing junk, invalid UTF-8 under fatal decoding, a forced `redirected` flag, a throwing fetch and a non-Response return are all refused. `application/json` with parameters, any case and surrounding whitespace is accepted. The size boundary is exact: 8,388,608 bytes succeed and 8,388,609 fail; a declared 8 MiB is accepted and one more refused; a 9 MiB chunked body is refused with an absent or falsely small declared length.
- Redirects: a 301 to `https://evil.test/` is refused after exactly one fetch, so no second request or Authorization header exists.
- Deadlines: hanging fetch, token provider and body read each refuse within the per-request budget; the timeout aborts the fetch signal and cancels a locked reader; the shared overall deadline lets two 500 ms calls through a 1,200 ms budget and refuses the third, a later call fails immediately with no I/O, and a fresh adapter starts a fresh deadline. Three concurrent calls each get their own controller. No unhandled rejection and no surviving timer handle. A fetch that ignores the abort and resolves 400 ms late still refused on time, and the orphaned continuation cancelled that late response's body itself because the signal was already aborted.
- Composition: real Account metadata flows through the transport into the ADR 0021 normalizer; hostile-but-parsable JSON is still refused by the normalizer rather than the transport; failures become `PUBLISH_DESCRIBE_REQUEST` at `objects[0]` and `objects[1]` with exact counts; repeated names refetch, so no result or token cache exists; frozen input names and the provider's object are unmutated.

Documentation matches the implementation. Note 52 is implemented at this HTTP boundary, and note 34 correctly stays open for tenant OAuth selection, encrypted credential persistence and rotation, restricted-user FLS proof, definition loading and the refusing publisher.

Note 59, informational: the per-request timer is unref'd, so in a process where nothing else keeps the event loop alive and the fetch registers no handle, the timer cannot fire and the call never settles. This matches the ODK adapter's deliberate pattern and is unreachable with the real `fetch` or inside the running service, but it matters for a future short-lived CLI publish command.

Two observations that are not defects: the declared `content-length` is parsed leniently, so values like `1e3` are accepted while the streamed counter remains authoritative; and nothing enforces the ADR's rule against reusing an adapter across attempts, though a reused adapter simply finds its deadline expired and fails closed.

## 2026-09-23: ODK Collect runtime evidence; notes 36 and 37 tested, finding 60 on empty instanceID

Bill approved and prepared an Android emulator, so the Collect half of notes 36 and 37 finally has real runtime evidence. Environment: BlueStacks 5 instance `Pie64`, Android 9, ODK Collect v2026.3.4 installed by Bill, driven over the ADB that BlueStacks ships. No package was installed by the verifier and nothing was fetched from the network. Forms were compiled with the merged compiler at main `d9fce7e` and pushed into the local demo project. This is emulator evidence, not a physical-handset result; both questions concern JavaRosa evaluation rather than device hardware.

Note 36 is answered for ODK Collect: nested relative `jr:count` evaluates per repeat instance. The compiler emits `jr:count="/data/house_count"` for the root repeat and `jr:count="../person_count"` for the nested one. With two houses whose own counts were 1 and 3, Collect produced exactly one person row in house 1 and three in house 2, and the finalized submission contained one `people` element under the first house and three under the second. A second run with counts 2 and 1 produced two and one. The relative path is therefore evaluated against the repeat instance, not one level too high, so the feared off-by-one-level reading does not occur in Collect. The Enketo half of note 36 remains untested by the verifier, because the browser tooling is still uninstalled, so it rests on the builder's evidence alone.

Note 37 is confirmed for ODK Collect, and the divergence between engines is real. Three rows were answered, the count was then reduced from 3 to 2, and Collect continued to show `Rows > 3` holding `ROW-3`. The finalized submission contained `<row_count>2</row_count>` with three `rows` elements, including `ROW-3`. The submitted repeat count therefore does not equal the answered count, exactly as the note predicted, while the builder's Enketo run removed the trailing answered row. A publication or ingestion rule must be decided before any C10.2 or C10.3 claim, and the compiler warning must say the behaviour is client-specific.

Finding 60, high severity for the product, in merged compiler output rather than any open pull request: a finalized submission can carry an empty `instanceID` when the form has nested counted repeats and a nested count is changed during entry. The compiler emits the submission metadata as `<orx:meta><orx:instanceID/></orx:meta>` with the bind `/data/orx:meta/orx:instanceID` and `calculate="once(concat('uuid:', uuid()))"`. Observed in Collect v2026.3.4:

- The note 36 form finalized twice with `<orx:instanceID />` empty. In both runs a nested count was changed during entry.
- The single-repeat note 37 form populated `instanceID` normally, including when its count was reduced, so one repeat is not enough to trigger it.
- Four minimal forms with no repeats, covering the prefixed metadata, unprefixed metadata, `jr:preload="uid"` and a visible calculate, all populated `instanceID` normally, so neither the prefix nor `once(concat('uuid:', uuid()))` is broken on its own.
- The decisive comparison used two forms identical except for the metadata namespace, each run through the same sequence with a nested count changed from 1 to 3. With `orx:meta/orx:instanceID` the submission had an empty `instanceID` while an unprefixed root calculate in the same submission held its uuid. With `meta/instanceID` the submission carried `uuid:8d396042-3245-4d35-8acf-46e5a6a940d9`.

The observable rule is that changing a nested repeat count clears the value of the namespace-prefixed metadata node, while an unprefixed root calculate in the same form keeps its value. Consequence: submissions for any form using nested counted repeats can reach a server with no submission identity, which breaks OpenRosa deduplication and edit semantics and would make ingestion unreliable. Bounded correction: emit the metadata block unprefixed as `<meta><instanceID/></meta>` with the bind `/data/meta/instanceID`, which is what XLSForm and pyxform produce, and add a compiler test asserting the unprefixed nodeset. Verified fix behaviour is recorded above. A runtime regression test should drive a nested counted repeat whose count changes and assert a non-empty `instanceID`.

Notes ledger: note 36 is answered for Collect and still open for Enketo; note 37 is confirmed on the Collect side and stays open until a publication or ingestion rule is decided and tested in both engines; finding 60 is new and open. Notes 34, 39 remain open, note 52 is implemented, note 57 stays with Bill, and notes 47, 49, 50, 53, 55, 56, 58 and 59 remain informational.

## 2026-09-23: note 57 closed; the ODK Validate pin matches ODK's published release

With Bill's approval, the verifier made one read-only query to the public GitHub release metadata for `getodk/validate` at tag `v1.20.0`. Nothing was downloaded and no repository outside Kusanya was modified.

The release publishes exactly one asset, `ODK-Validate-v1.20.0.jar`, released 8 January 2026, size 5,885,216 bytes, with digest `sha256:92756ea4aed195355a07e5572f025f0921a31282387a870ae63e1f5cdf37e0c3`.

Three independent values now agree: the digest ODK publishes, the constant `odkValidate120Sha256` pinned in the merged `service/src/publication/odk-validate.ts`, and the SHA-256 of the JAR on the build machine, which is also 5,885,216 bytes. The concern behind note 57 was that the file and the pin shared one origin, the builder's download, so a wrong or tampered file would have produced a self-consistent but meaningless match. That is now excluded by an independent publisher-side value.

Note 57 is closed. The ADR 0024 gate therefore runs the genuine reviewed ODK Validate 1.20.0, and any future change of pinned version must repeat this check against the publisher's digest.

It was the only test without `System.runAs`, so it ran as the deploying administrator, who holds no field-level security on the optional definition fields because a metadata deploy grants none. Assigning `Kusanya_Integration` to that administrator made the class pass 4 of 4 with no code change, which established the cause. The hosted log showed only `Verification failed; unrecognized exception details withheld`, because the pinned coverage checker throws a plain `Error`, which the harness does not classify and therefore suppresses, recording `failed-tests`. Reproducing the harness's own sequence locally, `sf project deploy start --source-dir force-app --wait 5` followed by `sf apex run test --test-level RunLocalTests --code-coverage --result-format json --wait 5` and then the pinned `apex-coverage.mjs`, showed the checker rejecting the report because `result.tests.length` was 84 while `summary.testsRan` was 85. This unit introduced the repository's first `@TestSetup`, and the platform counts that setup method in the summary but omits it from the detailed rows. Removing the annotation, calling the fixture builder first in each test and isolating the setup-object DML inside `System.runAs(new User(Id = UserInfo.getUserId()))` produced 84 rows against 84 and an accepted report at 701 of 715 executable lines, 98.04 percent. The corrective head was confirmed to be that same variant line for line, differing only by an explicit `private` modifier that Apex applies by default.

The verifier's earlier clearance had missed this because the Apex suite was run without the harness's flags and its coverage checker was never applied. The procedure is now to reproduce the harness sequence and run the pinned checker before clearing any Salesforce unit.

## 2026-09-24: PR #44 atomic publication lifecycle (ADR 0027); findings 65 to 68 closed, PASS at `5f424c9`

The unit began at head `0979c8a`, one commit on accepted main `a0980bd`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, with the declared tree `702b301`, and reached `5f424c9` after one corrective commit whose tree was `64030eb`. Thirty-seven files changed in total. No dependency, lockfile or workflow change at either head; `.github` was untouched by the whole pull request, the two changed scripts were the root tripwires `form-model.test.mjs` and `publication-lifecycle.test.mjs`, all other harness and policy scripts stayed byte-identical to main, and the pin appeared twice throughout. Local checks reproduced at both heads: root 257 of 257, service 216 of 216, integration 1 passed and 1 skipped, lint, typecheck, format, scaffold, both production audits at zero and `git diff --check`. The corrective delta was nine Apex class and test files and nothing else.

Source review of `PublicationLifecycle`, `PublishedDefinitionGuard` and the eight rewired triggers produced no findings. `authorizeVersions` is private, so the trigger authorization map cannot be primed from outside the class, and both maps clear in a `finally` that runs before the outer handler. The retry path requires the digest, both file identifiers, the warning text, equal compile and publish timestamps, a single Published version that is the target, a matching Form status and pointer, exactly the two expected links, and `Published_By__c` equal to the running user, so a retry is same-principal by construction and performs no DML. The commit locks the Form `FOR UPDATE` first and then every version of that Form in one statement, which is the property that serialises two concurrent publications of the same Form. `PublishedDefinitionGuard` is `without sharing` by design, performs no DML and only adds errors. The permission change adds field permissions for the new `Publication_Digest__c` to the three sets with no line removed anywhere.

The lock order is verified by construction rather than by a race. Two genuinely concurrent transactions cannot be executed inside an Apex test, so the serialisation property rests on reading the statement order together with the single-transaction consequences that were observed.

Four findings were then raised in the verifier's own scratch org, none of which is visible from reading the source, and all four were reproduced with a proven correction before any hosted run was cleared.

Finding 65, high: the unit did not deploy. `PublicationLifecycleTest` bound an inline set constructor inside a `COUNT()` query, as `WHERE LinkedEntityId IN :new Set<Id>{ first.Id, second.Id }`, and the deploy failed with an implementation restriction requiring a filter by a single identifier. Four anonymous Apex variants isolated the cause precisely: `COUNT()` with `= :variable` compiles, `COUNT()` with `IN :setVariable` compiles, selecting rows with an inline constructor compiles, and only `COUNT()` with an inline constructor fails. The production class issues the same inline construction at line 91 and is unaffected because that query selects rows. Since a failed metadata deploy rolls back entirely, the hosted job would have recorded `failed-infrastructure` with no indication of the cause. Binding the set to a local variable first made the deploy succeed at 146 components with zero errors.

Finding 66, high: with the deploy fixed, every call to `commitPublication` refused. The clone taken for the update carried `Form__c`, a master-detail field, and `Version_Key__c`, a formula field, neither of which is updateable for any profile, and `Database.update` with `AccessLevel.USER_MODE` enforces field updateability and rejects the whole record. `getDmlFieldNames` named both fields against `CANNOT_INSERT_UPDATE_ACTIVATE_ENTITY`. The same clone updates without complaint in system mode, which is why the defect survived design review. It was confirmed not to be a permission problem: the Integration principal held access, create and update on all eight publication fields plus `Status__c`, `Change_Log__c` and `Version_Number__c`, and no permission set change could help because those two fields are not updateable for anybody. The proven correction keeps the full clone as the authorization expectation and sends a separate sparse record carrying only writable fields.

Finding 67, high: with the first two corrected, the commit still refused, now with `Publication transition was altered.` swallowed by the inner catch. `committedAt` was `System.now()`, which carries milliseconds, and Salesforce truncates a Datetime field to whole seconds during the save pipeline before the before-update trigger runs. The trigger record therefore held the truncated value while the authorized copy held the original, `sameVersion` returned false and the transaction rolled back. Dumping both records at the comparison point showed every field matching except `Compiled_At__c` and `Published_At__c`, which differed only by 183 milliseconds. Because `System.now()` almost never lands on a whole second, this failed in practice always, and it was neither intermittent nor environment specific. Truncating the stamp to whole seconds corrected it, after which all five lifecycle tests passed.

Finding 68, medium: `PublishedDefinitionGuard` adds SOQL to the before triggers, which invalidated seventeen fixed query assertions across seven existing test classes. Fourteen tests failed and three further assertions were masked behind an earlier failure in the same method. The guard itself was not at fault: every one of its queries is a bulk query over the incoming set with none inside a per-record loop, so the added cost is a constant per trigger invocation rather than per record, and only the assertions needed to move. Each measured delta was supplied to the builder as a file, line and increment table so that all seventeen could be corrected in one pass.

Behaviour was verified in the verifier's own orgs with probe classes and one forced-failure validation rule deployed only there and never committed. Every hostile input refused with the static string alone and left no links behind, covering null and wrong-type version identifiers, a non-hexadecimal digest, identical XForm and XLSForm identifiers, a cross-type document identifier, a document created by another user, and wrong file extensions. A first publication succeeded, an exact retry returned `alreadyCommitted` with zero DML, and retries with a changed digest, swapped files or altered warning text were refused. A replacement publication superseded the first, the Form pointer advanced, exactly one version remained Published, and republishing a superseded version was refused. With the validation rule forcing the final Form update to fail after the links and version updates had already happened, the savepoint left zero orphan links, the version back to Draft with null XForm, null digest and null published timestamp, and the Form back to Draft with a null pointer. The immutability matrix blocked direct lifecycle edits, direct digest edits, direct Form pointer edits, inserting a version already Published, inserting a version carrying publication metadata, deleting a published version, deleting a non-draft Form, editing or deleting published questions, mappings, field mappings and skip rules, editing a shared choice list, inserting a choice into a frozen list, and inserting a question into a published version, while draft edits and draft deletes still succeeded and a mixed batch let the draft record through and failed the frozen one.

All three probes were re-run against the builder's corrected implementation rather than the verifier's patch, and returned results identical to it. One further check was added because a sparse update is where an audit trail can quietly vanish: after a replacement publication the superseded version still held its digest, both file pointers, its publisher, its published timestamp, its compile warnings, its change log and both content document links, the Form kept its name, and both timestamps on both publications carried zero milliseconds and were equal to each other.

## 2026-09-24: Enketo tooling authorized; finding 70 raised as issue #46 and closed by PR #47 (ADR 0028); note 36 answered, PASS at `015fd6b`

Bill authorized the isolated Enketo browser tooling, which had been parked since ADR 0015. The first run of it found a high severity defect in code that had already merged, and the correction closed it.

### The tooling, and how it was kept isolated

A portable Node 22.23.2 was taken from the official archive and verified against the published SHA-256 `1177b413…99f97`, with no change to the global PATH or the service toolchain. Dependencies were installed only under `scripts/runtime`, with `npm ci --ignore-scripts` against the committed lockfile. The lockfile was reviewed before installing: version 3, 134 packages, every entry carrying an integrity hash including the Leaflet.draw archive pinned to an exact upstream commit, and zero entries without one. 109 packages installed. `node_modules/` and `work/` are ignored, so the working tree stayed clean. The two high advisories recorded in the licence inventory were accepted on the documented basis: the probe blocks page requests, starts no server API, sees only synthetic fixtures and touches no org or credential. Nothing was installed in `service/` and no browser binary was downloaded.

### Finding 70, high, raised against merged main rather than any open unit

Every form the compiler produced failed to initialize in Enketo. `render.ts` emitted the instance root as `<data xmlns="">`, which placed the whole instance in the null namespace. Transformer 4.2.0 therefore did not recognize the conventional `<meta><instanceID/></meta>` block, injected its own `<xf:meta><xf:instanceID/></xf:meta>`, and never declared the `xf` prefix, leaving a model that is not well-formed XML. Enketo Core refused it with `Error trying to parse XML model. Invalid XML`. Both tracked fixtures failed identically, and the committed probe's own assertion failed at line 109, which is exactly what finding 61 had predicted would happen the first time the corrected bytes were actually run.

The cause was isolated with four browser variants through one Chromium build rather than inferred. As shipped, it failed. The pre-finding-60 prefixed `orx:meta` shape initialized, but that is the shape that finalized with an empty identifier on a real device. Removing only ` xmlns=""` initialized cleanly, with no injection and the existing unprefixed metadata retained. The second shipped fixture failed exactly like the first. So the null-namespace reset was the cause, the metadata qualification was not, and the apparent conflict between the two clients did not exist. ADR 0015 had implied it did.

Before recommending anything, the candidate was checked against the other engine and then the device. It passed the tracked JavaRosa 6.0.0 probe, including identifier stability across reload on both fixtures with both negative controls firing, and the shipped bytes were confirmed to pass JavaRosa and fail Enketo, so the comparison had a baseline. It was then run on ODK Collect v2026.3.4 on the BlueStacks `Pie64` emulator: Collect parsed and listed the form, the nested count was changed during entry from 0 to 2 in the first family and 0 to 3 in the second, which is the finding 60 trigger, the repeats expanded correctly, and the finalized submission carried `uuid:75ee177e-b91d-4f2b-a1fc-889bd19a1f47`. One shape therefore satisfied all three surfaces.

The finding was recorded as issue [#46](https://github.com/kusanya-io/kusanya/issues/46), with the device evidence added to it, so that the correction had a durable reference rather than a relayed message.

### The correction, PR #47 under ADR 0028

One commit `015fd6b` on accepted main `9b44ad3`, tree `1b66634`, ten files. The source change is a single line removing only the namespace reset. Nothing else under `service/src` changed, so the generated byte delta can only be that removal. No dependency, lockfile, workflow, harness, policy script, Salesforce metadata, Enketo deployment or device configuration change, `.github` untouched, and the pin present twice. The one script touched, `check-enketo.mjs`, cannot reach either pipeline: the protected job runs only the pinned trusted scripts, and `ci.yml` does not reference the runtime probes at all.

Local checks reproduced at the exact head: root 257 of 257, service 216 of 216, integration 1 passed and 1 skipped, lint, typecheck, format, scaffold, `git diff --check` and both production audits at zero.

The four reviewed compiler hashes were recomputed from source rather than compared as text, and all four matched, each rendering `<data id="synthetic_form" version="1">` with no reset, unprefixed metadata and no `orx` metadata. Both runtime fixture hashes matched. Decisively, the unit's `nested.xml` is byte-identical, by SHA-256, to the exact file already run on the Collect device, so the stated condition for skipping a further device run was met and none was performed.

Engine evidence was re-run at this head rather than accepted. The Enketo probe exited zero, with both fixtures initializing, independent nested counts moving from `[0,0]` to `[2,3]`, eleven answers and the identifier surviving reload, and the count decrease removing one answered row; its report records the negative controls as `wrong-count-context` and `null-instance-namespace`, zero escaped requests, no Collect UI claim and no acceptance claim, and its manifest carries exactly the two declared hashes. The JavaRosa probe exited zero including its guarded-UUID negative. ODK Validate 1.20.0, with the jar's SHA-256 verified before use, accepted the four compiler fixtures and both runtime fixtures and refused both built-in negative controls.

Two observations were recorded without being findings. The Enketo probe swaps its negative control rather than adding one, but the removed bare-UUID control still exists unchanged in the JVM runner and fired during verification, so the coverage moved between engines rather than disappearing. Separately, ODK Validate accepts the null-namespace variant and reports the form valid, so it cannot detect finding 70; this is why the null-namespace control belongs in the browser probe, and it means the only guard running automatically in CI is the renderer assertion, since CI never runs the optional probes. That assertion is present and correct.

The documentation was checked for overclaiming. ADR 0028 states the cause accurately, attributes the device evidence without overstating it, makes no C10, deployed-Enketo, publish-ready or Phase 1 claim, and gates the finding on exact-head verification. The ADR 0015 correction does not repeat finding 61: it deletes no record, it replaces a wrong interpretation with the measured one and adds the namespace rule alongside the metadata rule, stating explicitly that the two are complementary rather than competing.

A hosted Salesforce run was required despite this unit changing no Salesforce path, because the documentation exemption excludes `docs/decisions/` and the unit also changes source. That is correct policy behaviour. No verifier scratch org was created, since there is no Salesforce path to exercise, and the expectation was recorded in advance that the Apex figures should reproduce those verified for PR #44.

## 2026-09-24: PR #49 submitted repeat cardinality (ADR 0029); finding 71 raised and closed, note 37 closed at the policy boundary, PASS at `afbd7cb`

The unit began at head `9fa9076`, one commit on accepted main `b9f2f38`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, and reached `afbd7cb` after one corrective commit. Sixteen files changed. No dependency, lockfile, workflow, harness, policy script, Salesforce metadata, Enketo tooling or device path was touched at either head, and the pin stayed present twice. Local checks reproduced at both heads: root 257 of 257, service 226 and then 227 of 227, integration 1 passed and 1 skipped, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold, the interchange checker, the XLSX checker with the fixture digest still `e8aa7ba5…0931`, `git diff --check`, and both production audits at zero.

The decision itself is sound and is what note 37 had been waiting for. ADR 0029 makes the submitted count authoritative for every `from_answer` repeat, per enclosing repeat context, retains the first N instances in document order, excludes the surplus from normalized Answers while reporting it structurally, keeps the raw submission as the audit and reprocessing source, and fails closed on anything ambiguous. It pins the rule into the publication package so a later ingestion adapter cannot silently substitute its own default.

Finding 71, high, raised at `9fa9076` and closed at `afbd7cb`: the normalizer refused essentially every real submission for a whole class of ordinary forms. `normalize` recursed into unknown nodes passing the child's definition as the parent, which is `undefined` for an unknown node and is the same value that means root scope. Inside any unknown group subtree it therefore re-evaluated the root-level repeat limits, found none of those instances there, and raised `SUBMISSION_REPEAT_DEFICIT`. The reachability is what made this severe rather than theoretical: every real ODK submission carries `<meta><instanceID/></meta>`, which is an unknown group here because metadata is not a question, and ADR 0028 had just made that block canonical. A count question followed by a top-level repeat is an ordinary form design. So for any such form no real Collect or Enketo payload could be normalized, the failure was a total refusal rather than data loss, and the diagnostic pointed at the metadata node rather than the cause.

The matrix was established by probe rather than by reading. With a root-level `from_answer` repeat and a submitted count of one: no unknown group succeeded; adding the canonical metadata group refused; an unknown group nested inside a retained repeat row refused; an unknown empty group refused; an unknown leaf succeeded, because leaves return before the limit logic; and a submitted count of zero succeeded, because the deficit comparison cannot fire at zero.

No shipped fixture could have caught it. `nestedCountRuntimeForm`'s root repeat is open and `sectionCountRuntimeForm`'s root repeat is fixed, so the limits loop inside an unknown group met a non-`from_answer` repeat and returned harmlessly. The unknown-node tests covered only an unknown leaf being preserved, while the two unknown-group cases in the file were negative controls for scope movement and for the depth bound. ADR 0029's claim to cover "unknown nodes" was therefore true only for leaves.

The correction is the minimal one, and it reuses a pattern already present in the same file: the sibling `index` pass had long carried a `knownPlacement` flag, and `normalize` simply lacked its equivalent. The corrective head adds a `knownScope` flag distinct from the parent value, clones unknown subtrees verbatim without consulting the repeat map, and passes `definition !== undefined` at the recursion site. The corrective delta was exactly three files: the normalizer, its test and the ADR.

Re-verification reproduced the complete matrix at `afbd7cb`. Every previously failing case now succeeds with the unknown node preserved in place, including canonical metadata, an unknown group two levels deep inside a retained repeat row, an unknown empty group, an unknown leaf and a submitted count of zero. Every refusal that should remain does remain: a known question below an unknown wrapper, below nested unknown wrappers, and the count-source question itself hidden below a wrapper, which was not on the builder's list and was added by the verifier. Surplus, deficit and malformed counts behave identically with metadata present, and three unknown groups interleaved around the count source and the repeat rows produce exactly one exclusion entry, so no duplicate evaluation arises. The new regression test uses a genuine root-level `from_answer` repeat with canonical metadata, an unknown group inside the retained row and an empty unknown group, and asserts the normalized tree deep-equals the input; it fails on the previous head, so the defect cannot return silently.

Everything else in the unit held under adversarial probing at both heads and needed no change. Exact, zero, surplus and deficit rows behave as specified, document-order retention is exact, trimming is independent per enclosing instance, nested dynamic repeats trim per outer instance, root-scoped count sources resolve correctly from inside repeated parents, and fixed and open repeats are untouched. Excluded outer branches are genuinely not evaluated: a surplus outer instance carrying an invalid nested count and one carrying a nested deficit both pass, while the same deficit inside a retained instance refuses. Exclusion reports are in canonical definition order and stable per parent context. Count validation refused all fourteen malformed forms tried, including empty, whitespace on either side, signed, zero-padded, decimal, exponent, hexadecimal, a word and an Arabic-Indic digit; `repeatMax` is enforced and the documented one-thousand fallback is exact, accepting 1,000 and refusing 1,001; and a missing, duplicated or wrongly shaped count source each refuse.

The strict decoder is the strongest part of the unit. Proxies are refused at the root, as a child and as the children array, including a prototype-lying proxy. Accessor properties are refused and never invoked, which was confirmed with instrumented getters on both `name` and `value`. Symbol keys, extra keys, both `value` and `children` together, and neither, are refused. Sparse arrays, arrays carrying extra or symbol properties, array subclasses, null-prototype arrays and arrays with accessor indices are all refused, while null-prototype plain records are accepted as documented and class instances are not. Over-long names, colons, leading digits and empty names are refused, as are a control character and a lone surrogate in a value. Depth, node count, per-value and aggregate text bounds and cyclic trees are all enforced. Every diagnostic carries only a code and a structural index path, disclosing no submitted name or value.

The publication package advances to schema version 2 and adds `submissionPolicy.dynamicRepeatCardinality` set to `submitted-count-v1`. The marker sits inside the canonical bytes and participates in the digest: changing its value and removing the field both change the SHA-256 over the canonical JSON. The constant is imported from the normalizer rather than duplicated, so the two cannot drift. Existing authoring, XForm, XLSX and target-schema digests remain valid. ADR 0023 records correctly that no version-one production package requires migration.

A hosted Salesforce run was required despite this unit changing no Salesforce path, because the documentation exemption excludes `docs/decisions/` and the unit also changes source. No verifier scratch org was created, and the Apex figures were predicted in advance to reproduce those already verified for PR #44.

Hosted run 36056128373 attempt 1 at `afbd7cb`: marker `{"schemaVersion":1,"role":"ci","runId":"36056128373-1","headSha":"afbd7cb22cc296e5931754447bae7629239c929c","startedAt":"2026-09-24T21:00:21.892Z","outcome":"passed","retryable":false}`, with `89 passed; 1094/1119 executable lines (97.77%)`, identical to the PR #44 and PR #47 results as predicted. The only repository commit in the log is the reviewed head. Cleanup deleted one owned org, tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__36056128373-1__afbd7cb22cc2__2cdc5df93b06`, which the Dev Hub shows as Deleted with `ErrorCode` null and a matching `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 21:01:40 UTC, with zero active orgs. All 938 log lines were clean of credential material, with no scratch username or org identifier printed. All five required checks passed on the unchanged head.

The superseded run 36052471718 at head `9fa9076` again sat in `waiting` and held the single concurrency group, leaving its successor in `pending` until it was cancelled. This is the third unit in which that has happened, after PR #42 and PR #47, and it has cost a manual cancellation each time. The workflow question remains open with the builder.

`PASS: PR #49 merged as 9bb1afc`, with squash tree `bb09a92` identical to the verified head.

Ledger: finding 71 is closed and guarded by a regression test. **Note 37 is closed**, at the policy and pure-normalization boundary only. The client difference it recorded remains real, because Collect can still retain and submit trailing answered rows after a count reduction while Enketo removes them; what closes is the open question of what Kusanya does about it, which is now a reviewed, content-addressed rule rather than an undecided one. No C10.2, C10.3 or executable-ingestion claim follows: this unit parses no XML, stores no submission, creates no Answer, executes no mapping transform, writes no target record, acknowledges nothing and enforces no retention, and those acceptance tests stay unclaimed until later units enforce the package-pinned rule end to end. Notes 34, 39 and 69 remain open, notes 47, 49, 50, 53, 55, 56, 58, 59 and 64 remain informational, and notes 25, 27 to 31 and 35 carry forward.

## 2026-09-25: PR #51 safe cancellation of superseded Salesforce waits (ADR 0030); 0 findings, PASS at `fcac883`

One commit `fcac883` on accepted main `e7861a1`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, tree `a4ee16d`, seven files. This unit adds a privileged workflow, so it was reviewed to a different standard from the product units: the question was not only whether the policy is correct but whether untrusted code can ever reach the write authority the workflow holds.

The problem it solves is recorded three times in this log. The credentialed Apex job uses one repository-wide concurrency group with `cancel-in-progress: false`, which protects an active scratch lifecycle but also lets an older run that is merely waiting for environment approval hold the group, leaving a replacement head pending. That cost a manual cancellation on PRs #42, #47 and #49.

### The trust boundary

`pull_request_target` combined with `actions: write` is the shape that hands write authority to pull-request code when it is done wrong, so each failure mode was checked individually rather than inferred from the design description.

The trigger is `pull_request_target` limited to `branches: [main]` and `types: [synchronize]`, and the job additionally requires the head repository to equal the repository, which excludes forks, and the base to equal the default branch. The checkout takes `ref: github.event.pull_request.base.sha`, the trusted base commit, with `persist-credentials: false` and `sparse-checkout: scripts`. There is no dependency installation step, and the script imports only `node:child_process` and `node:url`, so no `package.json` script and no third-party package can execute. The workflow file itself is loaded from the default branch, so a pull request cannot alter what runs. Global permissions are empty and the single job carries only `actions: write`, `contents: read` and `pull-requests: read`; `actions: write` is the narrowest scope GitHub offers for cancelling a run. There are zero `secrets.` references and zero `environment:` keys, so no Salesforce credential or environment is reachable.

Script injection was ruled out by enumeration rather than by reading. The workflow contains exactly six expressions, all in `env:` or `with:` positions, and its single `run:` step is the literal `node scripts/cancel-superseded-salesforce.mjs` with no interpolation at all. The script uses `execFileSync` with an argument vector rather than a shell, and every value that reaches an API path is validated first: the repository against an owner/name pattern, the pull-request number as a positive safe integer, and every SHA against a forty-character lowercase hexadecimal pattern.

### The safety claim, checked against live data

The design rests on one claim: a protected job that is merely waiting for environment approval reports zero steps and no runner, which is positive evidence that authentication and scratch allocation never began. That was verified against the real waiting run on this very pull request, read without approving it. Run 36122059795 reported status `waiting` with a null conclusion, `total_count` 2 matching an array of 2, the policy job completed with ten steps and an assigned runner, and the protected job `Scratch org tests and 85 percent coverage` with status `waiting`, null conclusion, zero steps, null runner id and null runner name. Exactly one job carries the protected name, so the uniqueness check holds, and the script accepts either zero or null for the runner id and either empty string or null for the name, which is what live GitHub returns. A job with zero steps has not run even its first checkout, so no org can exist.

### Adversarial probing of the policy

The cancellation function is exported, so it was driven directly with injected GitHub responses across approximately fifty hostile scenarios. The cancellation adapter was invoked exactly once, in the single legitimate case, and never in any other.

It refused without mutation for the current head and same-head retries; a different branch; a foreign repository in either the run repository or the head repository; two superseded candidates; in-progress, completed, queued and `workflow_dispatch` runs; a different workflow name or path; a non-integer run identifier; an attempt above the bound; a malformed head SHA; a protected job with any step, with a runner identifier, with a runner name, already in progress, belonging to another run, or with an attempt mismatch; two jobs sharing the protected name; no protected job at all; a job total that disagrees with its array; an empty, foreign, closed, non-default or duplicated commit-to-pull-request association; a live pull request that is closed, has moved head, has a changed base or has a foreign head repository; total drift between pages; duplicate identifiers across pages; a total above the bound; a short page before the total was reached; and a runs payload that is null, a string, an array or missing its runs field. It also refused when state changed between the two proofs: the single-run reread showing in progress or completed, returning a different identifier, or reporting the current head; the jobs reread gaining a step; the pull-request head moving; and the association breaking.

Every refusal message is a fixed sentence, and the success output is one of two fixed sentences, so no response body, name, branch or other attacker-controlled value appears in any diagnostic.

The verifier's first probe harness contained two bugs of its own: a response-sequencing helper that mistook the association array for a list of per-call responses, and a nullish-coalescing default that swallowed a deliberate null payload. Three scenarios therefore appeared to be defects until the harness was corrected and rerun, after which all three failed closed. The results above are from the corrected harness, and the flaw was in the probe rather than in the reviewed code.

### The residual race is described accurately

GitHub's run-cancellation endpoint accepts no conditional or version argument, so a window remains between the final proof and the request. ADR 0030 states exactly that, says the repeated reads only minimise the interval, keeps the standing operator rule never to approve a superseded head as part of the control, and explicitly declines to present the guarantee as atomic. That is accurate.

### Everything else required

`salesforce-verify.yml` and `ci.yml` are byte-identical to main, the harness pin appears twice, and `cancel-in-progress: false` is unchanged on the repository-wide group. No existing harness or policy script was modified; the two new scripts are additions. No dependency, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, Enketo tooling, device path or product-runtime file is touched. The new workflow deliberately has no concurrency key, which is correct, because sharing the Salesforce group would deadlock it behind the very run it exists to cancel.

The shipped test matrix covers no candidate, one valid candidate, multiple candidates, the current head, dispatches, active and completed runs, foreign pull requests and repositories, malformed data, changed state during the final proof, and the privileged workflow's own shape. That last test runs in ordinary CI, so a future pull request cannot silently repoint the checkout at the head or widen the permission set without failing a required check. ADR 0030, the ADR 0006 amendment, `docs/ci.md` and the roadmap all match the implementation and state the deployment limitation plainly.

Local checks reproduced at the exact head: root 266 of 266, service 227 of 227, integration 1 passed and 1 skipped, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold, `git diff --check`, and both production audits at zero. `actionlint` is not installed in the verifier environment, so the workflow YAML was independently parsed with Prettier, which establishes syntactic validity but not Actions schema validity.

### The deployment limitation, confirmed and then discharged in part

Because `pull_request_target` workflows load from the default branch, the new workflow could not run from the pull request that introduces it. That was confirmed rather than assumed: only CI and Salesforce verification ran at the reviewed head, and the repository's registered workflow list contained only `ci.yml` and `salesforce-verify.yml`, so the new file was not registered and could not have executed. After the merge the workflow appears in that list as active, which means GitHub has now parsed it successfully and the schema question is settled. Its behaviour remains unproven until a real firing.

Hosted run 36122059795 attempt 1 at `fcac883`: marker `{"schemaVersion":1,"role":"ci","runId":"36122059795-1","headSha":"fcac883646805131ac29a0be07aa2d6b3787ef3c","startedAt":"2026-09-25T10:20:59.060Z","outcome":"passed","retryable":false}`, with `89 passed; 1094/1119 executable lines (97.77%)`, identical to the previous three units as predicted. Cleanup deleted one owned org, tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__36122059795-1__fcac88364680__0e522d6fdff4`, which the Dev Hub shows as Deleted with a null error code and a matching `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 10:22:20 UTC, with zero active orgs. All 938 log lines were clean of credential material. All five required checks passed on the unchanged head.

`PASS: PR #51 merged as 4b07e46`, with squash tree `a4ee16d` identical to the verified head.

Ledger: no findings. Note 72 is new and informational: the residual cancellation race has one concrete consequence worth recording, in that a cancellation landing in the same instant as an approval of a superseded head could start the Apex job, create an org and then interrupt it before cleanup, so if a cancellation and an approval ever coincide the Dev Hub should be checked for an org tagged with that run identifier before assuming nothing was created. Note 73 is new and informational: the workflow-shape tripwire is looser than it reads, which was confirmed by mutating the file, because an extra permission appended after the last listed one still satisfies the permissions pattern and an added `environment:` key is not among the forbidden patterns; the workflow as shipped is correct and tightening is cheap. A first live firing is still owed and will be verified as part of whichever unit triggers it, checking that the cancellation run appears and succeeds, that it cancelled the older SHA rather than the current head, and that the replacement run leaves the pending state without manual intervention. Notes 34, 39 and 69 remain open, notes 47, 49, 50, 53, 55, 56, 58, 59 and 64 remain informational, and notes 25, 27 to 31 and 35 carry forward. No C10 acceptance test is claimed.

## 2026-09-28: PR #53 immutable publication artifacts (ADR 0031); finding 74 and note 73 closed, note 75 raised, PASS at `35aacc8`

The unit began at head `31b4594`, one commit on accepted main `03f5790`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, and reached `35aacc8` after two corrective commits. Twenty-five files changed at the first head. No dependency, lockfile, workflow, harness or policy-script change at any head; the only scripts touched were the root tripwires `form-model.test.mjs` and `publication-lifecycle.test.mjs`, joined later by `cancel-superseded-salesforce.test.mjs`, and the harness pin appeared twice throughout. No endpoint, OAuth selection, credential persistence, uploader, delivery route, CLI, target write or ingestion implementation appears anywhere in the unit.

The decision itself is sound and is what note 69 had been waiting for on the drift side. `commitPublication` now takes exact `ContentVersion` identifiers rather than document identifiers, validates on a first commit that both versions are visible under user mode, belong to the publishing principal, are latest, carry the exact `xml` and `xlsx` extensions and belong to distinct documents, then stores the derived `ContentDocument` identifiers in `XForm__c` and `XLSForm__c` for Salesforce Files linkage while pinning the exact immutable version identifiers in the new `XForm_Version__c` and `XLSForm_Version__c`. Both identities participate in trigger authorization, publication-metadata immutability and exact retry matching. The `IsLatest` requirement is correctly gated on the first commit only, through `requireLatest` being `target.Status__c == 'Draft'`, which is precisely what allows a later exact retry to succeed after the pinned versions have ceased to be latest.

Finding 74, high, raised at `31b4594` and closed at `bc4142a`: the unit did not deploy. `PublicationLifecycleTest` attempted `Database.delete(new ContentVersion(...))`, and Salesforce forbids delete DML on `ContentVersion` at compile time, so the entire metadata deploy rolled back. The hosted job would have recorded `failed-infrastructure` with no explanation. The blocker was isolated precisely: the package deployed 151 of 152 components with exactly that one error, and the identical package with only that class excluded deployed 151 of 151 with zero errors, after which the remaining suite ran 84 of 84 passing. The assertion could not be repaired in place, because Apex cannot delete a `ContentVersion` from any context, so the verifier deliberately did not edit the shipped test and reported the platform behaviour instead, leaving the choice of remedy to the builder.

That platform behaviour was measured rather than assumed. Deleting each pinned record by identifier through the REST API, as an administrator holding the Integration permission set, produced `insufficient access rights on object id` for the `ContentVersion`, which is the platform refusing, and `Published artifacts are immutable.` for both the `ContentDocument` and the `ContentDocumentLink`, which is the unit's own guard refusing. A second observation followed: the `PublicationContentVersionRetention` trigger could not be made to fire by any available route. Direct deletion is refused before any trigger runs, and it does not fire on cascade either, which was established by deleting a `ContentDocument` and observing exactly one SOQL query, the document guard's single query, so the version guard was never invoked for the cascaded versions. On that evidence the trigger was unreachable and `protectVersions` was dead code.

The builder took the deliberate option of removing the unreachable defence rather than keeping it. The corrective commit deleted the invalid assertion, the `ContentVersion` trigger and its metadata, and `protectVersions`, retained both reachable guards unchanged, and corrected the guard header, ADR 0027, ADR 0031, the data model, the roadmap and the Salesforce README so that direct version-deletion refusal is attributed to Salesforce rather than to an Apex trigger. ADR 0031 now requires a separate platform API probe for that refusal, on the stated ground that Apex does not permit the delete DML to compile, which is the correct form of evidence for a claim Apex cannot make. Re-verification repeated the API probe with no `ContentVersion` trigger present in the org at all and obtained identical results, which established that the removal deleted dead code rather than a defence.

Behaviour was verified in the verifier's own scratch orgs with probe classes deployed only there and never committed. After a first commit the document and version pins were both stored correctly. Adding a newer `ContentVersion` to the pinned document left the pin unchanged, left the document identifier unchanged, marked the pinned version as no longer latest, and left its original bytes readable while the new latest version held different bytes, which is exactly the post-commit drift that note 69 described. An exact retry using the original pinned identifiers, after those versions had ceased to be latest, returned `alreadyCommitted` with a DML delta of zero, while a retry offering the superseding version identifier was refused, so both identities genuinely participate in retry matching. A first commit using a non-latest version was refused, as were two versions of the same document. The guards refused deletion of the pinned document and of both required links, allowed an unpinned document to delete normally, and in a mixed bulk delete produced a refusal for the pinned row alongside success for the unpinned row. The guard's cost is constant rather than per record: deleting one document and deleting fifty each consumed exactly one query and one DML statement. A replacement publication left the first version Superseded while retaining its version pin, document pin and compile warning, gave the second its own pins, advanced the Form, left exactly one Published version and two links on each publication, and still refused deletion of the superseded publication's artifacts, so the audit trail survives supersession. Every refusal carried only the static `Published artifacts are immutable.` with no record or file identity, from both Apex and the REST API. Permission changes were additive only, with Admin and Integration gaining editable access to both new fields as the user-mode lifecycle update requires, and Supervisor gaining readable with editable false.

Note 73, informational and raised against PR #51, was closed in this unit by the third head. It had recorded that the supersede workflow's shape tripwire was looser than it read, and both gaps had been confirmed at the time by mutating the file. The fix captures the whole indented permissions block and compares it for exact equality rather than matching a prefix, adds `environment:` to the forbidden patterns with the multiline flag, and requires exactly one `run:` step. Closure was proved by the same method that found the gap: in a throwaway worktree at the reviewed head, each of the three mutations was applied to the real workflow and the real shipped test file was executed, and each mutation produced one failing test where an extra permission and an added environment key had previously passed unnoticed. The unmutated file passes nine of nine, and the worktree was restored and re-verified clean.

The first hosted attempt failed and the failure was environmental rather than a property of the source. Run 36146874920 at `bc4142a` recorded `failed-tests` with `retryable` false and the single diagnostic `Verification failed; unrecognized exception details withheld.`, with no `Apex for` line, so it died during the test phase before the coverage checker reported. The ownership tag printed at 14:44:54 and the failure at 14:50:41, so the deploy and test together consumed five minutes and forty-seven seconds against a `--wait 5` ceiling on each command. The head was then proved good in a second fresh org using the harness's own flags and its own five-minute wait: the deploy took 51.4 seconds and the test 46.4 seconds, reporting ninety passing tests with ninety detail rows, and the pinned checker accepted the report. Nothing was stranded: the harness deleted its own org, and every scratch org created that day was confirmed Deleted with a null error code, with zero active.

Note 75, informational and against the pinned harness rather than this unit: a transient timeout or network failure during the Apex test phase is recorded as a permanent test failure whose cause is withheld. In `verify-salesforce.mjs` the catch block tests the `testing` branch before the `SalesforceCommandError` branch, and `testing` is set true immediately before the test command, so every test-phase error becomes `failed-tests` with `retryable` left false and the command-error branch is unreachable. Independently, `transientStages` contains only `quota-read`, `scratch-read`, `scratch-create` and `source-deploy`, so a timeout at the `apex-tests` stage would be non-retryable in any case, and `apex-run-budget.mjs` lists `failed-tests` among the outcomes that deny retries. The consequence is that an environmental failure permanently denies the affected head, which is what obliged a fresh commit here. A reviewed harness change could classify the command error before the short-circuit, consider adding the test stage to the transient set for timeout and network codes only, and surface the coverage checker's own message, which contains no provider data. Because the harness is pinned, that belongs in its own unit with its own decision record, and note 64 has now contributed to three hosted failures.

The fresh head `35aacc8` changed exactly one file, the supersede tripwire. The claim that the rest of the source was unchanged was checked by tree hash rather than accepted: the `salesforce`, `service` and `.github` trees were byte-identical to `bc4142a`, so every conclusion about ADR 0031 carried forward on identity. The harness sequence was nonetheless reproduced at the new head in a third independent org, giving a 25.0 second deploy of 151 components with zero errors, a 54.2 second test run reporting ninety passing tests with ninety detail rows and 45,796 milliseconds of Salesforce execution, and the pinned checker reporting 1153 of 1180 lines at 97.71 percent. Three orgs in succession produced the identical result with the suite taking between 45 and 51 seconds, which reinforces the environmental reading of the earlier five-minute test phase.

Both production branches of the ADR 0030 cancellation workflow were observed during this unit. On the first corrective push, run 36146871539 cancelled the superseded waiting run at the older SHA while leaving the current head's run untouched, and the replacement run left the pending state without manual intervention for the first time after three consecutive manual cancellations. On the second corrective push, run 36167104373 exercised the opposite branch: because the previous run had already concluded `failure` rather than waiting, there was no candidate and the output was `No superseded, zero-step waiting Salesforce run exists.` Both runs checked out the trusted base commit rather than either pull-request head, both emitted only their fixed sentence, and the cancelling run disclosed no run identifier or response body. The cancelled run's protected job showed zero steps and no runner, no scratch org existed for it in the Dev Hub, and its gate job concluded failure, confirming that cancellation consumes nothing and cannot make a stale head's gate pass.

Hosted run 36167106990 attempt 1 at `35aacc8`: marker `{"schemaVersion":1,"role":"ci","runId":"36167106990-1","headSha":"35aacc8bbaeee284e7a335b07825ecb32c81d17b","startedAt":"2026-09-28T09:27:48.195Z","outcome":"passed","retryable":false}`, with `90 passed; 1153/1180 executable lines (97.71%)`, matching the verifier's three independent reproductions exactly. Cleanup deleted one owned org, tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__36167106990-1__35aacc8bbaee__942ca8e753b1`, which the Dev Hub confirms as Deleted with a null error code, with zero active orgs. All 938 log lines were clean of credential material, with no scratch username or org identifier printed. All six required and reported checks passed on the unchanged head.

## 2026-09-28: PR #55 strict publication commit API (ADR 0032); 0 findings, PASS at `f4bdd11`

One commit `f4bdd11` on accepted main `1f0afc1`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, added the narrow Salesforce commit edge deliberately deferred by ADRs 0027 and 0031. Thirteen files changed. No dependency, lockfile, workflow, harness, policy-script, Enketo, Collect or target-ingestion file changed, and the harness pin remained `1d0edc1` twice. Local evidence at the exact head was root 271 of 271, service 227 of 227, integration 1 passed and 1 skipped, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold, both fixture checkers, both production audits at zero and `git diff --check`. The XLSX fixture digest remained `e8aa7ba59d675ce3dbad3aaa7c96a9c696ada1c92dcadb1157ed2339ae030931`.

`PublicationCommitResource` exposes one `POST /services/apexrest/v1/publications/commit` operation and delegates once to the previously reviewed user-mode lifecycle. It requires class access and the new `Publish_Kusanya` custom permission, both granted only by `Kusanya_Integration`, checks the permission before reading the request body, bounds the body at 200,000 bytes and strictly streams an exact schema-versioned envelope. Success and refusal responses are deterministic, static and non-cacheable. The resource accepts no tenant, credential or publishing principal, performs no upload, download or outbound request and never reads `VersionData`. Source and adversarial review found zero findings. Hosted execution added three passing tests, taking the suite from 90 to 93 and proving the first commit, exact zero-DML retry, malformed and bounded input refusals and denial of an ungranted principal.

Note 76 is informational: platform authorization and verb routing precede the resource. A principal without Apex class access receives Salesforce's platform-level 403 before the custom-permission response can be universal, and a non-POST request receives the platform's 405 before the resource's own method branch, making that branch unreachable through the live route. This does not weaken the intended boundary, but ADR 0032's documented response envelope is not universal across platform-level refusals.

It produced no ownership tag, no scratch org and no verification marker, and exposed no auth URL or credential material. The pinned policy correctly represented the skipped verification as retryable infrastructure, leaving one same-head attempt for the UTC day. Bill authorized the CI credential to be reissued; the secret value never appeared in a command, output or transcript, and the source head remained unchanged.

Run 36429845164 attempt 2 succeeded at the exact head. The budget recheck printed `infrastructure-retry` twice, the only repository commit in 938 log lines was the reviewed head, and the trusted harness was checked out at `1d0edc1`. The single marker identifies `36429845164-2`, the full head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`. The pinned checker reported `93 passed; 1280/1317 executable lines (97.19%)`, with 93 detailed rows, and stale-success rejection passed.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__36429845164-2__f4bdd11e9c5f__3b641c023ba1`. The Dev Hub independently showed its ScratchOrgInfo row created at 18:24:53 UTC, Deleted at 18:26:11 with `ErrorCode` null, a matching `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 18:26:18 and zero active orgs for the attempt. Fallback cleanup correctly skipped after the harness cleanup, and logout succeeded. The log contained no scratch username, org identifier, auth URL or token and had 21 masked redactions.
