## 2026-09-30: PR #57 CI authentication isolation (ADR 0033); note 77 closed except shared capacity, note 80 raised, PASS at `b2b849f`

Three commits `0075c71`, `c0b28aa` and `b2b849f` on accepted main `c5c4ed4`, all authored and committed by Bill Owiti, answered note 77 by separating hosted CI's Salesforce identity from the verifier's. Four files changed, 204 insertions and 9 deletions, every path under `docs/`: the new ADR 0033, `docs/ci.md`, `docs/operations/salesforce-org-registry.md` and `docs/roadmap.md`. The unit is documentation only, so its substance is the operational state it describes, and that state was checked against the live org rather than read from the claims. `.github`, `scripts`, `salesforce` and `service` were byte-identical to the base at every reviewed head, the harness pin appeared twice in `salesforce-verify.yml`, and no dependency, lockfile, workflow, policy-script or Salesforce metadata file changed. Local checks reproduced at the exact head at root 271 of 271, with Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check`.

The two principals are genuinely separate. The verifier is `005bm00000X3cnJAAR` on System Administrator and CI is `005bm00000YXZrRAAX` on Minimum Access - Salesforce, distinct user identifiers in the same Dev Hub `00Dbm00000yeiz3EAA`. The permission set `Kusanya_CI_Dev_Hub` was retrieved whole rather than sampled: `ScratchOrgInfo` read, create, edit and delete; `ActiveScratchOrg` read, edit and delete with create false; no View All or Modify All on either object; no class accesses, field permissions, tab settings, record types, custom permissions or application visibilities; and no permission set group membership. A live confirmation made that real rather than declared, because querying as the CI principal returned only records it owns while the verifier saw all 52. **The permission set contents were applied to the org by the verifier at Bill's instruction, so the verifier's later attestation of those contents is operator-applied and not fully independent.** Everything else in this entry is arm's length.

The first reviewed head `c0b28aa` was permanently denied, and the reason was the inverse of what the brief asked. The review had confirmed the permission set matched what ADR 0033 declared, which it did, but nobody had proof the declared scope was sufficient. Run 36708122785 authenticated the new CI credential cleanly and then failed at `quota-read` with `CLI_FAILED`, which is correctly non-retryable, so `decideInfrastructureRetry` denied that head for good. Reproducing it directly showed the Limits REST resource failing for the CI principal with `limits resource is not enabled` while succeeding for the verifier. Two discoveries followed that are worth recording for any future permission work here. The required permission is `ViewSetup`, and Salesforce refuses `ViewSetup` without `ViewRoles`, which surfaced only as `FIELD_INTEGRITY_EXCEPTION: Permission ViewSetup depends on permission(s): ViewRoles` on a direct field update. Before that, a metadata deploy adding `ViewSetup` reported success with one of one components and zero errors and yet left `PermissionsViewSetup` false, so the metadata API silently dropped it. With both enabled, the whole Dev Hub path was then proven end to end as the CI principal ahead of spending another head: quota read, scratch org creation, `CreatedById` equal to the CI user, an `ActiveScratchOrg` query and delete in the same shape the harness uses, and a `ScratchOrgInfo` reaching Deleted with a null error code. Record visibility did not widen: both added permissions are read-only setup permissions, and the CI principal still saw only its own record.

Run 36769924388 attempt 1 at the corrected head failed after 1.3 seconds at Dev Hub authentication with the static class `RefreshTokenAuthError/other`, before the harness started. It created no ownership tag, no scratch org and no marker, exposed no credential material, and left zero active orgs. The pinned collector represented the completed and skipped verification step as `failed-infrastructure` with `retryable: true`, leaving one same-head attempt for the UTC day. **The credential was deliberately not reissued, and that decision is the interesting part of this unit.** The harness diagnostic's `other` category excludes `invalid_grant`, expired tokens, DNS, timeout and connection errors, so the error was by construction not a rejected token. Three org queries agreed: the CI `OauthToken` record was still present with `UseCount` 3 and `LastUsedDate` 19:44:13, there was no `LoginHistory` row at the failure time at all, and a verifier `OauthToken` carried `UseCount` 58 against nine consecutive successful hosted runs on 21 September, every one ending in the same `sf org logout` step. Stored auth URLs are therefore not single-use in this org, reuse does not invalidate them, the logout step does not revoke them server-side, and the verifier's own operator probe had registered no refresh grant. A second point removed most of the risk from spending the remaining attempt: `usedToday` counts only attempts whose start date equals the current UTC day, so a retryable head becomes eligible again at the next midnight UTC regardless.

Run 36769924388 attempt 2 succeeded at the unchanged head with the secret never reissued, which settled the diagnosis. The same `OauthToken` record moved from `UseCount` 3 to 4 with `LastUsedDate` 20:37:01, so the identical refresh token that had failed at 20:14 worked at 20:37 with nothing changed in between. That token has now served four successful authentications. It also casts the PR #55 precedent in a new light: attempt 1 of run 36429845164 failed with the same static class on 28 September and the credential was reissued, and this unit's evidence suggests that reissue was never necessary either.

The hosted evidence at attempt 2 is complete. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted`, sparse to `scripts`, with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed`, sparse to `salesforce`, as data only, both confirmed by the log's own resolution lines. Every forty-character hex string in the log was enumerated so nothing could hide behind a count, and all five are explained: the reviewed head 44 times, the pin 6 times, the `actions/checkout` and `actions/setup-node` pins, and `abac9266`, which is the Azure hosted compute agent's image build commit and not a repository reference. The Salesforce CLI was pinned to `@salesforce/cli@2.135.7`. Identity isolation was proven by a negative, because across the whole run window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX` at 20:37:01 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, while the verifier user did not authenticate at all. The single marker identifies `36769924388-2`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`. The pinned checker reported `93 passed; 1280/1317 executable lines (97.19%)`, and stale-success rejection passed on the unchanged head after testing. Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__36769924388-2__b2b849f7ceb8__72be997dcb14`, whose `ScratchOrgInfo` the Dev Hub independently showed created at 20:37:07 with `CreatedById` equal to the dedicated CI user, Deleted with a null error code, a matching `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 20:38:23 by that same user, and zero `ActiveScratchOrg` and zero active `ScratchOrgInfo` records org-wide. Fallback cleanup correctly skipped, because its condition requires a failed or cancelled verification, and logout succeeded. The log contained no auth URL, token, scratch username or org identifier, with 10 masked redactions. All required checks passed.

Two figures were corroborated rather than accepted. The reported test count was checked independently at the reviewed head, where 25 Apex classes, 12 of them test classes, carry 105 `@IsTest` or `testMethod` annotations, which after subtracting the 12 class-level annotations leaves exactly 93. The Apex job completed in 2 minutes 1 second, which looked far too fast for a scratch org creation, deploy and full test run, so the harness step was compared against prior successful runs at 83, 75 and 77 seconds against 79 here before the duration was accepted as normal. The comparison is recorded because the figure invites that suspicion.

One disclosure keeps the org count unambiguous. A second `ScratchOrgInfo` exists for the CI user from that evening, created at 19:53:29 and named `Kusanya disposable verification`. It is the verifier's own operator probe from the permission testing described above, it carries no harness tag so the harness correctly never adopted or counted it, and it too reached Deleted with a null error code. The run created exactly one org.

`PASS: PR #57 merged as 25bc9e4`, with squash tree `d4b32a1` identical to the verified head and all four trust-boundary trees unchanged. Post-merge main CI run 37669476734 passed at that commit, so nothing was added at merge time.

Ledger: zero findings. **Note 77 closes on three of its four axes.** Identity is closed, because the only authentication in the run window was the dedicated CI user. The refresh-token axis is closed, because the protected secret carries the CI principal's token, that token was the one used, and the verifier's authorizations were untouched. Principal-ownership coupling is closed, because `ScratchOrgInfo.CreatedById` is the dedicated CI user. **The shared-capacity portion remains open by design**, since hosted CI and the verifier still draw on one Dev Hub allocation of three active and six daily scratch orgs; identity isolation granted no separate budget, ADR 0033 states this itself, and this PASS must not be read as resolving it. Note 78 is informational and was re-confirmed: three unused Salesforce CLI approvals remain live for the verifier user, one from 10 September with use count 58 and two from 28 September, one of which has never been used, so only one approval is in use and revoking the others would reduce standing credential surface for a System Administrator with no loss of function. Note 79 is informational and is deliberately not upgraded despite this unit's failed attempt: the CI principal's refresh token exists both in the protected secret and in the operator's private CLI store, which ADR 0033 explicitly permits, and the evidence showed the duplication did not cause the failure. Note 80 is new and informational: the authentication diagnostic recognises `dns`, `timeout`, `connection` and `token-rejected` and collapses everything else into `other` with the raw CLI output withheld, so a transient token-endpoint failure cost a protected run twice and the log could not say it was transient; withholding raw output is correct and must stay, but bounded classifications for `inactive_user`, `inactive_org`, `invalid_client_id`, `unsupported_grant_type` and the HTTP status class of the token response would close the gap without disclosing credential material, and this belongs to a harness unit rather than a documentation one. The verifier has also adopted an interim operating rule that the CI authorization is not exercised from a workstation, and that operator testing of CI-equivalent permissions uses a separate throwaway authorization of the same user. One post-merge observation is recorded as not a defect: the Dev Hub shows 3 October audit activity from `Automated Process`, including a permission set group recalculation and platform feature activations, none of it a Kusanya change and none of it able to reach the CI user, which holds exactly two permission set assignments, its profile's implicit set and `Kusanya_CI_Dev_Hub`, with no permission set group on either, and whose scope was re-checked as unchanged. Notes 34, 39 and 69 remain open, note 75 remains informational, and ADR 0033's own deferral of the JWT bearer flow with a dedicated certificate-based connected app stands as the preferable long-term approach requiring its own reviewed harness unit. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-07: PR #59 strict definition snapshot API (ADR 0034); 0 findings, notes 81 and 82 raised, PASS at `1e9f45d`

One commit `1e9f45d` on accepted main `e480bec`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, exposed the existing ADR 0026 definition reader through a single bounded integration-only Apex REST operation at `/services/apexrest/v1/publications/definition`. Ten files changed, 719 insertions and 2 deletions, matching the declaration. Main remained `e480bec` throughout, so the head stayed zero commits behind.

`.github` and `service` were byte-identical to main and the harness pin appeared twice in `salesforce-verify.yml`. `scripts/` did differ, which the tree comparison alone would make look alarming, so it was enumerated: both changed files are tests, the new `definition-snapshot-api.test.mjs` and a two-line edit to `form-model.test.mjs`. All six harness and policy scripts were checked individually and every one was byte-identical, namely `verify-salesforce.mjs`, `apex-coverage.mjs`, `apex-pr-policy.mjs`, `apex-run-budget.mjs`, `scratch-lifecycle.mjs` and `cancel-superseded-salesforce.mjs`. The `form-model.test.mjs` edit is also the right shape, remaining a `deepEqual` on the exact class-access list now reading `['DefinitionSnapshotResource', 'PublicationCommitResource']` rather than loosening to a containment check. No dependency or lockfile changed, so the two moderate `fast-uri` and `fastify` advisories are pre-existing. Local evidence at the exact head reproduced at root 275 of 275, with Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` clean.

Authorization precedes request-body access, and this was proven live rather than inferred from statement order. Acting as an Admin holding `Kusanya_Admin` but not the `Publish_Kusanya` custom permission, a garbage body sent with a `text/plain` content type returned only `{"schemaVersion":1,"error":"DEFINITION_SNAPSHOT_FORBIDDEN"}`. Neither the malformed body nor the wrong media type ever surfaced, which is only possible if the permission gate runs first. At the metadata level `Kusanya_Integration` is the only permission set carrying any `classAccesses` or any `customPermissions`, and `Kusanya_Admin` and `Kusanya_Supervisor` carry neither; Supervisor and ordinary-user denial rests on the shipped Apex tests, while the Admin denial was established live. One incidental confirmation of least privilege is worth recording: the verifier's System Administrator user could not even compile `Form__c.Folder__c` in anonymous Apex until `Kusanya_Admin` was assigned, because the org describe exposed only two accessible custom fields on `Form__c`. Field-level security here is carried entirely by the permission sets and nothing by the profile, and Apex tests are unaffected only because they run in system mode.

The decoder was driven over the live HTTP route as an Integration principal and failed closed on every adversarial case: duplicate `formVersionId`, missing `schemaVersion`, missing `formVersionId`, an unknown extra field, `schemaVersion` as a string, `schemaVersion` of 2, `schemaVersion` of 1.0, a nested object in place of the identifier, a null identifier, a trailing object after the envelope, an array at the root, truncated JSON, malformed identifier text, a wrong-object `Form__c` identifier, a wrong-object `Question__c` identifier, and a well-formed but absent identifier. The request bound is exact at the documented value, with a body of exactly 2,048 bytes returning 200 and exactly 2,049 bytes returning `413 DEFINITION_SNAPSHOT_TOO_LARGE`. A 15-character identifier is accepted, which is correct because the pattern deliberately permits the 15 or 18 character form. Wrong-object identifiers are handled better than first assumed: rather than reaching a SOQL bind and producing a generic failure, `DefinitionSnapshotReader.readVersion` checks `getSObjectType()` against `Form_Version__c` up front and raises its own refusal, so they return the 400 refusal. Every refusal across twenty live requests was the two-field static envelope, with no supplied value, record identity, provider message or exception text. The request schema contains exactly two fields and offers no tenant, user, owner or credential to supply, so the authenticated principal is structurally the read principal. The resource holds exactly one `DefinitionSnapshotReader.readVersion` call site and contains no DML and no SOQL of its own.

Two separate HTTP transactions two seconds apart returned byte-identical 936-byte bodies. The success body was scanned with a combined 15-and-18-character identifier pattern and for every known record identifier in both forms, and nothing was present; the output carries portable keys only, such as `form_7315da92946203511997572b424813c5` and `mapping_1`, with questions identified by name. A remark on the shipped test rather than the code: its identifier-absence assertion uses `String.valueOf(question.Id)`, which yields the 18-character form, so a hypothetical 15-character leak would pass it. No leak exists, established independently by the pattern scan, so this is only a note that the assertion is narrower than it reads.

The response cap is where a defect was expected and none was found, and the expectation was disproved rather than reported. Because the cap is applied after `JSON.serialize` and `Blob.valueOf`, the transaction holds the whole body twice before the bound is evaluated, and a heap `LimitException` cannot be caught by `catch (Exception)`; the hypothesis was therefore that the 5,000,000-byte cap was effectively dead code and that real amplification would escape as a raw platform error. Definitions were built carrying eight long-text fields of 32,768 characters per question, all well inside the reader's 500-question bound, and the size walked upward. Responses of 1,313,720 bytes at five questions, 2,626,892 at ten, 3,152,164 at twelve, 3,940,072 at fifteen and 4,990,616 bytes at nineteen questions all returned 200, that last figure sitting 9,384 bytes below the cap. Twenty questions crossed the cap and returned the designed static 500. The cap is therefore reachable at its documented bound and its refusal is non-disclosing. The amplification concern survives only as a thin-headroom observation: at the boundary the transaction holds roughly five megabytes as a String and five megabytes again as a Blob and still completes, so the design depends on the Apex heap allowance comfortably exceeding twice the cap, which held on API 64.0 in a fresh scratch org. Raising the cap, or any future reduction in the synchronous heap allowance, is what would turn a clean refusal into an uncatchable governor failure.

Media-type handling behaved exactly as written, with `application/json` and `application/json; charset=UTF-8` returning 200 while `text/plain` and `application/json; charset=ISO-8859-1` returned `415 DEFINITION_SNAPSHOT_MEDIA_TYPE`. The missing-header path remains verified by code reading and the shipped test rather than live, because the CLI used for probing supplies a default content type and the header could not be omitted through it.

No `@TestSetup` appears anywhere in the Apex source, preserving the hosted checker's requirement that detail rows equal `testsRan`, and the suite adds exactly four methods. In an independent fresh verifier scratch org the deploy completed at 156 of 156 components with zero errors, and the full local test level returned outcome Passed with 97 tests ran, 97 passing, zero failing, zero skipped and 97 detail rows, the expected increase from 93. The pinned `apex-coverage.mjs` at `1d0edc1`, byte-identical to this head's copy, reported `Apex: 97 passed; 1383/1428 lines covered (96.85%)`.

Before approval the protected run was inspected and found waiting at attempt 1 on the exact reviewed head, with its Apex job carrying zero steps so that nothing had executed and no credential had been used, its policy job passed, and public CI run 37671609952 green at the same head. Zero scratch orgs were active beforehand.

Hosted run 37671610049 attempt 1 then passed at the exact head, and its figures were identical to the independent reproduction, which is the strongest single result in this unit: two unrelated orgs and two separate deploys produced `97 passed; 1383/1428 executable lines (96.85%)`, an arithmetic 96.8487%. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only, both confirmed by the log's own resolution lines. Every forty-character hex string in the 526-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the reviewed head 44 times, the pin 6 times, the `actions/checkout` and `actions/setup-node` pins, and `c3d12f33`, which is the Azure runner image provisioner commit rather than a repository reference. The Salesforce CLI was pinned to `@salesforce/cli@2.135.7`. Identity isolation was again proven by a negative, because across the entire Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX` at 19:32:23 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, while the verifier user did not authenticate at all. ## 2026-10-09: PR #61 Salesforce artifact attestation (ADR 0035); 0 findings, notes 83 and 84 raised, PASS at `2215778`

One commit `2215778` on accepted main `dd69170`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, added a bounded service-side transport that uploads the XForm and XLSForm as two `ContentVersion` records in one atomic request and returns version identifiers only after reading both exact versions back and comparing them byte for byte. Five files changed, 870 insertions and zero deletions: ADR 0035, an ADR 0031 amendment, the roadmap, a 528-line transport and its 254-line test. Main remained `dd69170` throughout, so the head stayed zero commits behind.

`.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were byte-identical to main, so no workflow, harness, pin, policy-script or Salesforce metadata changed, and the pin appeared twice in `salesforce-verify.yml`. No dependency or lockfile changed, so the two moderate `fast-uri` and `fastify` advisories are pre-existing. Local evidence at the exact head reproduced at root 275 of 275 and service 233 of 233, with service lint, service typecheck, Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` all clean.

The unit was verified by deploying to an independent fresh scratch org and driving the real transport against live Salesforce Files as a least-privilege non-administrator Integration principal, then running 45 adversarial cases against the compiled module. Live success took exactly four requests, all pinned to API 64.0: one `POST /services/data/v64.0/composite/sobjects` carrying `allOrNone: true` with exactly two records, one metadata read of both versions, and two `VersionData` downloads. Every request carried `cache: no-store`, `credentials: omit`, `redirect: manual` and an `Authorization: Bearer` header, and `getAccessToken` was observed to be called four times for four requests, which proves the token is genuinely fresh per request rather than acquired once and reused. Origin validation requires `https:`, an exact origin match with no path, credentials or port, and a `.salesforce.com` suffix. Titles and paths derived only from the package digest, matching `kusanya-<64 hex>-(xform|xlsform)` and `<64 hex>.(xml|xlsx)` when checked against the stored records.

Strict refusal was proven by counting token acquisitions as well as requests. Twenty-nine hostile shapes were refused with `requestCount` zero, zero `getAccessToken` calls and zero network calls: proxy, accessor-backed property, null-prototype object, class instance, array, extra key, missing key and null identities; malformed package JSON, `schemaVersion` 3, wrong kind, wrong audience, an unknown top-level key, package digest mismatch, xform hash mismatch, xlsform hash mismatch, noncanonical base64 padding and base64 containing whitespace; and an `http:` origin, a non-Salesforce origin, origins carrying credentials, a path or a port, a malformed origin string, an overall timeout below the per-request timeout, both timeouts above their bounds, and an extra configuration key. One nuance is worth recording so a future reader does not repeat a pointless probe: an exotic object cannot be smuggled inside the package, because `packageJson` is a string and `JSON.stringify` flattens any proxy or accessor before the transport ever sees it, so the `record` guard matters for the identity and configuration arguments, which is exactly where it is applied.

Bounds and deadlines hold. The shared overall deadline covers token acquisition, which is the part most easily missed: a four-hundred-millisecond token callback against a sixty-millisecond overall budget refused in sixty-two milliseconds with `requestCount` zero, and a slow fetch under the same budget refused in sixty-two milliseconds after one request, with nothing hanging in either case. JSON provider responses are held to HTTP 200, `application/json`, fatal UTF-8 decoding, strict expected shapes and 64 KiB as both a declared `content-length` and a streamed length; a 201 status, a `text/plain` body, an invalid UTF-8 body, a declared length of 65,537 and a 70,000-byte streamed body were each refused separately. Binary reads are capped at the corresponding local artifact length, and a ten-thousand-byte response against a thirty-eight-byte artifact was refused. Success additionally requires two distinct `ContentDocumentId` values, each row's `Id` equal to the uploaded version at the same index, `IsLatest` true, the `xml` and `xlsx` extensions in that order, and byte equality; the equality test checks length before `timingSafeEqual` and short-circuits, so `timingSafeEqual` is never reached with unequal lengths, which would otherwise throw. No version identifier is returned before both downloads pass.

Forced-mismatch cleanup was proven live by wrapping fetch to flip one byte of each `VersionData` response. The result was `PUBLICATION_ARTIFACT_MISMATCH` after five requests, being upload, metadata, one download and two deletions, and both documents were removed. A stub harness confirmed that the two deleted identifiers are the two newly created documents, and that a deletion not returning 204 produces the distinct `PUBLICATION_ARTIFACT_CLEANUP` code. Failure results expose only `ok`, `diagnostic` and `requestCount`, with the diagnostic carrying only `code` and `location`; no token, origin, provider message, digest, title or record identity appears. Both objects are frozen and the caller's identity object was byte-identical after the call.

ADR 0035 records the uncertain-outcome limitation honestly and calls cleanup best-effort rather than guaranteed. One phrase would read more precisely: the window is wider than an upload whose response is lost, because a fully decoded upload followed by any failure of the metadata readback also leaves files behind, since cleanup needs `ContentDocumentId` values that only that second call supplies. The ADR's wording, that a response may not be decoded far enough to recover identities, does cover this when "identities" is read as document identities, which the preceding sentence supports.

The Files permission question was settled by measurement rather than inference. A non-administrator user was created on a `Standard Platform User` profile holding only `Kusanya_Integration`, and the whole operation ran as that principal: two distinct owned documents, the `xml` and `xlsx` extensions, `IsLatest` true, byte-identical downloads and deletion after the forced mismatch. `Kusanya_Integration` grants no `ContentVersion` or `ContentDocument` object permissions and none are required, because the capability comes from the license and profile baseline, which is why this unit correctly changes no Salesforce metadata. One constraint on the probe is recorded: the scratch org's `Salesforce` licenses were exhausted at two of two, so the probe user took a `Salesforce Platform` license, and the observed Files behaviour is therefore proven for a platform-licensed least-privilege user rather than for every license type a future publisher might use.

Hosted run 37919064509 attempt 1 then passed at the exact head with no retry consumed. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only. Every forty-character hex string in the 526-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the reviewed head 44 times, the pin 6 times, the `actions/checkout` and `actions/setup-node` pins, and `c3d12f33`, the Azure runner image provisioner commit, which is not a repository reference. The stale-success rejection re-read the live head after testing and passed.

The Apex figures served as a useful negative control. The harness reported `97 passed; 1383/1428 executable lines (96.85%)`, identical to PR #59's hosted figures. That is the correct result rather than a coincidence, because this unit adds no Apex and changes no Salesforce metadata, so unchanged numbers are positive evidence that the `salesforce` tree really is untouched and corroborate the byte-identical tree comparison.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__37919064509-1__22157785825d__b7bd1a235257`, whose `ScratchOrgInfo` the Dev Hub independently showed created at 11:57:58 with `CreatedById` equal to the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null `ErrorCode`, a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 11:59:13 by that same user, and zero `ActiveScratchOrg` and zero active `ScratchOrgInfo` records org-wide. The 11:55 to 12:05 audit window held that one entry and nothing else, so no permission, user or configuration change rode along with the run. Fallback cleanup correctly skipped because its condition requires a failed or cancelled verification, and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the dedicated CI user at 11:57:52 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, while the verifier user did not authenticate at all. The log contained no auth URL, refresh token, client secret, access token, private key, bearer string, scratch username or org identifier, with ten masked redactions, and the verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

The verifier's own probe org is recorded separately and doubles as evidence of principal separation: created at 10:47:59, named `Kusanya disposable verification`, with `CreatedById` `005bm00000X3cnJAAR`, the verifier user, against `005bm00000YXZrRAAX` for the run's org, and also Deleted with a null error code. One daily scratch slot was consumed by that reproduction.

This unit also produced the first production exercise of the ADR 0030 supersede workflow's association proof against an orphaned candidate. An obsolete zero-step run at the previous head `b572fa88` held the Salesforce concurrency group while the fresh run waited. Supersede run 37919060106 refused with `Candidate pull-request association is incomplete` and cancelled nothing, because `GET /commits/b572fa88.../pulls` returns an empty array once a force-push orphans the commit, and the script stops inside `readPullAssociation` before reaching the cancellation adapter. The obsolete run's Apex job was confirmed `waiting` with zero steps and no runner identity, so nothing had authenticated and no org existed, and the operator was advised to cancel only that run, which was then cancelled while the fresh run and the public CI run were left untouched.

Three disclosures concern the verifier's own work. A first service-suite run reported 227 of 233 tests because the working tree was still on `main` rather than the reviewed head; at the head it is 233 of 233. Two of forty sweep rows returned unexpected codes and both were harness faults rather than defects: a proxy nested inside the package is meaningless as a probe, and an oversized-binary case returned the cleanup code only because the stub answered deletions with 200 instead of 204, which with a correct 204 returns the readback code and deletes both documents. Most importantly, a first cleanup check counted `ContentDocument` as the administrator and saw zero both before and after, which would have reported deletion on evidence that was simply blind, because an administrator cannot see another user's private files; the check was re-run as the owning principal, where exactly the successful case's two documents remained and the forced-mismatch documents were absent. The conclusion held but the first method did not.

`PASS: PR #61 merged as 9d3451e`, with squash tree `f08aebc` identical to the verified head and all top-level trees unchanged. Post-merge main CI run 37932010044 passed at that commit. One non-required check, `Cancel superseded zero-step run`, is red on that head and left the pull request `UNSTABLE` rather than `CLEAN`; every required context was green, which was confirmed programmatically rather than by eye.

Ledger: zero findings. Note 83 is new and informational, for publisher composition: the attested files are owned by and private to the uploading principal, since querying as the scratch org administrator returned no `ContentDocument` and no `ContentVersion` rows while the owner saw both, and the only `ContentDocumentLink` rows point at the owner user itself with share type `I`, so the files are linked to no business record; the composed publisher must therefore run the ADR 0032 commit and any later read of these versions as the same principal, or establish sharing or links first, or it will store version identifiers that other principals cannot resolve, and ADR 0035 does not mention this. Note 84 is new and informational, against ADR 0030 rather than this unit: the supersede automation cannot fire when the superseded head was force-pushed away, because an orphaned commit has no pull-request association, and that is the commonest way a run becomes superseded, so an operator will usually have to cancel by hand; the refusal is safe and the association proof should not be loosened, because it is the control that makes a `pull_request_target` workflow with `actions: write` trustworthy, but the coverage claim deserves tempering, and a candidate could instead be attributed by matching the waiting run's own `head_branch` and workflow identity against the live pull request without trusting the orphaned commit. **Note 69's remaining initial-byte obligation is answered at the durable-storage boundary** on live evidence, because the bytes read back from Salesforce were byte-for-byte equal to the canonical artifacts; it is recorded as answered at that boundary rather than closed outright, because nothing yet compels a publisher to call this operation immediately before ADR 0032 commit, and that compulsion belongs to note 34, as ADR 0035 states itself. Note 34 remains open for tenant connection, restricted-user target-object FLS and publisher and CLI composition. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81 and 82 remain informational. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-09: PR #63 attempt-scoped Salesforce publisher (ADR 0036); finding 1 raised and closed, note 85 raised, PASS at `6897d3f`

Three commits on accepted main `e22258f`, all authored and committed by Bill Owiti: `dd7c0ae` composed the publisher, `f4eabe0` added the bounded same-attempt commit retry, and `6897d3f` corrected ADR 0036 after this review raised finding 1. The unit composes the previously reviewed boundaries into one attempt-scoped operation: the strict ADR 0034 definition request, fresh API 64.0 Describe calls as the same integration principal, ADR 0023 package creation with bounded ODK validation, ADR 0035 artifact upload with exact-byte readback, and the ADR 0032 immutable commit using only the attested version identifiers. Four files changed against main, 1,190 insertions and zero deletions at the source-reviewed head, with the correction adding 39 insertions and 18 deletions across the ADR and the roadmap. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were byte-identical to main throughout, the harness pin appeared twice, and no dependency or lockfile changed. Local evidence at the exact head reproduced at root 275 of 275 and service 245 of 245, with service lint, service typecheck, Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` all clean.

Verification ran the composed operation against live Salesforce in an independent fresh scratch org, as a non-administrator Integration principal created on a `Standard Platform User` profile holding only `Kusanya_Integration` and a verifier-only probe permission set granting Account access. The org's `Salesforce` licences were exhausted at two of two, so the probe principal took a `Salesforce Platform` licence; the behaviour observed is therefore proven for a platform-licensed least-privilege user rather than for every licence type. The ODK validator used the genuine pinned artifact, fetched by the repository's own documented procedure in `docs/compiler.md`, whose SHA-256 `92756ea4aed195355a07e5572f025f0921a31282387a870ae63e1f5cdf37e0c3` matches the `odkValidate120Sha256` constant exactly.

**Finding 1, raised against `f4eabe0` and closed by `6897d3f`.** ADR 0036's verification section required proving, against a cross-owner definition, that a permitted mapping publishes successfully. It does not and cannot with the shipped permission set. With a definition owned by the administrator, the definition read, Describe, artifact upload and byte readback all succeeded and the commit then returned `400 PUBLICATION_COMMIT_REFUSED` twice. The cause was read from the org's own debug log rather than inferred: `validateRequest` and `validateArtifacts` both pass, and the refusal is a `System.DmlException: Update failed ... INSUFFICIENT_ACCESS_OR_READONLY` on the final `Form_Version__c` update inside `PublicationLifecycle.commitPublication`. `Form__c` carries `sharingModel: Private`, `Form_Version__c` is `ControlledByParent` through a non-reparentable master-detail, and `Kusanya_Integration` grants `viewAllRecords: true` with `modifyAllRecords: false`, so the principal can read any definition but write only those shared to it. The control experiment settled it: the identical operation against a definition owned by the Integration principal returned `ok: true` in exactly seven requests, in the same org, as the same principal, with the same mapping and the same code, differing only in record ownership. The practical weight is that the natural deployment has administrators and supervisors authoring forms while an integration principal publishes them, which is exactly the failing path, and it fails after uploading, so each attempt leaves two private unlinked Files; the probe org finished with eighteen `ContentVersion` records against two successful commits. The obvious remedy was the one the project has deliberately forbidden, since ADR 0011 and ADR 0026 both pin `modifyAllRecords=false` and the package ships no sharing rules, groups, roles or Apex managed sharing, so the recommendation was to correct the document rather than widen a permission.

The correction was accepted and is documentation only. All four top-level trees, including `service` at `c727c7d3e16d75eef6fb9b9a22d5bd9d358b0d9c`, are byte-identical between `f4eabe0` and `6897d3f`, so the entire source review and live evidence carried over without repetition. Each of the nine new assertions was checked against the collected measurements rather than against the handoff: the record-level write prerequisite against the logged `INSUFFICIENT_ACCESS_OR_READONLY`; View All Records permitting cross-owner reads but not updates against the successful definition request; the private parent and controlled-by-parent master-detail against the object metadata; `modifyAllRecords` remaining false against the permission set; read, compile and attest then refuse against the observed sequence; the absence of added sharing against the byte-identical `salesforce` tree; successful publication requiring an owned or write-shared definition against the control experiment; the refusal leaving the Form Version unchanged against those versions remaining `digest=null`, `xform=null`, `xlsform=null` and `status=Draft` after both failed attempts; and the orphaned-Files obligation against the accumulated pairs. The verification section no longer treats the cross-owner fixture as a successful-publication fixture and states instead what it does prove, the note 69 wording was narrowed to the same-principal path, note 34 gained the scoped record-level write-access decision, the exclusion list gained sharing configuration, and the roadmap matches.

The composition order and request budget were proven live. An ordinary success uses exactly seven requests for one target object: `POST /services/apexrest/v1/publications/definition`, `GET /services/data/v64.0/sobjects/Account/describe`, `POST /services/data/v64.0/composite/sobjects`, the composite metadata read, two `VersionData` reads and `POST /services/apexrest/v1/publications/commit`. Every request carried `cache: no-store`, `credentials: omit`, `redirect: manual` and a Bearer header, and the token callback was invoked once per request, so tokens are genuinely fresh rather than hoisted.

The commit retry, which was the correction under review at `f4eabe0`, behaves exactly as claimed. With the first commit response deliberately lost after the server had really processed it, the result was `ok: true` with `alreadyCommitted: true`, a request count of eight, exactly two commit requests whose bodies were byte-identical, exactly one artifact upload, and a digest matching the result. The body is constructed once before the loop, so the second attempt necessarily reuses the same attested `ContentVersion` identifiers, and the published Form Version pinned exactly those identifiers and that digest. With both commit responses lost the operation refused with `PUBLICATION_PUBLISHER_COMMIT` after two commit requests and made no publication claim. A whole-operation retry is not falsely idempotent: re-running the entire operation against an already-published version uploads a fresh artifact pair and the commit then refuses, because `matchesCommit` requires the pinned identifiers to equal the submitted ones, so `alreadyCommitted: true` is reachable only from the internal retry with identical identifiers.

Restricted-principal target field-level security was proven against real Salesforce rather than inferred from system-mode Apex. Two probe fields were deployed to Account, with field access granted only to the permitted one. Both exist per `FieldDefinition`, so absence is genuinely field-level security, and the Integration principal's own Describe response contained `Kusanya_Probe_Visible__c` and not `Kusanya_Probe_Hidden__c`. Mapping to the hidden field refused after two requests, definition and Describe, with `[{"code":"PUBLISH_TARGET_FIELD","location":"mappings[0].fields[0].targetField"}]`, zero artifact uploads, zero commit requests and no field name anywhere in the diagnostics. Corrupting a byte of each `VersionData` response gave `PUBLICATION_ARTIFACT_MISMATCH`, zero commit requests, two DELETE requests and a net change of zero `ContentDocument` records, so no commit follows a failed attestation and both documents are removed. All `ContentVersion` records were created by the Integration principal and readable by it while the administrator saw none, carrying note 83 into composition, and the downloaded bytes equalled the bytes the publisher had uploaded for both artifacts.

Forty hostile inputs were driven through the compiled publisher. Thirty-six refused with `PUBLICATION_PUBLISHER_INPUT` at zero requests, zero token acquisitions and zero accessor invocations: ordinary and prototype-lying proxies, accessor-backed configuration and request properties, null-prototype objects, arrays, class instances, a symbol key, extra and missing keys, null inputs, fourteen and sixteen character identifiers, malformed identifiers, `http:`, non-Salesforce, credentialed, pathed, ported, trailing-slash, bare `salesforce.com` and malformed origins, every invalid timeout combination, and non-function validators. Accessors never ran, measured with a counting getter rather than inferred. The remaining four were boundary acceptances behaving correctly, since fifteen and eighteen character identifiers and the exact 30,000 millisecond and 120,000 millisecond bounds are accepted while fourteen, sixteen, 30,001 and 120,001 are refused; the verifier's own pass rule had flagged them only because they legitimately proceeded to the first request. One composition property is worth crediting: the publisher's own bounds of 30 seconds per request and 120 seconds overall are deliberately tighter than the nested ADR 0035 transport's 60 and 180 seconds, so a derived configuration can never be rejected on bounds.

The 21:28 to 21:36 audit window held that one entry and nothing else. Fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. The log contained no auth URL, refresh token, client secret, access token, private key, bearer string, scratch username or org identifier, with ten masked redactions, and the verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

This unit also exercised the ADR 0030 supersede workflow twice more, both times successfully, cancelling the zero-step waiting runs at `dd7c0ae` and at `f4eabe0` after checking out the trusted base rather than either pull-request head. That brings the record to three verified live cancellations against one correct refusal, and confirms note 84's scope precisely: the automation fires when the superseded head remains associated with the pull request and refuses only when a force-push has orphaned the commit. `PASS: PR #63 merged as ba397fb`, with squash tree `9bf399c8` identical to the verified head and all top-level trees unchanged. Post-merge main CI run 37995283683 failed twice on an external cause and succeeded on attempt 3. Both failures were `docker pull postgres:17` returning `net/http: request canceled (Client.Timeout exceeded while awaiting headers)` against `auth.docker.io`, at the `Initialize containers` step before any test ran, while `Scaffold and lint` passed on the same commit and the identical job had passed on the pull-request head with a byte-identical `service` tree. That is a Docker Hub availability problem on the runner network, not a Kusanya regression, and it is worth recording that `Service and container tests` is a required context whose service container is an unauthenticated pull of the mutable `postgres:17` tag, so a Docker Hub outage blocks every merge in the repository until it clears. Reducing that exposure would change `.github/workflows/ci.yml` and therefore needs its own reviewed unit.

Ledger: finding 1 raised and closed within this unit. Note 85 is new and informational: ADR 0036 now offers two routes to successful publication, a definition owned by the Integration principal or one explicitly shared to it for write, and only the first was demonstrated, alongside proof that the unshared cross-owner case refuses; the shared-for-write route is very likely correct, since Edit on the private `Form__c` parent would satisfy the user-mode update on its master-detail child, but it is presented as supported while untested and would be cheap to demonstrate in whichever unit first establishes sharing. **Note 69's initial-byte evidence is now compelled immediately before commit at this boundary and is answered for the same-principal path that can reach commit**, verified live through the attested result flowing straight into the commit body and the published Form Version pinning exactly those version identifiers and that digest; no cross-owner commit claim is made where write sharing is absent. Note 83 remains enforced in two independent places, the publisher's single principal throughout and `PublicationLifecycle.validateArtifacts` requiring `CreatedById` to equal the running user, which was observed passing in the debug log. Note 34 advances through publisher composition and a real restricted-principal target-FLS proof, and remains open for tenant connection selection, encrypted credential persistence and rotation, the publish CLI, and the scoped record-level write-access decision for administrator-authored definitions. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84 and 85 remain informational. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-16: PR #65 scoped publisher record access (ADR 0037); finding 1 raised and closed, note 85 closed, note 86 raised, PASS at `06f5fb6`

Two commits on accepted main `f8ac9cd`, both authored and committed by Bill Owiti: `cdc7a3a` added the scoped publisher record access and `06f5fb6` corrected it after this review raised finding 1. The unit answers the gap ADR 0036 left behind, where an administrator-authored definition could be read, compiled and attested by an integration principal but never committed, by adding an optional `Form__c.Publisher__c` lookup to one Salesforce User and maintaining exactly one `Form__Share` row with Edit access under a packaged `Publisher_Access__c` Apex sharing reason. `.github` and `service` were byte-identical to main, the harness pin appeared twice, `scripts/` differed only in two test files with all seven harness and policy scripts individually identical, and no dependency or lockfile changed. Local evidence at the exact head reproduced at root 278 of 278 with Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` clean.

**Finding 1, raised against `cdc7a3a` and closed by `06f5fb6`: the package did not deploy.** A fresh Developer-edition scratch org created from this repository's own `config/project-scratch-def.json` rejected the deploy at 157 of 162 with five errors. There was one root cause, the `Publisher_Access` sharing reason failing with `'Publisher_Access' does not resolve to a valid sObject type, fullName must end with: __as, __b, __c, __e, __kav, __mdt, __x`, and four cascades, because `PublisherRecordAccess` references `Schema.Form__Share.RowCause.Publisher_Access__c` twice, its test once, and the trigger could not compile without the class. This mattered more than an ordinary test failure because the harness performs exactly that one deploy into a brand-new org, so the protected run would have failed at `source-deploy` before any Apex test executed, spending an approval. Four experiments narrowed it, each with the working tree restored afterwards: a second full deploy into the same org with `Form__c` already present failed identically, ruling out same-deploy ordering; isolating the component reproduced the error alone against `objects/Form__c.object`; and renaming the same file to an unrelated reason failed identically, which established that the chosen name was not at fault and that no sharing reason deployed at all by that route. Source conversion emitted the conventional `<members>Form__c.Publisher_Access</members>` shape, so the layout was right and the defect lay in the full name itself. A related observation was recorded at the time: `scripts/publisher-record-access.test.mjs` asserted the exact undeployable value, so the root suite was green at 278 of 278 while the package could not deploy, which is precisely the gap the protected scratch-org gate exists to close.

The correction renamed the component and its full name to `Publisher_Access__c`, and the result is unambiguous. A fresh Developer-edition scratch org accepted the full package at **161 of 161 with zero errors**, with `[SharingReason] Form__c.Publisher_Access__c`, `[ApexClass] PublisherRecordAccess`, `[ApexClass] PublisherRecordAccessTest` and `[ApexTrigger] FormPublisherAccess` all reported as successes, so the sharing reason resolves and every Apex component compiles.

The cross-owner path was then exercised end to end in that org, with every definition owned by the administrator and published by a non-administrator principal on a `Standard Platform User` profile holding only `Kusanya_Integration`. Before selection the publication was refused with `PUBLICATION_PUBLISHER_COMMIT` after eight requests and two commit attempts, reproducing ADR 0036's boundary exactly. Selecting the publisher created one share, `Edit` access under `RowCause: Publisher_Access__c`, to that user. The same publication then succeeded with `ok: true` in **exactly seven requests** and `alreadyCommitted: false`, which is the canonical ADR 0036 path reached cross-owner for the first time. Replacing the publisher removed the previous share and left exactly one share for the replacement; clearing the publisher left no reason-scoped share at all; and a publication attempted after clearing was refused again with `PUBLICATION_PUBLISHER_COMMIT`, so revocation removes both the share and the capability. Throughout all of these transitions the record's existing `All`/`Owner` share was present and never disturbed, which is the unrelated-share isolation requirement.

Ownership transfer, the revocation-bypass vector this reviewer raised against the first head, is closed from two directions. As the selected publisher, attempting to take ownership of a Form it holds Edit on was refused by the platform itself with `INSUFFICIENT_ACCESS_OR_READONLY: insufficient access rights on object id`, and the administrator remained the owner. That left the new guard untested, so it was made reachable by having an administrator select themselves as publisher, the one case where the platform permits the update and `before.Publisher__c == UserInfo.getUserId()` holds; the guard then fired with its exact message, `FIELD_CUSTOM_VALIDATION_EXCEPTION: The selected publisher cannot transfer ownership of this Form.` The same test confirmed that a publisher equal to the owner suppresses the redundant share, leaving only the Owner row.

The two fail-closed behaviours documented in the correction were both confirmed live. Selecting an inactive user as publisher failed with `CANNOT_INSERT_UPDATE_ACTIVATE_ENTITY` caused by `INACTIVE_OWNER_OR_USER`, and the whole transaction rolled back, leaving `Publisher__c` null and only the Owner share. Selecting a user who lacks `Kusanya_Integration` did create the share, correctly, because the trigger's responsibility is record access alone, and that user still could not publish: the platform refused with `FORBIDDEN: You do not have access to the Apex class named: DefinitionSnapshotResource`. Record access and capability are therefore independent grants, and selecting a publisher confers only the former.

The escalation question was tested directly rather than inferred. The publisher holds Edit on the Form it publishes, and it still could not appoint itself or anyone else: `INVALID_FIELD_FOR_INSERT_UPDATE: Unable to create/update fields: Publisher__c`, with the field unchanged afterwards. Live field-level security read `Kusanya_Admin` readable and editable, `Kusanya_Integration` readable and not editable, and `Kusanya_Supervisor` readable and not editable, and `PermissionsModifyAllRecords = true` returned zero rows across every Kusanya permission set, with View All Records unchanged at eighteen rows on the definition objects. No queue existed and no user, permission set or group was created by the unit; the only additional permission set in the org was the verifier's own disposable probe grant for Account target access, which is not part of the package.

Published state pinned exactly what was attested: `Status__c` Published, `Publication_Digest__c` equal to the digest the publisher returned, both `XForm_Version__c` and `XLSForm_Version__c` equal to the attested ContentVersion identifiers, and `Published_By__c` the publisher principal rather than the administrator. Every `ContentVersion` was created by the publisher principal and the administrator saw none, so note 83's same-principal Files constraint continues to hold through the new sharing path. The Apex gate run at the full local test level with the pinned checker reported `outcome Passed; testsRan 101; passing 101; failing 0; skipped 0` with 101 detail rows and `Apex: 101 passed; 1428/1473 lines covered (96.95%)`, with `PublisherRecordAccess` at 39 of 39 and `FormPublisherAccess` at 5 of 5.

Hosted run 38311211487 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, and its figures were identical to the independent reproduction at `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`. These are the first changed Apex figures in four units, which is correct because this unit genuinely adds Apex, where the three preceding units correctly left them unmoved. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only. Every forty-character hex string in the 526-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the reviewed head 44 times, the pin 6 times, the `actions/checkout` and `actions/setup-node` pins, and `f3dc54e1`, the Azure runner image provisioner commit. The single marker identifies `38311211487-1`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`, with one trusted subject line and one ownership tag, and the gate took `POLICY_DECISION: full-apex` with `BUDGET_ALLOWED: true` and `APEX_RESULT: success`.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__38311211487-1__06f5fb6e5244__186562ab0455`, whose `ScratchOrgInfo` the Dev Hub independently showed created at 13:33:15 with `CreatedById` equal to the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null `ErrorCode`, a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 13:34:36 by that same user, and zero `ActiveScratchOrg` records. The 13:30 to 13:40 audit window held that one entry and nothing else, which is worth stating explicitly for a unit that introduces sharing: the only org change the run made was creating and deleting its own scratch org. Fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the dedicated CI user at 13:33:10 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication. The log contained no auth URL, refresh token, client secret, access token, private key, bearer string, scratch username or org identifier, with ten masked redactions, and the verifier's authorization remained usable afterwards.

The pull request merged `CLEAN` with all six checks green, confirmed by computing the intersection of red checks against the required contexts rather than reading the list, which returned no red checks at all.

Ledger: finding 1 raised and closed within this unit. **Note 85 is CLOSED.** It had recorded that ADR 0036's "explicitly shared for write" route to successful publication was documented but never demonstrated; that route is now proven end to end, with an administrator-authored definition shared for write through the `Publisher_Access__c` reason and published by a non-administrator Integration principal in seven requests, and with revocation proven to remove both the share and the capability. **The scoped record-level write-access portion of note 34 is closed on the same evidence.** Note 86 is new and informational: for the case it was written for, a non-owner publisher, the `validateOwnerTransfers` guard is unreachable because the platform refuses the transfer first, so it is defence in depth rather than the operative control; it was nonetheless proven to fire correctly where reachable and should be kept, since it covers a configuration in which transfer is permitted, and this is the same pattern as notes 76 and 82. Note 83 remains enforced, now through the sharing path as well. Note 34 remains open for tenant connection selection, encrypted credential persistence and rotation, and the publish CLI.
