## 2026-10-19: PR #67 encrypted tenant connection vault (ADR 0038); 0 findings, note 87 raised, PASS at `3bbc5e2`

One commit `3bbc5e2` on accepted main `c616dbb`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, added the first persisted tenant state in Kusanya: an OAuth authorization-code with PKCE decision, a normalized one-org-per-tenant binding, tenant and org bound AES-256-GCM refresh-token envelopes, a rotatable deployment keyring with conditional rewrap, an exact tenant-scoped PostgreSQL repository and explicit transactional, advisory-locked, checksum-pinned migrations. Sixteen files changed, 1,572 insertions. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were byte-identical to main and the harness pin appeared twice, so no workflow, harness, pin, policy-script or Salesforce metadata change. `service/package.json` did change, which the trust-boundary check flags by default, so it was inspected: it adds one npm script, `"migrate": "node dist/src/migrate.js"`, and no dependency, so no lockfile change was needed and none was present. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: service unit 254 of 254, root 278 of 278, service lint, service typecheck, Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` all clean. The integration suite was run against a real database rather than skipped, giving 3 of 3 passed with zero skips where the builder's local run reported two expected skips.

Verification ran 90 adversarial probes against the compiled module and a genuine PostgreSQL 17.11 instance started locally for the purpose and removed afterwards, so the database claims rest on measurement rather than inference. No Salesforce scratch capacity was consumed by this unit.

The cryptographic envelope is AES-256-GCM with a fresh 96-bit random nonce and a 128-bit tag, and the additional authenticated data is `kusanya:tenant-credential:v1` followed by NUL, the tenant UUID, NUL and the normalized org identifier. The NUL delimiters are load bearing and correct, because neither field can contain NUL under its own pattern, so no two distinct tenant and org pairs can collide by concatenation. Thirty-nine probes covered the cryptography. Round-trip, envelope freezing and exact key sets passed. A ciphertext copied to another tenant and a ciphertext copied to another org were each refused with `TENANT_CREDENTIAL_DECRYPTION`, which is the property ADR 0038 claims and the one that matters most. Tampering the ciphertext, the tag or the nonce was refused in each case. Five hundred seals produced five hundred distinct nonces, consistent with a fresh `randomBytes(12)` per operation, which is the critical precondition for GCM safety. After rotating the active key with both keys present, an old-key envelope still opened, `needsRotation` reported true, `rewrap` moved the envelope to the active key while preserving the plaintext and changing the ciphertext, and `needsRotation` then reported false. Removing a key from the ring produced a decryption failure rather than a crash, and rewrapping with the wrong context was refused. Hostile shapes were refused with `TENANT_CREDENTIAL_INPUT` on both the envelope and the context, covering ordinary and prototype-lying proxies, null-prototype objects, extra and missing keys, `schemaVersion: 2`, non-canonical base64, wrong nonce and tag lengths and an oversize ciphertext; accessor-backed properties were refused and the getter never ran, measured with a counting getter rather than assumed. Configuration refused a non-32-byte key, an absent active key, more than eight keys and a duplicate key identifier in `TENANT_CREDENTIAL_KEYS`. No error message or stack contained the plaintext token or any key, asserted on every refusal rather than spot-checked.

The migration boundary was exercised against the real database. It applies on a clean database, is repeatable with the second run a no-op leaving exactly one row, and refuses on checksum drift with the fixed `MigrationError: DATABASE_MIGRATION_FAILED`. Three concurrent migrators were run simultaneously and all three fulfilled while leaving exactly one row, which confirms the serialisation works in practice. The advisory lock is taken with `pg_advisory_xact_lock` inside the transaction and after `BEGIN`, so it releases automatically on commit or rollback and the migrations table is created under the lock rather than before it. One detail deserves credit because it is easy to get wrong: the checksum column is `char(64)`, which PostgreSQL blank-pads, and the comparison applies `.trim()`; without that every re-run would have reported false drift.

The tenant store was exercised the same way. A second tenant claiming an org already bound to another tenant was refused with `TENANT_CONNECTION_CONFLICT`, and repointing an existing tenant to a different org was refused with the same code while leaving the stored org unchanged. The two refusals arise from different mechanisms, which is the right design: the first is the `UNIQUE` constraint surfacing as SQLSTATE 23505, while the second is the upsert's `WHERE tenant_connections.salesforce_org_id = EXCLUDED.salesforce_org_id` guard, with `salesforce_org_id` never appearing in the `DO UPDATE SET` list at all. Loading an unknown tenant returned undefined with no fallback row. Every statement in the file is tenant-scoped; there are exactly four, and both reads carry `WHERE tenant_id = $1::uuid`. The credential version starts at one and bumps on re-save. Neither the refresh token nor any key material appeared in any column at rest, checked by dumping every row. Rotation through the store moved the stored key identifier from the old key to the active key, left the token readable, and correctly left `credential_version` unchanged, since that version tracks the credential rather than the key that wraps it; a second rotation was correctly a no-op. `rotateEncryption` additionally guards its update with `AND credential_version = $7 AND credential_key_id = $8`, so a concurrent change yields a conflict instead of a lost update. Org identity normalisation behaved as documented, converting a 15-character identifier to the checked 18-character form, refusing an 18-character identifier carrying a wrong checksum, and refusing a non-`00D` identifier.

A further twenty-seven probes covered origins, namespaces and input objects. Refused with a fixed code were `http` origins, non-Salesforce origins and origins carrying a path, port, credentials, query, fragment or trailing slash, on both the authorization origin and the instance origin; namespaces without the double underscore, containing a path character or a space, or overlong; and hostile input objects including proxies, prototype-lying proxies, extra and missing keys and a non-UUID tenant. An accessor on the input was refused and its getter never ran. An empty namespace and a valid `ksny__` namespace were both accepted.

ADR 0038's substantive claims were checked against measurement rather than read. AES-256-GCM with a 96-bit nonce and 128-bit tag, additional authenticated data binding schema, tenant and org, the statement that copying a ciphertext to another tenant or org fails authentication, storage of only the key identifier, nonce, ciphertext and tag in canonical base64, no encryption key in PostgreSQL, at most eight 32-byte keys, fixed error classifications, the rule that reconnecting a tenant may replace the credential only when the org identifier is unchanged, and the 15-to-18 normalisation rule all hold exactly as written. The exclusions it claims, namely the callback, the PKCE transaction, token exchange, the refresh transport and administrator authorization, are genuinely absent from the diff.

Note 87 is new and informational, and arose from two probe expectations that were wrong in an instructive way. `TenantConnectionStore.save()` declares its failures through `TenantConnectionFailureCode`, and `decodeInput` checks `typeof refreshToken === 'string'`, but the token's shape is validated inside `vault.seal`, so an empty token or one containing a control character escapes as a `TenantCredentialError` carrying `TENANT_CREDENTIAL_INPUT` rather than a `TenantConnectionError`. Both are fixed-code and secret-free, so nothing leaks and nothing wrong is accepted. The only consequence is for callers, since a `catch` narrowing on `instanceof TenantConnectionError` will miss token-shape refusals from `save()`. Worth either documenting on the method or normalising at the store boundary when the OAuth route is composed.

The protected run was judged required rather than merely permitted. The unit changes no Salesforce metadata and no Apex, but it does change service code, so the policy classifies it `full-apex` rather than documentation-exempt and the gate cannot pass without a successful Apex run. Hosted run 38932573195 attempt 1 then passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, and its figures read `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, unchanged from PR #65. Unchanged figures are the correct result and serve as a negative control confirming the Salesforce tree really is untouched, which the byte-identical tree comparison also shows. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only. Every forty-character hex string in the 526-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the reviewed head 44 times, the pin 6 times, the `actions/checkout` and `actions/setup-node` pins, and `f3dc54e1`, the Azure runner image provisioner commit. The single marker identifies `38932573195-1`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`, with one trusted subject line and one ownership tag, and the gate took `POLICY_DECISION: full-apex` with `BUDGET_ALLOWED: true` and `APEX_RESULT: success`.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__38932573195-1__3bbc5e2644df__1285dacecaca`, whose `ScratchOrgInfo` the Dev Hub independently showed created at 16:44:26 with `CreatedById` equal to the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null `ErrorCode`, a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 16:45:54 by that same user, and zero `ActiveScratchOrg` records. The 16:42 to 16:50 audit window held that one entry and nothing else. Fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the dedicated CI user at 16:44:21 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication. The log contained no auth URL, refresh token, client secret, access token, private key, bearer string, scratch username or org identifier, with ten masked redactions. Because this unit introduces an encryption keyring, the log was additionally searched for `TENANT_CREDENTIAL`, covering both the environment variable names and any key material, with zero matches. The verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

`PASS: PR #67 merged as e3b7343`, with squash tree `7c1419c` identical to the verified head and all five top-level trees unchanged. The pull request merged `CLEAN` with every check green, confirmed by computing the intersection of red checks against the required contexts rather than reading the list, which returned no red checks at all.

Ledger: zero findings. Note 87 is new and informational, as described above. This unit advances note 34's encrypted credential persistence strand without closing it, because Salesforce refresh-token rotation, tenant connection selection from trusted authentication and the publish CLI all remain outside it, exactly as ADR 0038 states. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86 and 87 remain informational. No C10.14 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-19: PR #69 attempt-scoped tenant Salesforce sessions (ADR 0039); 0 findings, note 87 closed, note 88 raised, PASS at `ca8858e`

One commit `ca8858e` on accepted main `4543183`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, turned the stored tenant binding of ADR 0038 into a usable Salesforce session: one connection selected by an internal trusted tenant UUID, the encrypted refresh token exchanged through a bounded no-cache form POST, the returned identity bound to the stored org and instance origin, refresh and rotation serialized across replicas by a PostgreSQL row lock, a rotated token persisted before any access token is exposed, and the access token held only in a frozen attempt-scoped object behind a non-enumerated capability. Eleven files changed, 1,252 insertions. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were byte-identical to main, the harness pin appeared twice, and no `package.json` or lockfile changed at all. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 264 of 264, service lint, service typecheck, Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` all clean. The integration suite was run against a disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 with `DATABASE_SSL=false` and `REQUIRE_DATABASE_TESTS=true`, giving 3 of 3 passed with **zero skips**, confirming the builder's server-side claim independently.

Fifty adversarial probes were driven against the compiled module and that real database. No Salesforce scratch capacity was consumed by the review itself; everything ran in a local PostgreSQL container that was removed afterwards.

Tenant selection admits no untrusted authority. The only identifier the operation accepts is a UUID, and there is no parameter for an instance URL, Salesforce org ID, namespace or header, so nothing untrusted has a path across a tenant boundary. A non-UUID is refused with `TENANT_SALESFORCE_SESSION_INPUT` before any database work and an absent row yields only `TENANT_SALESFORCE_SESSION_NOT_CONNECTED`, which discloses nothing about other tenants. The authorization origin, instance origin, normalized org ID, namespace and encrypted refresh-token capability all come from the selected row.

Serialization was proven rather than read. `withRefreshLock` opens a transaction, takes `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE` on the exact tenant row, runs the whole exchange under that lock, persists any rotated token in the same transaction and commits before returning, rolling back and releasing the connection on any error. Two sessions against the same tenant through two separate store instances, the first with a deliberately slow exchange, produced the ordering `start:RT3 end:RT3 start:RT4 end:RT4`: the second refresh did not begin its exchange until the first had finished and committed, and the final stored token was the later rotation with the credential version advanced twice. A different tenant was measured as unaffected.

Response binding is strict on every axis that was attacked. Refused were an `instance_url` belonging to another tenant, an `id` whose org segment belongs to another tenant, a `token_type` other than `Bearer`, any unknown response key, a malformed `id`, an `id` on a non-Salesforce host, an `id` with the wrong path shape, an `id` whose user segment is not a `005` identifier, and a `token_format` other than `jwt`. The org segment is normalized before comparison, so a 15-character or wrong-case alias cannot slip past.

Secret handling held under direct attack. `JSON.stringify` of the session contains neither the access token nor the refresh token, `getAccessToken` is non-enumerable, non-writable and non-configurable on a frozen object, and the client secret and refresh token appear only in the request body, never in the URL or headers. An error thrown from the injected fetch whose message embedded both the client secret and the refresh token was swallowed into the fixed `TENANT_SALESFORCE_SESSION_REFRESH` code. Every refusal in the suite was asserted against the client secret, refresh token and access token appearing in the message or stack, and none ever did.

Ordering and the unavoidable failure window were both exercised. After a successful rotation the database held the new token with the credential version incremented and no plaintext token or access token in any column. Simulating a rotation returned by Salesforce followed by an impossible database write produced `TENANT_SALESFORCE_SESSION_ROTATION`, exposed no session, left the previous token stored and did not advance the version. The policy is sound: no local transaction can make a remote rotation and a local commit atomic, and the unit does not pretend otherwise. It fails closed, never retries the dead token, and raises a code distinct from an ordinary refresh failure so an operator can tell that reauthorization rather than a retry is required. ADR 0039 states exactly that rather than claiming a guarantee the two systems cannot provide.

Transport bounds held. The request is a form POST to the stored authorization origin with `cache: no-store`, `credentials: omit` and `redirect: manual`. Refused were a non-200, a redirected response, a non-JSON media type, malformed JSON, invalid UTF-8, a declared `content-length` over the cap and a streamed body over the cap. A stalled fetch that honours the abort signal, which is what Node's built-in `fetch` does, timed out in 311 milliseconds against a 300 millisecond budget. Proxy and accessor-backed configurations were refused and the configuration accessor never ran, measured with a counting getter.

Note 88 is new and informational. The timeout depends entirely on the injected `fetchImplementation` honouring the abort signal. Unlike the two sibling transports already reviewed, the ADR 0035 artifact transport and the ADR 0036 publisher, which each race the operation against a rejecting timeout, this module sets an `AbortController` and a timer but never races. Measured with a 250 millisecond budget, a signal-ignoring fetch was still pending after 2,509 milliseconds, a same-tenant refresh through a different store instance was blocked behind it after 2,515 milliseconds, and a different tenant completed in 22 milliseconds. The blast radius is therefore exactly one tenant, because the lock is per row, and the shipped default path is bounded, so this is not a finding. It is recorded because this module is the one place where an unbounded call holds a PostgreSQL row lock, so a non-conforming fetch would stall that tenant's refreshes across every replica rather than one request. Wrapping the exchange in the same `Promise.race` the other two transports already use would make the bound hold regardless and make the three transports consistent.

**Note 87 is closed.** The store now validates refresh-token shape at its own boundary, and empty, control-character and oversize tokens are each refused with `TenantConnectionError` carrying `TENANT_CONNECTION_INPUT`. A caller narrowing on `instanceof TenantConnectionError` no longer misses token-shape refusals, which was the whole of the note.

One probe expectation was wrong and the correction belongs in the record. The assertion that `createTenantSalesforceSession.length === 4` failed because JavaScript's `Function.length` stops counting at the first defaulted parameter and `fetchImplementation` has a default, so the arity is 3. The signature is `(store, trustedTenantId, configuration, fetchImplementation?)` exactly as documented, and the property being tested, that no instance URL, org ID or namespace is accepted as authority, holds.

The protected run was judged required rather than optional, because the unit changes service code and the policy therefore classifies it `full-apex`. Hosted run 39258110451 attempt 1 then passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, reporting `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, identical to PR #65 and PR #67. Three consecutive units with unchanged figures, each against a fresh scratch org, is positive evidence that the Salesforce side is untouched rather than a stale reading, and it matches the byte-identical `salesforce` tree. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only. ## 2026-10-19: PR #71 bounds the tenant refresh deadline independently of fetch abort compliance; 0 findings, note 88 closed, PASS at `be9a758`

One commit `be9a758` on accepted main `5c8130b`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, answered the verifier's own note 88. The refresh exchange in `service/src/tenant/salesforce-session.ts` is now wrapped in an inner async function and raced against a locally rejecting timeout as well as aborting the supplied signal, which is the pattern the ADR 0035 artifact transport and the ADR 0036 publisher already used. Seven files changed, 139 insertions and 75 deletions, touching only that module, its unit test, the database integration test and four documents. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were byte-identical to main, the harness pin appeared twice in `salesforce-verify.yml`, and no `package.json`, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace changed. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 264 of 264, service lint, service typecheck, Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` all clean. The integration suite ran against a disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 with `DATABASE_SSL=false` and `REQUIRE_DATABASE_TESTS=true`, giving 3 of 3 passed with zero skips. The container was removed afterwards and no Salesforce capacity was consumed by the review itself.

Twenty-one adversarial probes were driven against the compiled module and that real database, and all twenty-one passed. The probe that matters most is delayed late success, because it is the case where a bounded refusal could still leak a credential behind the caller's back. A transport was given a 200 millisecond deadline and made to resolve a structurally valid response carrying a rotated refresh token 2,000 milliseconds later. The operation refused with the fixed `TENANT_SALESFORCE_SESSION_REFRESH` code after 219 milliseconds, returned no session, and produced an error containing neither the client secret, nor the stored refresh token, nor the late rotated token. The row lock went with it: a later same-tenant `withRefreshLock` entered after 4 milliseconds, the rollback left the original token in place, and the credential version did not advance. The abandoned transport was then allowed to resolve and the database was re-examined, including the raw row rather than the decrypting accessor, and the late rotated token was absent, the stored token was still the original, the credential version was still 1, and no unhandled rejection had been recorded.

The exact condition described in note 88 is now bounded. A fetch implementation that returns a promise which never settles and never listens for abort, measured at a 250 millisecond budget, was still pending after 2,509 milliseconds in PR #69; here it was refused after 263 milliseconds and its lock was released within 5 milliseconds. A transport that rejects 1,200 milliseconds after a 150 millisecond deadline, with the client secret embedded in its rejection message, produced the fixed refusal code and its late rejection was not unhandled. The happy path remained intact, returning a session, persisting the rotation and advancing the version. Listener hygiene held across the whole probe run, with zero process-level unhandled rejections and an `unhandledRejection` listener count of 1, and the deadline timer is unreferenced from the event loop and cleared in a `finally`, so a short deadline cannot hold the process open and a long one cannot outlive a fast success.

The tests became stricter rather than looser, which is worth recording because a timeout test can be weakened while still appearing to pass. The previous unit test supplied a fetch that rejected when its abort signal fired, so it only ever proved abort compliance, which is precisely the assumption note 88 questioned. The replacement supplies a promise that never settles and never registers an abort listener, and it additionally asserts that the signal was aborted, so the signal is proven to be sent as well as proven unnecessary. The database integration test adds the same shape at a 25 millisecond deadline and then asserts that a later same-tenant lock enters and observes credential version 3 with the prior token, which is real serialization evidence rather than a mock.

Documentation was checked claim by claim against measured behaviour rather than read for plausibility. ADR 0039 now states that the deadline is enforced by racing the complete exchange against a local rejecting timeout as well as aborting the supplied signal, that a transport ignoring `AbortSignal` cannot keep the tenant's PostgreSQL row lock past the deadline, and that its abandoned operation cannot expose a session or commit a rotated token. Each of those three claims corresponds to a probe result above. The ADR also describes the timeout test as deliberately using a fetch implementation that never settles and ignores its abort signal, and requires the real-database test to prove lock release and later entry, both true in the diff. The threat model's credential row now reads that ADRs 0038 and 0039 enforce a fetch-independent row-lock deadline, which is the accurate description. The roadmap and `service/README.md` match. Nothing overstates what the code does and the limitations already recorded for ADR 0039 are untouched.

## 2026-10-19: PR #73 adds the one-time Salesforce administrator authorization callback (ADR 0040); 0 findings, notes 89 to 93 raised, PASS at `45ab6df`

One commit `45ab6df` on accepted main `5859149`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, added the first administrator grant: an internal authorization-code start with S256 PKCE, a one-time callback, and a new `tenant_oauth_transactions` table created by migration 0002. Eleven files changed, 1,632 insertions and 32 deletions, comprising the new `service/src/tenant/oauth-authorization.ts` at 706 lines, 542 lines of unit tests, an expanded database integration test, ADR 0040, and accuracy edits to the architecture note, roadmap, threat model, service README and the ADR 0038 and 0039 cross-references. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were byte-identical to main, with the `.github` tree hash `a55cb1b8decb2906562fad89d39bc71f7b329265` on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice in `salesforce-verify.yml`, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace changed. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 275 of 275, service lint, service typecheck, Prettier, the scaffold check and `git diff --check` all clean. The integration suite ran against a disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 with `DATABASE_SSL=false` and `REQUIRE_DATABASE_TESTS=true`, giving 4 of 4 passed with zero skips, including the new case asserting that OAuth state is single-use and that a complete signed grant is the only durable connection. The container was removed afterwards and no Salesforce capacity was consumed by the review itself.

This unit received heavier adversarial attention than its predecessors because it is the first boundary at which a value arriving from outside the trust boundary can create or replace a durable tenant-to-org credential, a concern recorded in the PR #71 relay before this work began. One hundred and thirty-five assertions were driven against the compiled module and that real database, and all one hundred and thirty-five passed.

Single use, concurrency and supersession were proven against PostgreSQL rather than reasoned about. A replayed state is refused `TENANT_OAUTH_STATE` and the row is gone after first use. Two genuinely concurrent callbacks, issued through two separate connection pools with two separate store instances and a deliberately slow exchange, produced exactly one winner and one `TENANT_OAUTH_STATE` refusal, left exactly one connection row, and left `credential_version` at 1. That last figure is the assertion that matters, because it proves the authorization code was not exchanged into the row twice rather than merely that one caller observed an error. A newer start atomically replaces its tenant's pending transaction, after which the superseded state is refused and only the newest works; five concurrent starts for one tenant still left exactly one pending row, which the table's `UNIQUE` constraint on `tenant_id` guarantees. An expired transaction is refused.

The PKCE and storage claims were recomputed independently rather than read. The `code_challenge` in the returned authorization URL equals SHA-256 of HMAC-SHA256 over the versioned domain `kusanya:tenant-oauth-pkce:v1` with a NUL separator and the random state under the deployment state secret, base64url at both steps, alongside `code_challenge_method=S256`, `response_type=code` and the scope `api refresh_token`. The URL carries no verifier, no client secret and no state secret. The durable row holds exactly SHA-256(state) as hex plus the trusted binding, and its column set is precisely `authorization_origin`, `created_at`, `expected_salesforce_org_id`, `expires_at`, `namespace_prefix`, `state_digest` and `tenant_id`. An attacker holding the entire row cannot reconstruct the grant, since the state is 256 bits of randomness behind SHA-256 and the verifier additionally requires the deployment secret. That is a stronger property for the stated threat of database disclosure than storing a random verifier would have been. The ADR's rotation claim was also confirmed by capturing the outgoing form body: after a state-secret rotation the verifier transmitted is the new derivation and differs from the old one, so a pending transaction cannot survive rotation.

Response binding held on every axis attacked. Salesforce's signature, an HMAC-SHA256 over the identity URL concatenated with `issued_at` keyed by the client secret, is compared with `timingSafeEqual` behind an explicit length check and a canonical base64 round trip; a tampered signature, a signature over a different `issued_at`, a signature made with a different client secret, an empty signature, a non-canonical base64 signature and a signature of the wrong length were each refused with no connection created. A validly signed grant for a different org is refused `TENANT_OAUTH_BINDING`, and a case-variant org ID is refused as well. The reason was verified rather than assumed: `00Dbm00000yeiz3` and `00DBM00000YEIZ3` normalize to `00Dbm00000yeiz3EAA` and `00DBM00000YEIZ32AP`, so case is encoded in the checksum suffix rather than folded away, and a fifteen-character expected org correctly normalizes and binds to the eighteen-character response.

Hostile shapes, partial responses and the deadline all behaved. Accessor-backed callback and configuration objects are refused with counting getters proving zero invocations, and proxies, null-prototype objects, symbol keys, extra keys, both `code` and `error` together, short or non-base64url state, arrays, `null` and bare strings are all refused `TENANT_OAUTH_INPUT`. Seventeen malformed or partial provider bodies and five transport-level failures were each refused with no connection row and a secret-free error, including a fetch that throws with the client secret embedded in its message. The deadline follows the fetch-independent pattern established in PR #71: a signal-ignoring transport was bounded at 261 milliseconds against a 250 millisecond budget, and a delayed late success carrying a complete, correctly signed grant with a valid refresh token resolving 1,500 milliseconds after a 200 millisecond deadline was refused at 215 milliseconds, after which the resolved transport still produced no connection, the refresh token was absent from the raw database, and nothing was unhandled.

Partial persistence was examined closely because it is where an existing customer credential could be damaged. With a tenant already bound to one org, a complete and correctly signed grant for a different org is refused `TENANT_OAUTH_CONNECTION` and the existing row is byte-identical before and after, with the credential version not advanced and still exactly one row. The mechanism is the connection store's `WHERE tenant_connections.salesforce_org_id = EXCLUDED.salesforce_org_id` guard on the conflict path, so a rebind cannot be smuggled in through re-authorization. A store that throws at the final step leaves no row at all for a fresh tenant and reports a secret-free `TENANT_OAUTH_CONNECTION`. Same-org re-authorization, the supported path, succeeds and advances the version. The returned result is frozen and contains no access token, refresh token, authorization code, client secret or state; the access token is shape-validated and discarded rather than exposed.

Two judgment questions were put by the brief and both are answered affirmatively. On whether the trusted expected-org input is sufficiently explicit, it is: `expectedSalesforceOrgId` is a required explicit parameter with no default, no inference from the authorization origin and no fallback to whichever org answers, it cannot be supplied by the callback or the provider, and a validly signed wrong-org grant was proven to be refused. The service README states the obligation on the future route in the right terms, that it must supply the trusted tenant and expected org and must not accept either from request headers or callback parameters. On whether consuming state before the external exchange is the correct fail-closed policy, it is, and the alternative should not be accepted. Consuming first makes the state single-use regardless of what happens downstream, so neither a stolen code nor a retried callback can be exchanged twice, which the concurrency probe demonstrates. It also avoids holding a database transaction across external I/O, which is exactly the row-lock-across-a-network-call problem that note 88 concerned. Deferring consumption until after a successful exchange would open a replay and double-exchange window. The cost is the loss of automatic retry, which for a one-time administrator action is the right trade. Two details improve on the minimum: the callback and configuration are both validated before the transaction is consumed, so a misconfigured deployment does not burn a grant, and a provider error callback consumes the state with zero fetch attempts, measured with a counting transport.

Two probe defects are disclosed because they both initially looked like results. The first case-variant org test was vacuous, because the variant was built with `toUpperCase()` applied to `00D000000000001`, which is all digits and therefore unchanged, so the grant was correctly accepted and the assertion meant nothing; rebuilt with a genuinely mixed-case org ID, the binding refuses it. The second was a backdated-expiry setup that violated the table's own `CHECK (expires_at > created_at)`. That is the constraint working as intended, so it was retained as an assertion in its own right and the whole window was moved into the past instead. The lesson that an all-digit Salesforce identifier is invariant under case transformation is worth keeping, because it makes a case-sensitivity test silently vacuous rather than failing.

Documentation was checked against measured behaviour. ADR 0040, the architecture note, roadmap, threat model, service README and the ADR 0038 and 0039 cross-references all match, the threat model's new row for OAuth callback replay or wrong-org grant is accurate, and every document repeats that no public route is exposed and that trusted administrator authentication and tenant provisioning remain.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39288884035 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, the trusted budget reporting `{"allowed":true,"kind":"initial","reason":"No prior attempt consumed this head and UTC day."}`, and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`. That figure is identical to PRs #65, #67, #69 and #71, and five consecutive units with unchanged figures, each against a fresh scratch org, is positive evidence that the Salesforce side is untouched rather than a stale reading. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only. Every forty-character hex string in the 941-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the `actions/setup-node` pin 65 times, the reviewed head 62 times, the harness pin 12 times, the `actions/checkout` pin 10 times, and `f3dc54e1` three times, the Azure runner image provisioner commit, which is not a repository reference. The single marker identified `39288884035-1`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`, and the gate took `POLICY_DECISION: full-apex` with `BUDGET_ALLOWED: true` and `APEX_RESULT: success`. Steps 7 and 8 rechecked the exact head and the retry budget immediately before authentication, and step 11 re-read the live head after testing, all passing.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39288884035-1__45ab6df2d272__8bff3a815412`, whose `ScratchOrgInfo` the Dev Hub independently showed created at 17:12:39 with `CreatedById` equal to the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null `ErrorCode`, a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 17:14:11 by that same user, and zero `ActiveScratchOrg` records. The log reported one owned org deleted and none already deleted, the 17:05 to 17:20 audit window held that one entry and nothing else, fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the dedicated CI user at 17:12:34 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, one second before the marker's `startedAt`, with no verifier authentication. Because this unit introduces OAuth state and PKCE material, the credential search was extended beyond the usual patterns to `state_secret`, `stateSecret`, `code_verifier`, `TENANT_OAUTH`, `TENANT_CREDENTIAL` and `DATABASE_URL`, with zero matches across all of them and zero `00D` org identifiers, alongside 21 masked redactions. The single `sfdx-url` occurrence is the same explained case as the previous run, the echoed command line of the step named "Authenticate dedicated Dev Hub without printing its auth URL", in which that string is the `sf org login` subcommand name rather than a credential. The verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

`PASS: PR #73 merged as c92ab95`, with squash tree `9983be2` identical to the verified head tree, so the squash changed no content. The merge was confirmed by computing the intersection of red checks against the three required contexts, which returned no red checks at all. One process point belongs in the record: an earlier report that the pull request had merged was checked before anything was relayed and found to be incorrect, the pull request still being `OPEN` with a null merge commit and main unmoved at `5859149`. The discrepancy was reported rather than assumed away, and the merge was confirmed only once `c92ab95` existed.

Ledger: zero findings. Five informational notes are raised. Note 89 records that the org is bound but the authorizing user is not: the identity URL's user segment is shape-checked against the `005` prefix and then discarded, never compared or returned, so any user in the expected org who can complete the grant binds the tenant and the control is the External Client App's own authorization policy rather than this code. That is correct while no route is exposed, but the future administrator workflow should decide explicitly whether it needs to record or constrain the authorizing user, because a reader may assume the identity check already does so. Note 90 records that the expected-org comparison runs before the HMAC signature is verified, so a `TENANT_OAUTH_BINDING` refusal can be produced by an unauthenticated forged response and is a mild org-ID oracle to a party who already controls the token endpoint; this is not exploitable, since the refusal persists nothing and reaching that point requires defeating TLS, but no future reader should treat `BINDING` as evidence that the response came from Salesforce. Note 91 records that the response `scope` is allowlisted but never validated, so a narrower grant than `api refresh_token` would persist and fail later at call time rather than at authorization time, while the critical half is self-enforcing because a missing refresh token fails outright; relatedly, `token_format` is validated in the ADR 0039 session module but only allowlisted here, which is harmless because this unit discards the access token but means the two modules now differ on the same field. Note 92 records that a failed consume does not delete an expired row, which lingers until the same tenant starts again, bounded at one row per tenant by the `UNIQUE` constraint and containing no secret, so neither a growth nor a disclosure risk, but the table is not self-cleaning. Note 93 records that the callback accepts exactly two keys, so the future HTTP route must normalize a real Salesforce error redirect, which also carries `error_description`, or every provider error will surface as `TENANT_OAUTH_INPUT` rather than `TENANT_OAUTH_PROVIDER`.

This advances note 34 through the authorization and callback protocol without closing it, because trusted administrator HTTP authentication and tenant provisioning, deployment secret wiring, privileged disconnect and rebind, the final `publishSalesforceDefinition` composition and the publish CLI all remain, exactly as ADR 0040 states and as confirmed against the diff.
