## 2026-10-19: PR #75 adds the trusted administrator OAuth route contract (ADR 0041); 0 findings, notes 89 to 93 all closed, notes 94 and 95 raised, PASS at `22844ad`

One commit `22844ad` on accepted main `9700b5a`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, wrapped the ADR 0040 authorization protocol in a Fastify HTTP adapter and deliberately did not mount it. Eleven files changed, 1,061 insertions and 25 deletions, comprising the new `service/src/tenant/oauth-routes.ts` at 349 lines, 401 lines of new route tests, targeted changes to `oauth-authorization.ts` answering five earlier verifier notes, an expanded database integration test, ADR 0041, and accuracy edits to ADR 0040, the architecture note, roadmap, threat model and service README. No migration was added. `.github` at tree `a55cb1b8decb2906562fad89d39bc71f7b329265`, `scripts` at `11acf84b4a464fecdfa89ba6e0c954c04d412704` and `salesforce` at `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice in `salesforce-verify.yml`, `service/src/app.ts` was unchanged, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace changed. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service 283 of 283, service lint, typecheck, `format:check`, `check:scaffold` and `git diff --check` all clean, and the integration suite against a disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 with `DATABASE_SSL=false` and `REQUIRE_DATABASE_TESTS=true` gave 4 of 4 passed with zero skips. The container was removed afterwards and no Salesforce capacity was consumed by the review itself.

One hundred and sixty-five adversarial assertions were driven at the HTTP boundary through `app.inject` against the compiled adapter and that real database, and all one hundred and sixty-five passed.

The central claim, that no request value can choose the tenant or expected org, holds on every channel that was attacked. With `x-tenant-id`, `x-expected-org`, `x-kusanya-tenant`, `x-namespace` and `x-authorization-origin` all forged to a second tenant and a hostile origin, the start still succeeded and the stored transaction row carried the session's tenant, the session's normalized org, the session's `https://login.salesforce.com` and the session's empty namespace. The forged headers were never read rather than being filtered. Every other channel is refused outright: a tenant query selector, an expected-org query selector, any query string at all, and a JSON body each return 400 with no transaction created, because the start requires an empty query and an undefined body, and a callback carrying an extra tenant selector is refused as well.

The property that makes this robust is that `decodeContext` treats the injected session verifier's output as untrusted. Ten hostile principals were driven through it: a thrown error whose message embedded the session value, `undefined`, a bare string, a proxy, an object with extra keys, an object missing `csrfToken`, a non-UUID tenant, a non-Salesforce authorization origin, a malformed org ID and a hostile `Evil__` namespace. All ten produced a fixed 401 with no transaction created and no session or secret material in the body. An accessor-backed principal was refused with a counting getter proving zero invocations. A buggy or compromised identity provider therefore cannot inject a cross-tenant binding or an open-redirect origin through this adapter, which is the strongest thing that can be said about a boundary whose identity provider does not yet exist.

Session and CSRF handling refused every shape tried. A missing cookie header, a missing session cookie, a session too short for the opaque pattern and a session containing an illegal character each returned 401. A missing `Origin`, a wrong `Origin` and an `Origin` with a trailing slash each returned 403, as did a missing, wrong or malformed CSRF token, which is compared in constant time against the value bound to the verified session so it cannot be replayed from another session. Duplicate administrator session cookies and a malformed cookie header returned 400. A `GET` on the start path is not routed at all. No transaction was created in any of these cases.

The browser binding pins correctly on every attribute. The cookie uses the `__Host-` name with `Secure`, `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=Lax`, `Path=/`, no `Domain` as the `__Host-` prefix requires, and a maximum age equal to the transaction lifetime. `SameSite=Lax` is the correct choice rather than an oversight, because the callback arrives as a top-level cross-site navigation from Salesforce and `Strict` would withhold the cookie entirely. The cookie value was recomputed independently and equals HMAC-SHA256 over the state under the versioned domain `kusanya:tenant-oauth-browser:v1` with a NUL separator, and it is domain-separated from the PKCE verifier's `kusanya:tenant-oauth-pkce:v1`, which was confirmed by deriving both from the same secret and state and observing they differ. The cookie does not reveal the state. Cross-browser completion fails closed in five shapes: no binding cookie, a wrong binding cookie, a wrong-shaped cookie, duplicate binding cookies, and another browser's binding cookie presented against this state. Each returned 400, persisted no connection, and still cleared the binding cookie, which the adapter expires on every callback outcome. Duplicate `state` and duplicate `code` query values were both refused, as were an unexpected extra key, `code` and `error` together, a missing state and an empty query.

Five notes raised against PR #73 are now closed, each on measured evidence. Note 90 is closed because the provider signature is authenticated before the expected-org comparison, proven both ways: a wrong-org response with an invalid signature now reports `TENANT_OAUTH_PROVIDER` where it previously reported `BINDING`, and a wrong-org response with a valid signature reports `TENANT_OAUTH_BINDING`, so that code now means a genuinely signed Salesforce identity for the wrong org. Note 91 is closed because the scope set is exact and order-independent across twelve cases, accepting `api refresh_token` and `refresh_token api` while refusing a single scope, a duplicated scope, an extra scope, a double space, leading and trailing spaces, a case variant, the empty string and a missing field, and because `token_format` accepts `jwt` while refusing `opaque` and `JWT`, making the two modules agree on a field they previously treated differently. Note 92 is closed because the consume statement now deletes the matching row in a common table expression and returns it only if still live, so against real PostgreSQL an expired transaction is still refused with `TENANT_OAUTH_STATE` and the row is deleted, while a non-matching digest deletes nothing, which prevents the self-cleaning from becoming a way to probe or clear other rows. Note 93 is closed because `error_description` is accepted only alongside a provider error, bounded at 1,024 characters and then discarded, with a marker description appearing in neither the response body nor any response header, the transaction consumed fail-closed and no connection created. Note 89 is closed at the decision boundary: the ADR now decides explicitly not to preselect one Salesforce user, with the sound reason that the trusted service administrator and the integration user need not be the same person, while the validated `005` user is returned as non-secret metadata, confirmed equal to the identity URL's user segment, confirmed absent from `tenant_connections` and confirmed absent from the HTTP response. The remaining obligation to persist an audit tying the trusted actor to that Salesforce user is stated in both the ADR and the README and stays tracked under note 34.

The brief asked for a judgment on whether an unmounted, injected-authentication contract is the correct incremental boundary, and it is, for reasons worth arguing rather than merely accepting. It gets the order right, because the credential-binding logic can be attacked against hostile principals before any identity provider exists, which is what this review did. Shipping the route together with a new session issuer would have meant verifying both at once with the route's correctness resting on an unreviewed issuer. The contract is narrow in the right place, since `authenticateAdministrator` receives only the opaque cookie rather than the Fastify request, so the adapter cannot be induced to trust anything else and widening it would require changing the type. Registration guards fail closed, verified for a mismatched redirect URI, an `http` origin, an origin with a path, a non-default port, embedded credentials, and a wrong-length or non-canonical state secret. The adapter is genuinely absent from `main.ts` and `app.ts`, and `docs/openapi.yaml` contains no reference to either path, so the health-only claim is accurate and no attack surface is added to the deployed service. The honest caveat is that the harder half remains outside this unit: issuing the opaque session, binding it to a tenant, and rotating or revoking it carry most of the residual risk, and the documents say so.

One probe defect is disclosed because it initially looked like a result. The injection helper spread per-case overrides after the merged headers object, so any case supplying headers replaced the whole set including the session cookie, which turned the forged-header test into a plain unauthenticated request returning 401. That looked like a refusal when it was a broken probe. After merging headers and stripping undefined values, the forged-header case correctly returns 303 and binds the session's tenant, which is the stronger result. The lesson is that in an injection helper the per-case overrides must be spread before the merged headers, or a dropped credential masquerades as a defence.

Documentation was checked claim by claim. ADR 0041, the architecture note, roadmap, threat model, service README and the ADR 0040 cross-references all match measured behaviour, including the threat model's revised rows for forged tenant identity and OAuth callback replay. Every document repeats that the adapter is unmounted and that concrete administrator identity, session issuance, tenant provisioning, audit persistence and secret wiring remain.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39302557773 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, the trusted budget reporting `{"allowed":true,"kind":"initial","reason":"No prior attempt consumed this head and UTC day."}`, and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`. That figure is identical to PRs #65, #67, #69, #71 and #73, and six consecutive units with unchanged figures, each against a fresh scratch org, is positive evidence that the Salesforce side is untouched rather than a stale reading. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only. Every forty-character hex string in the 941-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the `actions/setup-node` pin 65 times, the reviewed head 62 times, the harness pin 12 times, the `actions/checkout` pin 10 times, and `f3dc54e1` three times, the Azure runner image provisioner commit, which is not a repository reference. The single marker identified `39302557773-1`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`, and the gate took `POLICY_DECISION: full-apex` with `BUDGET_ALLOWED: true` and `APEX_RESULT: success`. Steps 7 and 8 rechecked the exact head and the retry budget immediately before authentication, and step 11 re-read the live head after testing, all passing.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39302557773-1__22844ad4ab5e__dee849cb7e62`, whose `ScratchOrgInfo` the Dev Hub independently showed created at 18:52:24 with `CreatedById` equal to the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null `ErrorCode`, a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 18:54:00 by that same user, and zero `ActiveScratchOrg` records. The log reported one owned org deleted and none already deleted, the 18:45 to 19:00 audit window held that one entry and nothing else, fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the dedicated CI user at 18:52:17 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, two seconds before the marker's `startedAt`, with no verifier authentication. Because this unit introduces browser session and CSRF material, the credential search was extended beyond the usual patterns to `state_secret`, `stateSecret`, `code_verifier`, `csrf`, `kusanya_admin_session`, `kusanya_oauth_binding`, `TENANT_OAUTH`, `TENANT_CREDENTIAL` and `DATABASE_URL`, with zero matches across all of them and zero `00D` org identifiers, alongside 21 masked redactions. The verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

`PASS: PR #75 merged as bc5eb79`, with squash tree `3117875` identical to the verified head tree, so the squash changed no content. The merge was confirmed by computing the intersection of red checks against the three required contexts, which returned no red checks at all, and by confirming the merge commit existed before anything was relayed.

Ledger: zero findings. Notes 89, 90, 91, 92 and 93 are closed as described above. Two new informational notes are raised. Note 94 records that the adapter itself never logs, confirmed by inspection and by capturing the service's own hardened logger, which produced zero bytes for a start and an error callback and leaked none of the authorization code, state, provider description, client secret, state secret, session cookie or binding cookie. That property comes from `service/src/app.ts`, which sets a request serializer emitting only the method, disables Fastify request logging and redacts the cookie header, and which this pull request does not change. Because `registerTenantOAuthRoutes` accepts any Fastify instance, the contrast was measured: mounting the same routes on a default-logger Fastify host does record the callback query string including the authorization code. The no-logging guarantee is therefore a property of the host application rather than of the adapter, and the unit that mounts these routes must preserve that hardening or move the equivalent inside the adapter. Note 95 records that the callback deliberately does not require the administrator session cookie and never consults the session verifier at all, which ADR 0041 documents with reasoning this review accepts, since the callback is a top-level cross-site redirect and a `SameSite=Strict` session cookie would not be sent. The narrow residual, not mentioned in the ADR, is that a logout or session revocation occurring between start and callback does not prevent the grant from completing inside the transaction lifetime of 60 to 900 seconds. Severity is very low, because completing only binds the org the authenticated administrator already chose and the `HttpOnly` binding cookie plus Salesforce's one-time state are still required, but the mounting unit should decide whether to check session liveness as well.

This advances note 34 through a reviewable HTTP adapter without closing it, because the concrete administrator identity and session issuer, tenant provisioning, audit persistence, deployment secret wiring, privileged disconnect and rebind, the final `publishSalesforceDefinition` composition and the publish CLI all remain, exactly as ADR 0041 states and as confirmed against the diff. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 94 and 95 remain informational. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-19: PR #77 adds revocable administrator sessions and immutable provisioning (ADR 0042); 0 findings, note 95 closed, notes 96 and 97 raised, PASS at `f34c10e`

One commit `f34c10e` on accepted main `323e1c9`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, supplied the concrete session issuer behind ADR 0041's injected contract. Twelve files changed, 1,173 insertions and 60 deletions, comprising the new `service/src/tenant/administrator-session.ts` at 391 lines, 328 lines of new unit tests, migration 0003 creating `tenant_administrator_bindings` and `administrator_sessions`, targeted changes to `oauth-routes.ts` answering verifier note 95, an expanded database integration test and route test, ADR 0042, and accuracy edits to ADR 0041, the architecture note, roadmap, threat model and service README. `.github` at tree `a55cb1b8decb2906562fad89d39bc71f7b329265`, `scripts` at `11acf84b4a464fecdfa89ba6e0c954c04d412704` and `salesforce` at `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace changed. The unmounted and health-only claims were verified rather than accepted, because `service/src/main.ts`, `service/src/app.ts` and `docs/openapi.yaml` are all unchanged. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 290 of 290, service lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` all clean, and the integration suite against a disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 with `DATABASE_SSL=false` and `REQUIRE_DATABASE_TESTS=true` gave 5 of 5 passed with zero skips. The container was removed afterwards and no Salesforce capacity was consumed by the review itself.

One hundred and thirty adversarial assertions were driven against the compiled modules and that real database, and all one hundred and thirty passed. The three concerns named in the PR #75 relay as the things this reviewer would probe hardest were each answered.

Session fixation is structurally impossible rather than merely defended. The issuer has arity two, taking an actor identifier and configuration, with no parameter through which a caller could propose a session value, and the token is thirty-two random bytes generated server side. An attacker-chosen forty-three character token does not authenticate before a login and, critically, still does not authenticate after a successful login for that same actor, which is the precise fixation scenario. Two issuances produce different tokens, and issuance requires an existing provisioned binding, so an unprovisioned actor is refused with the fixed `ADMINISTRATOR_SESSION_ABSENT`.

Revocation is immediate and database backed. Logout by session digest and operator revocation by actor each take effect on the very next authentication call, both are idempotent and report false on a second attempt, and the row is removed. Expiry is handled by a common table expression that deletes the matching expired row while returning no principal, which was confirmed to leave zero rows. There is no self-contained token and no cache, so every request resolves against live state across replicas. The table's own check constraint that expiry must exceed creation also refused a backdated expiry, which was retained as an assertion in its own right.

The tenant bound into a session cannot change. The returned frozen context is reconstructed from the immutable binding row on every call, so tenant identity never comes from the request or from the session. Re-provisioning the same actor with a different tenant, a different expected org, a different authorization origin or a different namespace each returned `ADMINISTRATOR_SESSION_CONFLICT` and left the stored row byte-identical, verified by comparing the serialized row before and after all four attempts, while an identical re-provision was idempotent and changed nothing. Exactly one active session exists per actor, and under a six-way concurrent race for one actor exactly one row survived and exactly one of the six tokens authenticated, with no caller observing a storage error.

Note 95 is closed, and more strongly than the note asked. The callback now requires the administrator session cookie and the browser binding is an HMAC over both the one-time state and the exact opaque session under the state secret, recomputed independently here and confirmed to differ from the session-free binding of PR #75. Six scenarios were measured with a counting transport. Logout between start and callback returns 401 with the transaction row surviving and zero fetches. An absent session cookie returns 401 with the state unconsumed. A session replaced by a later login returns 401. A new live session presented against the old flow returns 400 on binding mismatch. A different actor's valid live session returns 400. In every refusal the state was not consumed and Salesforce was never contacted, which is exactly the ordering the builder claimed. The live initiating session completes the grant with a 200, creating exactly one connection for the session's tenant and consuming the state, and the derived CSRF token cannot be used as a session. One consequence is worth stating rather than filing: because the binding now includes the session, a session replacement mid-flow makes an outstanding grant permanently uncompletable, which is the correct strict behaviour and which ADR 0042 states.

Durable storage holds only digests. PostgreSQL stores exactly the SHA-256 of the session as hex and nothing else, with the column set precisely `actor_id`, `created_at`, `expires_at` and `session_digest`. Raw rows contain no session token, no CSRF token and no session secret, and a guess derived from the stored digest does not authenticate. The CSRF token is a versioned HMAC over the session under a separate thirty-two byte deployment secret, recomputed here and confirmed domain-separated from both the OAuth browser binding and the PKCE verifier domains.

Hostile input was refused throughout. Accessor-backed provisioning input and configuration were both refused with counting getters proving zero invocations. Proxies, null prototypes, extra keys, a missing tenant, a non-UUID tenant, a malformed org, a non-Salesforce origin, a lookalike `login.salesforce.com.evil.com`, an origin carrying credentials, consecutive-underscore and over-length namespaces, an actor containing a traversal sequence, an over-long actor, an empty actor, `null` and an array were all refused with the fixed input code. Session values of forty-two or forty-four characters, or containing an illegal character, were refused. A lifetime below three hundred seconds, above twenty-eight thousand eight hundred seconds or non-integer was refused, as was a wrong-length, non-canonical or non-base64 session secret, and errors were confirmed secret-free. Migration 0003 applied idempotently, the ledger held exactly the three expected identifiers each with a sixty-four character checksum, a tampered checksum was detected and refused with `DATABASE_MIGRATION_FAILED`, and the foreign key cascade was confirmed by deleting a binding and observing that the actor's session stopped authenticating and its row was gone.

Two probe defects are disclosed, and the first was productive. A hostile-namespace case reused an already provisioned actor, so it returned a conflict rather than the input refusal expected, which initially read as a wrong refusal code. Investigating why produced notes 96 and 97. Separately a shared-tenant count asserted exactly two bindings when earlier sections had already provisioned five, and the migration ledger's primary key was guessed as `id` when it is `migration_id`. The lesson from the first is that when a refusal arrives with an unexpected code, the reason must be established rather than the assertion adjusted, because the unexpected code was reporting something real.

Documentation was checked against measured behaviour. ADR 0042, the ADR 0041 amendment, the architecture note, roadmap, threat model and service README all match. The ADR 0041 amendment correctly reverses its earlier reasoning about not requiring the session rather than leaving a contradiction in the record, and ADR 0042 states the constraint this change creates, that the future administrator cookie must be `SameSite=Lax` for the top-level Salesforce redirect to carry it. Note 94 is carried forward explicitly in ADR 0042, the roadmap, the threat model and the README.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39312991301 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, the trusted budget reporting `{"allowed":true,"kind":"initial","reason":"No prior attempt consumed this head and UTC day."}`, and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`. That figure is identical to PRs #65, #67, #69, #71, #73 and #75, and seven consecutive units with unchanged figures, each against a fresh scratch org, is positive evidence that the Salesforce side is untouched rather than a stale reading. The trusted harness checked out at `ref: 1d0edc1818e1eda0c42f752aca19eb3ec9a15894` into `trusted` sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, and the reviewed source at the exact head into `reviewed` sparse to `salesforce` as data only. Every forty-character hex string in the 941-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the `actions/setup-node` pin 65 times, the reviewed head 62 times, the harness pin 12 times, the `actions/checkout` pin 10 times, and `f3dc54e1` three times, the Azure runner image provisioner commit, which is not a repository reference. The single marker identified `39312991301-1`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`, and the gate took `POLICY_DECISION: full-apex` with `BUDGET_ALLOWED: true` and `APEX_RESULT: success`. Steps 7 and 8 rechecked the exact head and the retry budget immediately before authentication, and step 11 re-read the live head after testing, all passing.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39312991301-1__f34c10ee6a05__db7a214bb9e8`, whose `ScratchOrgInfo` the Dev Hub independently showed created at 19:58:02 with `CreatedById` equal to the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null `ErrorCode`, a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 19:59:43 by that same user, and zero `ActiveScratchOrg` records. The log reported one owned org deleted and none already deleted, the 19:50 to 20:05 audit window held that one entry and nothing else, fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, the dedicated CI user at 19:57:56 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, one second before the marker's `startedAt`, with no verifier authentication. Because this unit introduces administrator session material, the credential search was extended beyond the usual patterns to `session_secret`, `sessionSecret`, `session_digest`, `ADMINISTRATOR_SESSION`, `csrf`, `kusanya_admin_session`, `code_verifier`, `TENANT_CREDENTIAL` and `DATABASE_URL`, with zero matches across all of them and zero `00D` org identifiers, alongside 21 masked redactions. The verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

`PASS: PR #77 merged as b0011f5`, with squash tree `158b86d` identical to the verified head tree, so the squash changed no content. The merge was confirmed by computing the intersection of red checks against the three required contexts, which returned no red checks at all, and the merge commit was confirmed to exist before anything was relayed.

Ledger: zero findings. Note 95 is closed as described. Note 94 remains open, correctly, because `service/src/app.ts` is unchanged and the routes remain unmounted. Two new informational notes are raised, and they are two instances of one theme: the provisioning boundary admits a state that a later layer rejects, while immutability prevents correction. Note 96 records that the two namespace validators disagree. Provisioning validates through `createSalesforceNames`, which accepts a leading uppercase letter and internal single underscores, while the route's context decoder requires a lowercase alphanumeric prefix without underscores. Verified for `MyNs__`, `my_ns__` and `Evil__`, provisioning stores the binding, a session issues, authentication returns a context, and yet every route request for that actor returns 401 because the route is stricter. Re-provisioning with a corrected namespace returns `ADMINISTRATOR_SESSION_CONFLICT`, and there is no deprovision, rebind or delete operation, so the actor is permanently unusable without manual SQL. This is fail closed and carries no security impact, and the route's stricter rule is the one closer to Salesforce's actual namespace syntax; it is recorded because the symptom is an opaque 401 that gives no indication provisioning is at fault. Note 97 records that no cross-record invariant ties actors who share a tenant to the same expected org. Two actors were provisioned onto one tenant with different expected orgs and both were accepted, leaving one tenant carrying conflicting expected orgs across actors. Multiple actors per tenant is intended and correct; the gap is that they may disagree. The outcome remains fail closed, because the connection store's same-org guard refuses the second grant, but it surfaces late as an opaque `TENANT_OAUTH_CONNECTION` at completion rather than at provisioning time, and immutability again means neither binding can be corrected. The invariant wanted is that the tenant determines the org, which a simple unique constraint does not express.

This advances note 34 substantially without closing it, because the external identity verifier and its stable actor mapping, the approved provisioning interface, the audit tying the trusted actor to Salesforce's validated `005` user, deployment secret wiring, secure cookie, login and logout composition, privileged disconnect and rebind, the final `publishSalesforceDefinition` composition and the publish CLI all remain, exactly as ADR 0042 states and as confirmed against the diff. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 94, 96 and 97 remain informational. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-19: PR #79 enforces tenant-wide administrator provisioning invariants (ADR 0043); 1 finding raised and closed, notes 96 and 97 closed, HOLD at `86e6d8e` then PASS at `33ccf3e`

Two commits on accepted main `b0011f5`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, normalized administrator provisioning so that a tenant owns its Salesforce authorization tuple exactly once. Twelve files changed, 623 insertions and 59 deletions, comprising migration 0004 creating `tenant_administrator_tenants`, advisory-lock serialization and a shared namespace validator in `administrator-session.ts`, the route boundary adopting that validator, ADR 0043, and updates to ADR 0042, the architecture note, roadmap, threat model and service README. `.github` at tree `a55cb1b8decb2906562fad89d39bc71f7b329265`, `scripts` at `11acf84b4a464fecdfa89ba6e0c954c04d412704` and `salesforce` at `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace changed. `service/src/main.ts`, `service/src/app.ts` and `docs/openapi.yaml` were unchanged, so the unmounted and health-only claims were verified rather than accepted. Local evidence reproduced exactly at both heads: root 278 of 278, service unit 293 of 293, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 5 of 5 then 6 of 6 with zero skips. Containers were removed afterwards.

This unit was held on its first head and the finding was a real defect in the migration, so the sequence is recorded in full.

**Finding 1, raised at `86e6d8e`.** Migration 0004's backfill read `INSERT INTO tenant_administrator_tenants ... SELECT tenant_id, ... FROM tenant_administrator_bindings ON CONFLICT (tenant_id) DO UPDATE ...`. Because PR #77 deliberately permits several administrators to share one tenant, that select returns one row per actor, so a shared tenant supplies duplicate `tenant_id` values inside a single insert, which PostgreSQL refuses outright. The genuine legacy schema was built by applying migrations 0001 to 0003 from a build of the accepted main, three actors were seeded on one tenant with identical org, origin and namespace, and the new migrations were applied: the upgrade failed with the fixed `DATABASE_MIGRATION_FAILED`. Isolating the statement produced the mechanism exactly. The preceding consistency gate reported zero inconsistent tenants, correctly, because the rows did agree; the failure was `SQLSTATE 21000`, `ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE command cannot affect row a second time`, with PostgreSQL's own hint naming duplicate constrained values within one command as the cause. The one-line correction, `SELECT DISTINCT` with the redundant conflict clause removed, was proven against the same seeded database before it was proposed, succeeding with one tenant row.

The finding was fail closed in every respect, which was verified rather than assumed: the whole migration sequence runs in one transaction holding an advisory lock, so the failure rolled back completely, the legacy schema stayed intact, no partial tenant table remained, `0004` was not recorded as applied, and the legacy rows were untouched. It was nevertheless held rather than noted, for two reasons that are worth preserving as a rule. First, migrations are checksummed by `applyMigrations`, so while no persistent database has applied `0004` the statement can be edited freely, whereas once it has run anywhere persistent the correction requires an additional migration and the broken statement remains in history; fixing it before merge was therefore materially cheaper than fixing it after. Second, the migration is the unit's primary artifact and it could not complete for a state the system is designed to reach, while ADR 0043 claimed it backfilled consistent rows. The protected run on the held head was reported as unsafe to approve and was left unapproved; it consumed no scratch org.

The reason the defect survived the builder's own suite was also identified: the integration tests only ever called `applyMigrations` against a fresh database, twice in one place to prove idempotence, so the backfill was never exercised with a non-empty source table.

**Finding 1 closed at `33ccf3e`.** The corrected backfill uses `SELECT DISTINCT` with no conflict clause, which the consistency gate makes safe because tenant-wide disagreement is already rejected. The case that previously failed was re-run at two, three and five actors sharing one tenant, and each upgraded to exactly one tenant row with every actor binding preserved, tenant values preserved byte for byte, the three duplicated columns dropped from bindings leaving `actor_id`, `created_at` and `tenant_id`, the tenant foreign key present, all four migrations recorded with sixty-four character checksums, and reapplying the full plan a no-op. A mixed upgrade of one shared tenant holding three actors plus one solo tenant produced two tenant rows, four bindings, and each tenant retaining its own org. One hundred and eight assertions were driven at this head and all one hundred and eight passed.

A change of verification method on the second head must be disclosed, because the first review's method was load-bearing for the finding. Repeating the construction of the genuine prior schema, the local `main` branch was found to be stale at `1613b90` while the accepted main was `b0011f5`, so that rebuild was not what was intended. Every trust-boundary comparison in both reviews used `origin/main` explicitly and is unaffected, and the original finding stands because the first build was verified at the time to contain no migration 0004. Rather than re-derive the prior schema from a build that could no longer be vouched for, it was proven from the diff: `git diff origin/main` against the head shows the only changes to `migrations.ts` are the added 0004 block and the `applyMigrations` signature and loop, so migrations 0001, 0002 and 0003 are byte-identical to the accepted main and the builder's `through: '0003_administrator_sessions'` target executes exactly the three statements the prior plan executes. That conclusion was then checked structurally rather than assumed: three migration records, no tenant table, bindings still owning the org, origin and namespace columns, no tenant foreign key, and the prior schema still accepting a noncanonical `MyNs__` exactly as the earlier code did. The last of those matters, because it proves the fixture can reach the state the new preflight exists to catch; a fixture unable to reach the bad state would have been worthless.

The new optional migration target was reviewed as untrusted production code rather than as test scaffolding, since it is exported from a production module. The only production call site is `service/src/migrate.ts`, calling `applyMigrations(pool)` with no second argument, and the identifier appears nowhere else in `service/src`, so production always applies the complete plan. Unknown targets including `0005_nope`, the empty string, `DROP TABLE`, the truncated `0004` and `ADMINISTRATOR` were each refused with the fixed migration error and created no tables, and because the guard precedes `pool.connect()` a bad target never opens a connection. Non-string targets `null`, `0`, `1`, `{}`, `[]` and `true` were also refused, so the safety does not rest on the TypeScript union alone. The target can only ever select a prefix and never a gap, confirmed by applying through 0001, then through 0003, then the full plan in sequence; and a lower target applied afterwards is a harmless no-op that never reverts anything, which was checked explicitly because that is the shape that would be dangerous.

Fail-closed upgrade behaviour was verified on eight legacy states at the final head, each rolling back completely with the prior schema intact, no partial tenant table, `0004` unrecorded, legacy rows untouched and the fixed `DATABASE_MIGRATION_FAILED` leaking no database detail: tenant-wide org disagreement; disagreement on origin alone; disagreement on namespace alone; two tenants sharing one Salesforce org; legacy namespaces `MyNs__`, `my_ns__` and `Evil__`; and a shared tenant in which only one of two actors carries a bad namespace. That last case was added specifically for this head, because `SELECT DISTINCT` collapses rows and it had to be certain that a single offending actor among several still aborts rather than being deduplicated away. It aborts. A shared tenant with a valid `ksny__` upgrades and preserves it.

Note 96 is closed at three layers that were verified to agree exactly. `administratorNamespacePrefix` is now a single exported validator called by both provisioning and the route's context decoder, and migration 0004 repeats the same expression as a database check constraint. Twelve values were tested against all three layers with complete agreement: `MyNs__`, `my_ns__`, `Evil__`, `A__`, `a_b__`, `__`, `ksny` and an over-length prefix were refused by validator, provisioning and database alike, while the empty string, `a__`, `ksny__` and `abcdefghijklmno__` were accepted. The service README adds a read-only preflight query using that same expression, instructing an operator to stop the deployment, confirm the intended prefix with the tenant administrator, back up, and correct every binding for that tenant together under the audited change process, and stating explicitly that the prefix must not be guessed, lowercased or otherwise normalized automatically. Code that fails closed paired with a runbook that forbids silent repair is the correct combination for this hazard.

Note 97 is closed more tightly than the note asked. A tenant owns its authorization tuple in a table keyed by `tenant_id`, so one tenant cannot carry two orgs: a second actor proposing a different org, origin or namespace is refused with the fixed conflict code and no residue, while one agreeing on all tenant context is admitted and shares the single tenant row. The added unique constraint on the expected org additionally prevents a second tenant from claiming an org already owned, which the note did not request and which was confirmed to leave no orphan tenant row for the loser.

Provisioning serialization was proven rather than read. Advisory locks are always taken tenant first and actor second, forced by the actor lock's common table expression selecting from the tenant lock, so no lock-order inversion exists between callers. Six concurrent writers against one tenant with three competing org claims settled in seventeen milliseconds with exactly one tenant row, every successful writer agreeing with the winning org, every refusal carrying the fixed conflict code, bound actors exactly equal to the successful writers, and no actor bound to a non-existent tenant; four crossed concurrent provisionings did not deadlock. The eligibility expression is what makes a refused actor move residue free, and it works: moving an existing actor to a brand-new tenant and to an existing other tenant were both refused with the tenant and binding tables byte-identical before and after and, critically, with no orphan tenant row created for the target. The unique-violation mapping does not execute hostile accessors, reading the error code through a property descriptor and rejecting proxies, so an accessor-backed code returning the violation value maps to the storage failure with the getter never running while a genuine own property maps to the conflict code. Sessions now resolve through the tenant table, and with the foreign key dropped and the tenant row deleted a session is denied rather than returning a partial context.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39319802934 attempt 1 passed at the exact final head with no retry consumed, the trusted budget reporting an initial allowance, and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, identical to PRs #65, #67, #69, #71, #73, #75 and #77. Eight consecutive units with unchanged figures, each against a fresh scratch org, is positive evidence that the Salesforce side is untouched. Every forty-character hex string in the 941-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the `actions/setup-node` pin 65 times, the reviewed head 62, the harness pin 12, the `actions/checkout` pin 10, and `f3dc54e1` three times, the Azure runner image provisioner commit. The single marker identified `39319802934-1`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`, and the gate took `POLICY_DECISION: full-apex` with `BUDGET_ALLOWED: true` and `APEX_RESULT: success`. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, that same user at 20:42:15 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication. The credential search was extended for this unit's surface to `session_secret`, `session_digest`, `ADMINISTRATOR_SESSION`, `csrf`, `kusanya_admin_session`, `code_verifier`, `MigrationTarget`, `TENANT_CREDENTIAL` and `DATABASE_URL`, with zero matches across all of them and zero org identifiers, alongside twenty-one masked redactions.

The ADR 0030 supersede workflow fired twice during this unit and both firings were reviewed, because it runs on `pull_request_target`. On the corrected head it cancelled the stale zero-step run left waiting on the held head, reporting `Cancelled one superseded, zero-step waiting Salesforce run.`; on the rebased verification-log branch it correctly did nothing, reporting `No superseded, zero-step waiting Salesforce run exists.` In both cases it checked out the trusted default-branch sha rather than the untrusted head, sparse to `scripts` with `persist-credentials: false`, ran with least-privilege permissions of actions write, contents read and metadata read, created no scratch org, touched no Salesforce credential, and disclosed nothing. The Dev Hub independently confirmed that no scratch org record ever existed for the superseded run, so the workflow saved capacity rather than costing it. That matters because the day's allowance stood at six of six after this run.

The source pull request was therefore merged first and the log branch rebased afterwards, which cost nothing because a documentation-only change takes the exemption.

## 2026-10-21: PR #81 composes administrator login, CSRF bootstrap and logout (ADR 0044); 0 findings, note 94 closed in code, notes 98 and 99 raised, PASS at `5579b56`

One commit `5579b56` on accepted main `6dc9071`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, added the browser composition that ADR 0041 and ADR 0042 had deliberately left injected: unmounted administrator login, a CSRF bootstrap and a logout, together with a new runtime precondition that refuses to register either browser adapter on a host whose automatic request logging is not disabled. Twelve files changed, 883 insertions and 37 deletions, comprising the new `service/src/tenant/administrator-routes.ts`, the new `service/src/http-security.ts`, 340 lines of route tests, an expanded integration test, ADR 0044, and updates to ADR 0042, the architecture note, roadmap, threat model and service README. `.github` at tree `a55cb1b8decb2906562fad89d39bc71f7b329265`, `scripts` at `11acf84b4a464fecdfa89ba6e0c954c04d412704` and `salesforce` at `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace changed. Fastify remains pinned at exactly `5.12.3` with no caret, which matters because the new guard depends on that version's log-controller shape. `service/src/main.ts`, `service/src/app.ts` and `docs/openapi.yaml` were unchanged, so the unmounted and health-only claims were verified rather than accepted. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 302 of 302, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 6 of 6 with zero skips. One hundred and one independent assertions were driven against the compiled modules, that real database and real Fastify 5.12.3, and all one hundred and one passed.

A false alarm of the verifier's own making belongs at the top of this entry, because it was reported to the decision maker as it happened. Three successive integration runs failed, at worst five of six, and the failures were escalated before their cause was established. The cause was entirely local: Docker Desktop's daemon stopped between the container starting and the suite running, and the underlying error was `connect ECONNREFUSED`. After restarting the engine and recreating the container the suite passed six of six with zero skips, confirming the builder's claim exactly. The lesson recorded for future units is that a whole-suite database failure should be attributed to the engine before it is attributed to the change.

The cookie contract was asserted on the header Fastify actually emitted rather than on the constant that produces it. Login returns `204` with an empty body, `Cache-Control: no-store`, and a single `Set-Cookie` of the form `__Host-kusanya_admin_session=<forty-three character opaque value>; Path=/; Max-Age=3600; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax`, with no `Domain` attribute, which the `__Host-` prefix requires. No CSRF token appears in the login body or in any login response header. The cookie cleared by logout retains every one of those attributes with `Max-Age=0`. The `SameSite=Lax` choice was proven rather than reasoned about: a single flow of login, CSRF bootstrap, OAuth start and callback completed end to end with both the session and the browser-binding cookie present, which is exactly the top-level cross-site provider redirect that `SameSite=Strict` would break, and logging out mid-flow then blocked the callback with no connection persisted.

The CSRF disclosure boundary held on every axis attacked. A live same-origin session receives `{ "csrfToken": ... }` and nothing else, never in a cookie, a redirect or a URL. Absent, unknown, malformed and duplicate session cookies, a cross-origin request, a missing `Origin` header, a query string and a request body were each refused with no disclosure, and a `GET` on the CSRF path is not routed at all, so it cannot be reached by navigation. Isolation between principals also held: a second actor's live session receives only its own token and never the first actor's, a revoked session receives none and has its stale cookie cleared, and a CSRF token presented as a session cookie is refused.

Login cannot be talked into an identity. The injected verifier receives the request, but its output is narrowed to exactly one key. Nine hostile outputs were each refused, secret-free, with the cookie cleared: an extra key beyond the actor, a tenant instead of an actor, a proxy, a null prototype, a bare string, `undefined`, illegal actor characters, an unprovisioned actor, and a verifier throwing with the session secret embedded in its message. An accessor-backed output was refused with a counting getter proving zero invocations. Cross-origin, missing-origin and query-bearing logins were refused with the cookie cleared, and no session row was created by any of them.

Logout ordering and failure semantics were proven rather than inferred. Wrapping the store and observing the call sequence showed `authenticate` then `revoke` before the response, and the row was gone from real PostgreSQL afterwards, the cleared session could not authenticate again, and replaying the logout was refused. The four failure modes behave distinctly and correctly: a wrong CSRF token and a missing CSRF token each return `403` with no `Set-Cookie` at all, so the cookie is preserved and the row survives; a database failure during revocation returns `503` with the cookie preserved and the row surviving; and a revocation that reports false, meaning the session was already gone, returns `401` and clears the stale cookie.

Note 94 is closed in code, which is the outcome the PR #75 review asked for in preference to documentation. Both adapters call `requireHardenedRequestLogging` before registering, and five unsafe host shapes were refused with the fixed `HTTP_LOGGING_CONFIGURATION`: a default `logger: true` host, a default logger with a stream, `logger: false`, a log controller with `disableRequestLogging` false, and a bare Fastify instance with no options. In every case no route existed afterwards, confirmed by a request returning 404, so there is nothing to call rather than merely something that refuses.

The framework coupling fails closed, which was the principal concern about this design. Fastify 5.12.3 exposes exactly one `fastify.logController` symbol, and eight simulated shape changes were each refused: no such symbol at all, which is what a rename or removal would produce; two such symbols; a null controller; a primitive controller; a proxied controller; `disableRequestLogging` truthy but not exactly `true`; the string `"true"`; and the value inherited from a prototype rather than owned. An accessor-backed `disableRequestLogging` was refused with its getter never running, and a proxied application instance was refused. The guard validates shape rather than identity, so a minimal plain object with the correct own-value shape is accepted; that is sound here because mounting is a deployment decision made in trusted code rather than untrusted input, but it means the guard proves the host declares request logging disabled rather than proving it is a genuine Fastify instance.

One measurement overturned the verifier's own earlier framing, in the code's favour, and the correction belongs in the record. The PR #75 entry implied that the request serializer emitting only the method and the cookie redaction in `createApp` were load-bearing for the callback-query leak. They are not, for this vector. A host that satisfies the new guard but keeps default serializers and no redaction emitted zero bytes for the callback, and zero bytes even on an unhandled 500, while a default-logger host leaked 2,298 bytes containing the authorization code on the same two paths. Disabling automatic request logging is therefore sufficient to close the whole leak class in Fastify 5.12.3, the single condition the guard enforces is the correct one, and the serializer and redaction in `createApp` are defence in depth for other log lines rather than the barrier for this one.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39405275395 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, identical to PRs #65, #67, #69, #71, #73, #75, #77 and #79, a ninth consecutive unchanged reading. Ledger: zero findings. **Note 94 is closed in code.** Two new informational notes are raised. Note 98 records that `administrator-routes.ts` re-implements the trusted-context validation rather than reusing what note 96's fix centralized: its namespace check inlines the canonical expression instead of importing the shared validator that `oauth-routes.ts` imports, and its org check accepts eighteen characters only where the OAuth route accepts fifteen or eighteen. No input can reach that divergence today, because both decode the session store's output and the store always returns a normalized eighteen-character org, but it reintroduces precisely the duplicated-validator pattern that produced note 96, and the next edit to one copy will not reach the other; a shared context decoder would remove the class. Note 99 records that login requires an absent request body, which is what makes its empty-request check meaningful but which constrains provider choice in a way no document states: a form-post identity binding, such as the SAML HTTP POST binding or an OIDC `form_post` response mode, cannot deliver its assertion to this route, leaving only header-, cookie- or redirect-query-based verification. ADR 0044 records the empty query and body requirement as a decision but not this consequence, and its own revisit trigger is provider selection, so the constraint should be stated before a provider is chosen rather than discovered then.

## 2026-10-21: PR #83 adds the atomic administrator-to-Salesforce authorization audit (ADR 0045); 0 findings, notes 98 and 99 closed, notes 100 and 101 raised, PASS at `2394340`

One commit `2394340` on accepted main `e282919`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, discharged the audit obligation that ADRs 0042 and 0044 had recorded and left open. Twenty-one files changed, 725 insertions and 221 deletions, the deletions being largely the duplicated validators that note 98 asked to be removed. Migration `0005_salesforce_authorization_audits` adds an append-only audit table and a composite unique constraint on the administrator binding; `TenantConnectionStore.saveAuthorization` commits the encrypted connection and the audit event in one PostgreSQL transaction; `005` user identifiers now receive the same checked normalization as org identifiers; and a single exported `decodeTrustedAdministratorContext` replaces the per-adapter copies. `.github` at tree `a55cb1b8decb2906562fad89d39bc71f7b329265`, `scripts` at `11acf84b4a464fecdfa89ba6e0c954c04d412704` and `salesforce` at `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace changed. `service/src/main.ts`, `service/src/app.ts` and `docs/openapi.yaml` were unchanged, so the unmounted and health-only claims were verified rather than accepted. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 304 of 304, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 6 of 6 with zero skips. The integration test count is unchanged from the previous unit because the new audit assertions were added inside the existing session test rather than as a new case, and they were confirmed present and executing. One hundred and thirteen independent assertions were driven across two probes.

Atomicity is the central claim and it was proven twice over. The audit insert selects the exact actor-to-tenant binding, so a mismatch affects no row and the preceding credential upsert is rolled back. Five mismatch shapes were each refused with the credential version not advanced, the stored refresh token unchanged, the stored ciphertext byte-identical and the audit history unchanged: an actor with no binding at all, an actor bound to another tenant, an org the tenant was not provisioned for, an authorization origin disagreeing with the provisioned tenant, and a namespace disagreeing with the provisioned tenant. That exercises the refusal path, where no row is affected and the method fails on its own row-count check. The harder path was then forced deliberately, a thrown database error on the audit insert after the credential upsert had already executed, using a dedicated pool and a client wrapper that forwards every argument and restores `query` on release, which is the shape the PR #69 lesson requires. The sequence was observed directly rather than inferred: `BEGIN` ran, the credential upsert ran, `ROLLBACK` was issued, `COMMIT` was not, the credential version did not advance, the stored token did not change, the audit history did not change, and the new refresh token never reached the database in any form. The shared pool remained healthy afterwards, confirming the wrapper did not repeat the PR #69 pooling defect.

The strongest construction in this unit is that cross-tenant resistance is enforced by the schema rather than by application comparison. The audit's composite foreign key on actor and tenant references the immutable administrator binding, so an actor bound to another tenant cannot be recorded against this one even if the surrounding logic were wrong, and the join inside the insert makes the same guarantee a second time. That is the right instinct for an invariant of this importance.

Principal provenance holds at the HTTP boundary. With `x-actor-id`, `x-tenant-id`, `x-salesforce-user-id` and `x-expected-org` all forged to a second tenant and a foreign user, the callback still completed and the resulting audit event recorded the session's actor, the session's tenant, the session's org and the cryptographically signed `005` user rather than any forged value, with exactly one event appended. A tampered provider signature was refused and appended no event at all, which is the correct ordering: the Salesforce user identity can only originate from a response whose signature verified.

Reauthorization appends rather than rewrites. A second grant produced two events; the first remained byte-identical afterwards and still named the original `005` user, the second named the new one, the credential versions read one then two, and the identity-generated event identifiers strictly increased, while the connection held the second token at version two.

The audit table holds nothing secret. Neither refresh token appeared in the raw rows, nor an administrator session, a CSRF token, the session secret or vault key material, and the column set is exactly the event identifier, tenant, actor, org, user, credential version and timestamp, with no column name capable of plausibly holding a token, code, state or nonce.

Migration 0005 was exercised on both paths the brief named. A fresh install applies all five migrations and re-applying is a no-op. Stopping at `0004` reproduces the prior schema with no audit table, and that prior schema was then populated with a provisioned tenant and two actors sharing it before upgrading through 0005. Both pre-existing bindings survived, the audit table was created empty, the new composite unique constraint was present, and a grant on the upgraded database wrote exactly one event. The two-actor case was chosen deliberately, because migration 0005 adds a unique constraint over actor and tenant and PR #79 established that shared-tenant upgrades are precisely where this class of migration fails.

Note 98 is closed. The shared decoder is exported once and both browser adapters have lost their private copies, confirmed by searching each file for the previous patterns. Through that decoder a fifteen-character org is accepted and normalized to eighteen; an extra key, a missing CSRF token, `MyNs__`, `my_ns__`, a non-Salesforce origin, a non-UUID tenant and a malformed actor are refused; a proxy is refused; an accessor-backed context is refused with its getter never running; and the decoded context is frozen. The `005` normalization now matches the org normalization exactly, including that an org identifier is refused where a user identifier is required.

Note 99 is closed at the documentation boundary, which is what the PR #81 review asked for. ADR 0044 now states that the empty login query and body is a security boundary rather than a provider-neutral transport, names the SAML HTTP POST binding and the OIDC `form_post` response mode as excluded from direct use with the route, and requires either another injected mechanism or a separately reviewed route-contract change. Its revisit trigger now calls out a provider requiring a form-post assertion, so the constraint is recorded before a provider is chosen rather than discovered at that point.

Three of the one hundred and thirteen assertions did not pass on the first run and all three were defects in the verifier's own probe, which must be recorded because two of them repeat a lesson already written down. The first two built a wrong-checksum identifier by applying `toUpperCase` to `00D000000000001`, which is entirely digits after the prefix and therefore unchanged, so the supposed variant was the original value and was correctly accepted while the assertion proved nothing. This is the same vacuous construction recorded in the PR #73 entry. Rebuilt correctly, by corrupting the three-character checksum suffix and by swapping in the checksum of a genuinely mixed-case variant, every case is refused, for orgs, for `005` users and through the shared decoder: `00Dbm00000yeiz3` and `00DBM00000YEIZ3` normalize to `00Dbm00000yeiz3EAA` and `00DBM00000YEIZ32AP`, and presenting either checksum against the other base is refused. The third was a mis-specified characterization of the append-only question, which happened to select a cross-tenant actor and so was blocked by the composite foreign key, making the result look stronger than the storage warrants; it was re-tested properly and is reported as note 101. The recurring probe traps have since been consolidated into a single reference so that they are consulted before the next probe rather than rediscovered.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39596114122 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, identical to PRs #65, #67, #69, #71, #73, #75, #77, #79 and #81, a tenth consecutive unchanged reading against a fresh scratch org each time. Every forty-character hex string in the 941-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the `actions/setup-node` pin 65 times, the reviewed head 62, the harness pin 12, the `actions/checkout` pin 10 and the Azure runner image provisioner commit `267fdb4e` three times. That last figure also settles the anomaly recorded in the PR #81 entry, where an unfamiliar sixth hex string appeared and was attributed to GitHub rolling its runner image between job starts across two days: in this run all three jobs report `267fdb4e` and the previously familiar `f3dc54e1` has disappeared entirely, which is what a completed image rollover looks like. The single marker identified `39596114122-1`, the full exact head, outcome `passed` and `retryable: false`, and the gate took `POLICY_DECISION: full-apex` with `BUDGET_ALLOWED: true` and `APEX_RESULT: success`, while steps 7 and 8 rechecked the exact head and the retry budget immediately before authentication and step 11 re-read the live head after testing.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39596114122-1__2394340c15bd__60e17b574275`, which the Dev Hub independently showed created at 12:22:56 by the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null error code, with a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 12:25:55 and zero active scratch org records. The log reported one owned org deleted and none already deleted, the 12:15 to 12:30 audit window held that one entry and nothing else, fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, that same user at 12:22:51 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication. Because this unit records Salesforce principals, the credential search was extended to `authorization_audit`, `salesforce_user_id`, any `005` identifier and any `00D` identifier alongside `session_secret`, `csrf`, `kusanya_admin_session`, `code_verifier`, `TENANT_CREDENTIAL` and `DATABASE_URL`, with zero matches across every one and twenty-one masked redactions. The verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

`PASS: PR #83 merged as 4bdaa5f`, with squash tree `299be54` identical to the verified head tree. The strict-protection ordering hazard first recorded for PR #79 applied again, because the outstanding verification-log pull request shared the same base, and was avoided the same way: the source pull request merged first and the log branch was rebased afterwards, which costs nothing because a documentation-only change takes the exemption.

Ledger: zero findings. Notes 98 and 99 are closed as described. Two new informational notes are raised. Note 100 records that `TenantConnectionStore.save`, the audit-free write, remains a public method. No production path reaches it, since the callback's dependency is typed to expose only `saveAuthorization`, and the only remaining callers are test sites, but the asymmetry it leaves was proven: after `save` the database holds one connection and zero audit events, while inserting an audit for a tenant with no connection is blocked with `SQLSTATE 23503`. The audit therefore cannot exist without a connection, but a connection can exist without an audit, which makes the data-model statement that the shared transaction means neither may exist without the other half true as written, and leaves a bypass for any future caller selecting the wrong method. Narrowing `save` to test-only visibility, or requiring an audit at the schema level, would close both the inaccuracy and the bypass. Note 101 records that append-only is a property of the write path rather than of the schema. Measured against the real table, `UPDATE` of the `005` user, of the credential version and of the timestamp are all permitted, and `DELETE` of an event is permitted; only rewriting the actor to a cross-tenant value is blocked, and that is the composite foreign key acting incidentally rather than any immutability rule. The code never updates or deletes, so the behaviour matches ADR 0045's description of the write path and nothing here is reachable from a request; it matters only if the audit is ever expected to be tamper-evident against privileged database access, in which case revoking `UPDATE` and `DELETE` from the service role, or a trigger, would be the mechanism.

This advances note 34 substantially without closing it, because the external identity provider and its stable actor mapping, the approved provisioning interface, deployment secret wiring, privileged disconnect and rebind, the final `publishSalesforceDefinition` composition and the publish CLI all remain. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 100 and 101 remain informational. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion claim is made.
