Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 313 of 313, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 6 of 6 with zero skips, the new bound-session assertions being confirmed present and executing inside the existing OAuth test. Seventy independent assertions were driven against the compiled modules, that real database and a complete fake Salesforce, and all seventy passed.

The PR #83 relay named four properties this unit would be judged on, and all four hold.

The attempt-scoped session is the sole credential path. The request decoder admits exactly one key, so the attack was to smuggle credentials in alongside it: an access token, an instance origin, an org selector, a tenant selector and a `getAccessToken` callback were each refused, as were a proxy request, a null request, a missing and a malformed form version identifier. In every refusal there were zero store locks, zero refresh-token decryptions and zero network requests. Hostile contexts and configurations behaved the same way, including an accessor-backed context refused with its getter never running, and the instance origin and access token used for publication come only from the session rather than from any caller-supplied value.

Disagreement is refused before anything irreversible happens, which is the strongest result in the unit. Using the real `TenantConnectionStore` against PostgreSQL, with a wrapper counting refresh-token decryptions, a mismatched expected org, a mismatched authorization origin, a mismatched namespace and an empty namespace against a namespaced connection each produced `TENANT_PUBLICATION_BINDING` with the row lock taken once, the refresh token decrypted zero times and zero network requests issued. The comparison therefore happens inside the lock, against the stored row, and strictly before the credential is decrypted or any OAuth or Salesforce I/O begins. An unconnected tenant yields `TENANT_PUBLICATION_NOT_CONNECTED`, also with zero requests. The exactly matching binding then publishes successfully with the refresh token decrypted exactly once and exactly one OAuth token request.

Partial publication is explicit rather than implied. Two lost commit responses produce a refusal carrying the distinct `PUBLICATION_PUBLISHER_PARTIAL` code after exactly two commit attempts, with no `DELETE` request issued at all, a non-zero request count, and neither a publication digest nor an `alreadyCommitted` flag claimed. One lost response followed by a confirmation still succeeds, so the byte-identical retry was preserved rather than traded away for the new outcome. ADR 0046 states plainly that private unlinked artifacts may exist, that reconciliation is required, that the operation never claims success, and that a later job, retry or retention boundary must reconcile partial attempts, with a durable attempt table and abandoned-artifact retention named in its revisit trigger. That is a documented failure policy with a distinct error code for a boundary that cannot be made atomic, which is what the previous relay asked for.

The ADR 0036 and 0037 posture cannot be widened by this unit. Structurally there is nothing to widen, because no Salesforce metadata, permission set, sharing or field file appears in the diff. Behaviourally every request of a successful publication was collected and none matches `PermissionSet`, `FieldPermissions`, `ObjectPermissions`, `__Share`, `SetupEntityAccess`, `Profile`, `sharingRules`, `UserRole` or `PermissionSetAssignment`; every write targets only the publication endpoints, `composite/sobjects` and the token endpoint, and no `DELETE` is issued.

The shared deadline is real rather than nominal. Refresh time is charged against the publication budget instead of being added to it: with a 600 millisecond overall budget and a refresh taking 900 milliseconds the operation refused and the entire call was bounded at 612 milliseconds, with no publication request following the exhausted budget. A refresh that hangs while carrying its own twenty second timeout was bounded by the overall budget at 420 milliseconds, so the inner timeout cannot outlive the outer one, and a healthy refresh still leaves budget to publish.

Two defects in the verifier's own probe are disclosed, because together they caused every assertion in two sections to fail, including the happy path, and they looked exactly like a defect in the unit. The store wrapper copied every own descriptor from the frozen `TenantConnection` and then redefined `getRefreshToken`; that property is non-configurable, so redefining it throws `TypeError: Cannot redefine property` inside the caller's lock and surfaced as a generic session failure. Copying every key except the target and then defining it works. Separately, two diagnostics constructed a fresh credential vault while the stored row had been sealed by a different vault instance, so decryption failed and produced `TENANT_SALESFORCE_SESSION_REFRESH`, which reads like a protocol rejection but was a key mismatch. The method by which both were found is worth recording: rather than adjusting the assertions, the real store was isolated from the wrapper, four response variants were tried, and the probe was then instrumented to report whether `fetch` had been reached at all. The observation `fetches=0` localised the failure to the path before the HTTP request, which is what exposed both causes. Both have been added to the standing reference of probe traps.

Notes 100 and 101 are closed, and the new wording was checked against what was measured in PR #83 rather than accepted. Note 101 is closed because ADR 0045's heading changed from "Append one immutable event" to "Append one event" and its text now scopes append-only to the Kusanya write path, states that the schema does not revoke `UPDATE` or `DELETE` from a privileged database role, and says explicitly that this is not a tamper-evidence claim against database administration. That matches the measurement in PR #83 exactly, where updating the `005` user, the credential version and the timestamp, and deleting an event, were all permitted. Note 100 is closed because both ADR 0045 and the data model now state that the audited OAuth path commits the connection and the event together so that path cannot commit either alone, that the audit foreign key prevents an audit without its connection, and that the lower-level connection store can still create a connection without an audit for isolated tests and internal storage work, so the reverse is not a schema invariant. That is precisely the asymmetry proven in PR #83, including the direction that had previously been overstated, and the ADR keeps open the option of narrowing that method's visibility once test setup uses a dedicated fixture boundary.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39614930239 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, identical to every unit since PR #65 and an eleventh consecutive unchanged reading, which carries more weight for this unit than most because a publication composition that touched Apex, permissions or sharing would have moved that figure. The run spanned two days, queued on 21 October and approved on 22 October, so the trusted rechecks mattered: steps 7 and 8 re-verified the exact head and the retry budget immediately before authentication and both passed, and step 11 re-read the live head after testing. Every forty-character hex string in the 941-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the reviewed head 62 times, the `actions/setup-node` pin 38, the harness pin 12, the `actions/checkout` pin 10 and the Azure runner image provisioner commit `267fdb4e` three times. Unlike the comparable two-day run recorded for PR #81, all three jobs reported the same provisioner commit, so the count stayed at five, which is the expected shape now that the image rollover has completed.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39614930239-1__07096739646d__24ffd463ff48`, which the Dev Hub independently showed created at 05:19:51 by the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null error code, with a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 05:20:55 and zero active scratch org records. The log reported one owned org deleted and none already deleted, the 05:12 to 05:27 audit window held that one entry and nothing else, fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, that same user at 05:19:44 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication. Because this unit publishes artifacts and handles tenant credentials, the credential search was extended to `PUBLICATION_PUBLISHER`, `TENANT_PUBLICATION`, `VersionData`, any `005` identifier and any `00D` identifier alongside `session_secret`, `csrf`, `kusanya_admin_session`, `code_verifier`, `TENANT_CREDENTIAL` and `DATABASE_URL`, with zero matches across every one and twenty-one masked redactions. The verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

A correction to this log's own earlier capacity reporting belongs here. While checking headroom for this run it was confirmed, rather than assumed, that the Dev Hub counts its daily scratch-org allowance against the org's timezone, `America/Los_Angeles`, not UTC. At 2026-10-22 05:25 UTC the three orgs counted as the current day were created at 11:33 and 12:22 UTC on 21 October and 05:19 UTC on 22 October, which are 04:33, 05:22 and 22:19 Pacific on 21 October. The allowance window therefore ends at midnight Pacific, 07:00 UTC under daylight time, and earlier entries that described this allowance as resetting at UTC midnight named the wrong mechanism. The error was in the unhelpful direction, since late in a UTC day the allowance has not yet reset and there is less headroom than a UTC reading implies. The harness's own retry budget is a genuinely UTC-day clock, because `decideInfrastructureRetry` compares each attempt's `startedAt` date against the current UTC day, so the two must not be conflated.

`PASS: PR #85 merged as 17b51a6`, with squash tree `0703eec` identical to the verified head tree. It was the only open pull request, so the strict-protection ordering hazard recorded for PRs #79 and #83 did not apply, and the merge was confirmed clean by computing the intersection of red checks against the three required contexts, which returned none.

Ledger: zero findings. Notes 100 and 101 are closed as described. One new informational note is raised. Note 102 records that every refusal from `publishTenantSalesforceDefinition` reports `requestCount: 0`, including a refusal at the session stage that had already called the OAuth token endpoint once; a binding refusal correctly reports zero with zero requests, so the field is accurate there. The defensible reading is that the count covers Salesforce REST requests against governor limits and an OAuth token exchange is not one of those, in which case the name is doing double duty; the other is that a caller using the field for budget or rate accounting undercounts by one on every session-stage refusal. Neither is a defect, but the field should either include the token request or the ADR should state what it counts, because at present the same field means Salesforce API requests on the publisher path and nothing-happened on a session refusal that did make a request.

This advances note 34 substantially, discharging the publication composition strand, without closing it, because no publish route or CLI, no external identity provider, no approved operator provisioning interface, no deployment OAuth secret wiring and no privileged disconnect or rebind is added. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86 and 102 remain informational. Partial-publication reconciliation and abandoned-artifact retention are declared future work in ADR 0046 rather than silent gaps. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-22: PR #87 adds the durable administrator publication route (ADR 0047); 0 findings, note 102 closed, note 103 raised against this log's own earlier claim, PASS at `3798216`

One commit `3798216` on accepted main `99627bf`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, added the unmounted `POST /api/admin/salesforce/publications` adapter and the durable attempt ledger behind it. Sixteen files changed, 1,214 insertions and 15 deletions, comprising migration `0006_publication_attempts`, a new `publication-attempt-store.ts`, a new `publication-routes.ts`, a partial-recording seam in `salesforce-publisher.ts`, ADR 0047, and corrections to ADR 0006, `docs/ci.md`, the architecture note, data model, roadmap and service README. `.github` at tree `a55cb1b8decb2906562fad89d39bc71f7b329265`, `scripts` at `11acf84b4a464fecdfa89ba6e0c954c04d412704` and `salesforce` at `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission, namespace or sharing file changed. `service/src/main.ts`, `service/src/app.ts` and `docs/openapi.yaml` were unchanged, and all three were read to confirm that none registers or describes the route, so the unmounted claim was verified rather than asserted. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 322 of 322, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 7 of 7 with zero skips. One hundred and two independent assertions were driven against the compiled modules, that real database and a complete fake Salesforce, and all one hundred and two passed.

The PR #85 relay named four properties this unit would be judged on, and all four hold.

The route cannot be reached without the session and CSRF boundary established in ADR 0044. Sixteen attacks were each refused with no attempt row created: a wrong origin, a missing origin, a query string, no body, an empty body object, a tenant selector in the body, an org selector, a supplied attempt identifier, a malformed form version, an array body, a missing session cookie, a malformed session, duplicate session cookies, a missing CSRF token, a wrong CSRF token and another live session's CSRF token. `GET` is not routed and a revoked session is refused. The route also calls `requireHardenedRequestLogging`, so the note 94 guard applies here too: registration on an unhardened host is refused with `HTTP_LOGGING_CONFIGURATION` and no route survives on the refused instance.

An uncertain commit is surfaced distinctly rather than flattened into a generic error. A lost commit response returns HTTP 409 with `status: "partial"`, and the PostgreSQL row is `partial` carrying the exact package digest, both uploaded ContentVersion identifiers, an explicit failure code and a completion timestamp, while the response claims no success and carries no digest. The commit was attempted exactly twice with no further retry and no artifact deletion. A clean publisher failure instead records `failed` with a failure code and no digest, so the two conditions are genuinely separate durable states.

The uncertainty is recorded durably at the moment it arises, which was the property this reviewer most wanted and which is implemented well. The `started` row is inserted before the publisher runs, and that ordering was proven from inside the flow rather than by reading the code: the fake Salesforce's token handler queried the attempt table at the instant of the OAuth exchange and found exactly one row with outcome `started`, no terminal fields, and the session's tenant and actor. Every failure path then preserves that row. A throwing publisher returns 503 `unresolved` and leaves `started`. A failed partial-ledger write returns 409 `unresolved`, leaves `started` with no digest or version identifiers, asks the ledger exactly once, and does not retry the commit. A successful publication whose terminal ledger write fails returns 503 `unresolved` and leaves `started` rather than `succeeded`, so a publish that cannot be recorded is never reported as published.

The route cannot publish for a tenant its session is not bound to, tested with a second genuinely provisioned tenant rather than only a forged header. With tenant, actor, expected-org and attempt-identifier headers all forged to that second tenant, the request succeeded and the attempt recorded the session's tenant and actor, the provisioned org, and a server-generated attempt identifier rather than the supplied one. The second tenant's own session then bound only to its own tenant, and at the store boundary starting an attempt for a tenant the actor is not bound to is refused outright.

The state machine is enforced by the schema rather than only by code, which is the part of this unit worth singling out. Migration 0006's terminal-shape check constraint was attacked with raw SQL rather than through the application, and it refuses `succeeded` carrying ContentVersion identifiers, a `partial` missing a version identifier, `failed` carrying a digest, `started` with a completion timestamp, and an unrecognised outcome value, while accepting a coherent `partial`. A `partial` row therefore cannot exist without the digest and both version identifiers, which is exactly what any future reconciliation will need. Terminal transitions are same-tenant and one-way at the database level: completing with another tenant's identifier is refused and leaves the row untouched, completing with the correct tenant succeeds, replaying the transition afterwards is refused with the first terminal state preserved, and completing a non-existent attempt is refused. The composite foreign key on actor and tenant to the administrator binding makes a cross-tenant attempt unrepresentable rather than merely rejected, continuing the pattern established in PR #83.

The attempt table holds nothing it should not. No refresh token, access token, administrator session, CSRF token or session secret appears in the rows, and neither does form definition content nor a question label from the compiled form. The column set is exactly the attempt identifier, tenant, actor, org, form version, outcome, digest, the two version identifiers, failure code, request count and the two timestamps, and no column name could plausibly hold a token, warning, definition, body or provider response.

Note 102 is closed, and the closure was checked against the implementation rather than the ADR. ADR 0047 states that the counts cover definition, Describe, Files and commit REST requests after the attempt-scoped session is obtained and intentionally exclude the single OAuth token exchange. Measured on a full publication, the fake Salesforce received eight requests of which one was the token exchange, and both the operator field and the durable column reported seven. Seven equals eight minus the token exchange, so the documented semantics are the implemented semantics in both places.

Note 103 is raised against this log's own earlier reasoning, and the sequence belongs in the record because the error was the verifier's. The PR #85 entry corrected an earlier claim that the Dev Hub daily allowance resets at UTC midnight, establishing instead that the day boundary follows the org's `America/Los_Angeles` timezone. That correction stands in its essential part and is well evidenced from date grouping: at 05:25 UTC on 22 October, `CreatedDate = TODAY` returned three rows including one created at 05:19 UTC that day, while at 12:07 UTC it returned only an org created at 11:34 UTC; since 05:19 UTC is 22:19 Pacific on 21 October and 11:34 UTC is 04:34 Pacific on 22 October, the boundary sits at 07:00 UTC, and it is certainly not UTC midnight. However, the PR #87 review initially went further and cited the live limit counter reading six of six remaining at 09:32 UTC as corroboration that the allocation itself had reset. That corroboration was invalid and was publicly withdrawn on the pull request before it merged. Measured afterwards at 12:07 UTC, with exactly one org created in that Pacific day and subsequently deleted, `DailyScratchOrgs.remaining` still read six of six, so the figure does not track consumption and could not have shown a reset. Issue #5 note 16, from 11 September and already cited in ADR 0006, had recorded the same anomaly, which is precisely why the ADR previously described the reset time as unconfirmed; this reviewer pushed it to confirmed and then cited the one counter the project had already found unreliable for the purpose. Note 103 therefore asks that the reset of the allocation be described as inferred rather than observed while the day-boundary finding is kept as evidenced, and records that `scripts/scratch-lifecycle.mjs` reads that figure for the daily arm of its preflight, so that arm cannot block an exhausted day and the active arm is the one doing the real work. The harness is byte-identical to main in this change, so nothing here introduced that behaviour, and the real cap appears to be enforced only at creation time, which is consistent with no run having failed on it since note 16. The standing lesson recorded for future units is to search the ADRs and `docs/ci.md` for a prior contrary observation before asserting a fact about org limits, because a correction built on an unreliable source carries more confidence than the error it replaces.

One probe defect is disclosed. A section failed with an authentication refusal on a session that had been valid moments earlier, because an earlier section issued a fresh session for the same actor in order to test revocation, and issuing a session replaces that actor's prior one. That is the single-session-per-actor property verified in PR #77, tripping the verifier's own probe. Re-issuing before the section fixed it, and an assertion that the replacement occurred was kept, so the incident now tests the property rather than merely stumbling over it.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39731354939 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, identical to every unit since PR #65 and a twelfth consecutive unchanged reading. Every forty-character hex string in the 941-line log was enumerated and all five explained: the reviewed head 62 times, the `actions/setup-node` pin 38, the harness pin 12, the `actions/checkout` pin 10 and the Azure runner image provisioner commit `f3dc54e1` three times. That last figure corrects a phrase in the PR #83 entry, which described the appearance of a second provisioner commit as a completed image rollover; this run returned to the earlier commit on all three jobs, so GitHub's fleet evidently serves more than one image version rather than having rolled forward once. Both commits remain accounted for as runner infrastructure rather than repository references. Steps 7 and 8 rechecked the exact head and the retry budget immediately before authentication and step 11 re-read the live head after testing, all passing.

Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39731354939-1__37982166d405__12e358881c45`, which the Dev Hub independently showed created at 11:34:46 by the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null error code, with a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 11:36:11 and zero active scratch org records. The log reported one owned org deleted and none already deleted, fallback cleanup correctly skipped and logout succeeded. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, that same user at 11:34:40 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication. Because this unit records publication attempts, the credential search was extended to `publication_attempt`, `publication_digest`, `VersionData`, any `068`, `005` or `00D` identifier, alongside `session_secret`, `csrf`, `kusanya_admin_session`, `TENANT_CREDENTIAL` and `DATABASE_URL`, with zero matches across every one and twenty-one masked redactions. The verifier's own authorization remained usable afterwards.

`PASS: PR #87 merged as c927575`, with squash tree `c235b44` identical to the verified head tree. It was the only open pull request, so the strict-protection ordering hazard did not apply, and the merge was confirmed clean by computing the intersection of red checks against the three required contexts, which returned none.

Ledger: zero findings. Note 102 is closed on measured evidence. Note 103 is raised as described, informational, and concerns documentation accuracy about an operational boundary rather than any code path in this change. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86 and 103 remain informational. This advances note 34 through a reviewable durable-attempt route without closing it, because the route is deliberately unmounted until external identity and deployment-secret composition are separately approved, and reconciliation, abandoned-artifact retention, a privileged retry, disconnect and rebind, and the publish CLI all remain. ADR 0047 is explicit that an unresolved row is a reconciliation signal and never permission to retry, which is the right thing to have recorded before a reconciler exists. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-22: PR #89 adds read-only publication reconciliation (ADR 0048); 0 findings, note 103 closed, PASS at `6d8be57`

One commit `6d8be57` on accepted main `910d5a1`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, supplied the recovery primitive that ADR 0047 promised. Fifteen files changed, 1,633 insertions and 17 deletions, comprising migration `0007_publication_attempt_reconciliation`, a new `salesforce-publication-inspector.ts`, a new `publication-reconciler.ts`, a resolve operation added to the attempt store, ADR 0048, and the note 103 corrections to ADR 0006 and `docs/ci.md`. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission, namespace or sharing file changed. `service/src/main.ts`, `service/src/app.ts` and `docs/openapi.yaml` were unchanged and all three were read to confirm the reconciler is genuinely unmounted with no HTTP route. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 335 of 335, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 7 of 7 with zero skips. Eighty-two independent assertions were driven against the compiled modules, that real database and a complete fake Salesforce, and all eighty-two passed.

The PR #87 relay named four properties this unit would be judged on, and all four hold, three of them enforced by the schema rather than only by the calling code.

Reconciliation can only act on a `started` or `partial` attempt and never on a completed one. A `succeeded` attempt and a `failed` attempt are each refused with zero Salesforce requests and their reconciliation left null. More importantly, migration 0007's check constraint contains no branch at all for either outcome, so the database itself was attacked with raw SQL and refused to record any reconciliation against a `succeeded` attempt, including an attempt to mark one `indeterminate`. The same constraint refused a `partial` marked `indeterminate`, a `partial` recorded as committed with a zero request count, a `started` attempt classified as `committed`, an unrecognised status value and a status written without a reconciliation timestamp. The legal state space is expressed in the schema rather than merely respected by the caller.

Reconciling is idempotent and one-way, and racing reconcilers cannot double-resolve an attempt. Six concurrent reconcilers against one `partial` attempt all returned a result, all agreed on a single status, the persisted row matched that status, and the losers reported the winning reconciler's request count rather than their own. The mechanism is visible in the statement: the update is guarded by `reconciliation_status IS NULL`, and when zero rows are affected the store reloads the attempt and returns the winner's immutable result instead of erroring or overwriting. Replaying a resolved attempt afterwards returned the same immutable result with zero OAuth and zero Salesforce requests, leaving the row byte-identical.

The reconciler distinguishes the three real situations using only what the ledger retained. A `partial` attempt resolves to `committed`, `unlinked` or `artifacts_missing` from read-only inspection: a GET of the stored Form Version record by its stored identifier, and where that record is still a clean `Draft`, a SOQL `SELECT` for the two stored ContentVersion identifiers, where zero rows means `artifacts_missing` and two rows matching the expected titles and extensions mean `unlinked`. The digest and both version identifiers that PR #87 insisted on retaining are exactly what the comparison consumes, which is what they were kept for. A `Published` record whose digest disagrees with the ledger is not called `committed`, and a ContentVersion identifier that Salesforce does not match is refused; both leave reconciliation null rather than guessing.

The reconciler cannot publish or re-publish as a side effect, and this is true by construction rather than by discipline. The inspector contains exactly one fetch call site, with a hardcoded `GET` method, no request body, `cache: no-store`, `credentials: omit`, `redirect: manual` and an abort signal raced against the deadline. Across a full reconciliation the only non-GET request was the OAuth token exchange. Neither the reconciler nor the inspector imports the publisher or the artifact attestation, neither contains a `POST`, `PATCH`, `PUT` or `DELETE` method literal, and neither references the commit, definition or `composite/sobjects` endpoints. That is the boundary ADR 0047 stated in prose, now enforced by what the module is able to reach.

One property deserves separate mention because it is cheap to get wrong. A `started` attempt resolves to `indeterminate` with zero requests of any kind, so no session is created, no refresh token is decrypted and Salesforce is never contacted, and the schema pins that pairing by requiring a zero reconciliation request count for exactly that case. The original `started` outcome is preserved, which keeps the row's meaning as an unresolved signal rather than converting it into a verdict.

Every recorded identity and trusted tuple that could be mutated was refused with reconciliation left null and the original outcome untouched: a different expected org, a different authorization origin, a different namespace, a different actor and a different tenant. The store's update rejoins the administrator binding and the tenant row in one statement and rechecks actor, tenant, org against both the attempt and the tenant record, origin and namespace, so a drifted tuple cannot be reconciled against stale context.

Hostile shapes were refused without executing their traps. An accessor-backed context was refused with its getter never running, as were a proxy context, an extra key, a null context, and malformed, non-version-four and unknown attempt identifiers. A provider throwing with the access token, refresh token and stored identifiers embedded in its message produced the fixed `PUBLICATION_RECONCILIATION_SESSION` code, disclosed none of them, and left reconciliation null. The one injection shape worth testing was also checked: the SOQL interpolates the stored ContentVersion identifiers, so a value containing a quote was written toward the ledger and the `^068[A-Za-z0-9]{15}$` constraint refused it, meaning no quote can reach the query string.

Note 103 is closed, and because that note was raised against this log's own over-confident claim the new wording was read against the evidence rather than welcomed. Both `docs/ci.md` and ADR 0006 now separate the two things previously conflated: the `CreatedDate = TODAY` boundary is described as evidenced at midnight `America/Los_Angeles`, while the allocation resetting on that same boundary is described as inferred rather than directly observed. They record that `DailyScratchOrgs.remaining` returned six of six after an org was created and deleted within the same Salesforce day, state that it is therefore not a reliable consumption figure and cannot be called authoritative, and say that the preflight's daily arm cannot guarantee an exhausted day is blocked, with the Salesforce creation request remaining the authoritative enforcement point. The separation from the harness's deliberately UTC-day retry budget is preserved. That is exactly the distinction requested and it overstates nothing in either direction. The underlying harness limitation is correctly left as a separate trust-boundary unit, which is right because `scripts/` is byte-identical to main in this change.

Three defects in the verifier's own probe are disclosed, because together they cost three cycles before any assertion was meaningful and they share one cause: guessing a contract that was readable in the module under test. A `salesforceApiVersion` configuration key was invented and correctly rejected by an exact-key check; the entry point was assumed to throw when it in fact returns a discriminated result carrying `ok`, a status or code, and a request count, so the probe read every refusal as a success; and the Salesforce status field was guessed as `Publication_Status__c` when the inspector asks for `Status__c`, which made every inspection fail at the first GET. Following the standing rule of chasing an unexpected code rather than adjusting the assertion is what surfaced all three, and "read the contract before writing the fake" has been added to the standing list of probe traps.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39783440396 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, a thirteenth consecutive unchanged reading. Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39783440396-1__6d8be572ab29__d52363fd5466`, which the Dev Hub independently showed created at 13:18:03 by the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, Deleted with a null error code, with a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 13:19:15 and zero active scratch org records. Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login to the Dev Hub org-wide, that same user at 13:17:56 via Salesforce CLI from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication. The credential search was extended for this unit to `reconciliation_status`, `publication_digest`, any `068` ContentVersion identifier and any `00D` identifier, with zero matches across all of them and twenty-one masked redactions. One case-insensitive pattern returned a non-zero count and was accounted for rather than dismissed: `RECONCILIATION` matched twice, both times the commit subject line echoed by `git checkout`, with no reconciliation status, digest or artifact identifier anywhere in the log.

This run also settles a question this log has recorded three times. Six forty-character hex strings appeared: the reviewed head 62 times, the harness pin 12, the `actions/checkout` pin 10, the `actions/setup-node` pin 8, and both Azure runner image provisioner commits, `267fdb4e` twice and `f3dc54e1` once. The run is confined to a single day, with the classify job at 13:03 and the Apex job at 13:17 on `267fdb4e` and the verification gate at 13:19 on `f3dc54e1`. ## 2026-10-22: PR #91 presents publication attempts read-only (ADR 0049); 0 findings, notes 104 and 105, PASS at `3ffd2ad`

One commit `3ffd2ad` on accepted main `9093b53`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, gave the reconciler's verdicts a reader. Ten files changed, 896 insertions and 4 deletions, comprising a new `publication-attempt-presentation-routes.ts`, a `listForOperator` operation added to the attempt store, ADR 0049, and the usual architecture, data model, roadmap and service README updates. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were identical on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission, namespace or sharing file changed. `service/src/main.ts`, `service/src/app.ts` and `docs/openapi.yaml` were unchanged and all three were read to confirm the route is genuinely unmounted and undocumented. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 278 of 278, service unit 343 of 343, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 7 of 7 with zero skips. Ninety independent assertions were driven against the compiled modules and that real database, and eighty-nine passed on the first attempt.

The PR #89 relay named four properties this unit would be judged on. All four hold, and two are stricter than the relay asked for.

The route is read-only by construction rather than by discipline, which was checked by what the module can reach rather than by what it does. Only `app.get` is registered, so `POST`, `PUT`, `PATCH` and `DELETE` on the path all return 404. The adapter imports neither the publisher, the reconciler nor the inspector, imports no connection or session store that could yield a credential, contains no non-GET method literal and issues no fetch of its own. The injected dependency is typed as a single-method pick of the attempt store, and when a store that also exposed `start`, `complete` and `resolve` was deliberately supplied, a normal listing called none of them.

Tenant scoping was tested with two genuinely provisioned tenants rather than one. The first tenant's session saw exactly its own two attempts and neither of the second tenant's, and the second saw exactly its own one attempt and neither of the first tenant's. With the tenant, actor and expected org headers all forged to the second tenant, the listing was unchanged. The store's single `SELECT` joins the administrator binding on actor and tenant, joins the tenant row, and rechecks tenant, org against both the attempt and the tenant record, authorization origin and namespace.

The response cannot disclose what the attempt table excludes, and the exclusion is stronger than the relay asked for. Each item carries exactly six fields, being the attempt identifier, the form version identifier, an operator status and the started, completed and reconciled timestamps. The relay asked only that the route not reach past the column set; in fact it exposes neither the actor, the org, the digest, either ContentVersion identifier, the failure code nor the Salesforce request count, and the SQL does not select those columns at all, so there is nothing left to filter. The response schema then holds the same line independently: a store that injected the actor, org, digest, failure code, request count, a ContentVersion identifier and a session secret into every item was fully stripped by the serializer, leaving exactly the six fields, and the top-level object carries only the attempts array.

Uncertainty is presented as requiring human judgement rather than as failure, and the vocabulary does this at the level of the words themselves. Every legal ledger state was seeded and mapped as follows: an unreconciled `started` attempt to a status naming unresolved human judgement, a `started` attempt reconciled `indeterminate` to a status naming indeterminate human judgement, `succeeded` to published, `failed` to rejected, and `partial` reconciled as committed, unlinked or artifacts missing to three distinct confirmed statuses. No status anywhere in the set matches failed, pending, retry, error or unknown, and an unreconciled `partial` is called neither published nor rejected nor confirmed. The three confirmed names also carry the reconciler's actual finding through to the operator instead of flattening all three into one bucket, which is what makes the view useful rather than merely safe.

Eleven HTTP boundary attacks were each refused with no attempt data disclosed, the body being only a fixed error string: a wrong origin, a missing origin, a tenant selector in the query, any query at all, a missing session cookie, a malformed session, duplicate session cookies, an unknown session, a missing CSRF token, a wrong CSRF token and another live session's CSRF token. A revoked session is refused. Requiring a session-derived CSRF token on a `GET` is unusual and correct here, because it means the endpoint cannot be triggered by navigation at all. The listing is bounded at one hundred rows, confirmed by seeding one hundred and five, ordered newest first, with every item still carrying only the six fields. A store that throws yields 503 with a fixed message and no secret. The note 94 logging guard applies, so registration on an unhardened host is refused and no route survives.

Two defects in the verifier's own probe are disclosed. An assertion that the body contained no reconciliation status word used a needle that legitimately matches the intended public status naming indeterminate human judgement, which made the assertion meaningless until it was respecified against the raw field name. Separately, a first integration run reported 6 of 7 because the probe itself had left one hundred and five attempt rows and extra sessions in the shared database; on a reset schema the suite passes 7 of 7, so the builder's claim is accurate. Checking the environment before the code is what resolved the second in one step, which is the trap recorded after PR #81.

The protected run was required rather than optional. Hosted run 39824051054 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, the budget recheck immediately before authentication returning an initial allowance with no prior attempt against this head and UTC day, and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, a fourteenth consecutive unchanged reading. The exact head was confirmed four times, by the preflight commit check, twice by the recheck step against both the checked-out tree and the live API, and by the post-test stale rejection, with the gate job's policy sha agreeing. Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__39824051054-1__3ffd2ad598e4__ab9702afb1a1`, which the Dev Hub independently showed created at 19:42:51 by the dedicated CI user `005bm00000YXZrRAAX`, whose profile is Minimum Access, Deleted with a null error code, with a matching single `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 19:44:06 and zero active scratch org records. The fallback cleanup step is absent from the log, which is correct because it is skipped when the primary cleanup succeeds, and the final step logged out of the Dev Hub. The credential search covered the auth URL scheme, refresh and access tokens, bearer prefixes, private keys, client secrets and passwords, any organization, user or ContentVersion identifier pattern, and this unit's own vocabulary of reconciliation status, operator status, publication digest, CSRF and session, with zero matches across all of them and thirty-two masked redactions. One case-insensitive pattern returned a non-zero count and was accounted for rather than dismissed: a Salesforce auth URL error name matched twice, both times the literal error code inside the harness's retryable classifier echoed because the step prints its own source.

Six forty-character hex strings appeared and all six were mapped: the reviewed head 108 times, the harness pin 18, the `actions/checkout` pin 15, the `actions/setup-node` pin 12, and both Azure runner image provisioner commits, `f3dc54e1` three times and `267fdb4e` twice. The mapping confirms again what the PR #89 entry settled. The classify job at 17:35 and the verification gate at 19:44 both ran provisioner `f3dc54e1` with image 20261012.334.1, while the Apex job at 19:42 ran provisioner `267fdb4e` with image 20261018.339.1, so two jobs two minutes apart landed on different images. GitHub's hosted fleet serves both image versions concurrently, with no rollover and no time dependence, and both commits remain runner infrastructure rather than repository references.

Two hours separate the first job from the second, and that gap is the deployment approval wait rather than work. The credentialed window is about ninety seconds. This entry records that the verifier predicted fifteen to twenty minutes of run time and was wrong, and that the correction came from reading the PR #89 entry's own record of an org created at 13:18:03 and deleted at 13:19:15 rather than from treating the short window as an anomaly. A future entry should expect a credentialed window measured in seconds and should not read brevity as evidence that work was skipped, because the Apex result and coverage figure can only come from a real org.

`PASS: PR #91 merged as fbd72ca`, with squash tree `2b5ac63` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 20:36 by the repository account. It was the only open pull request, so the strict-protection ordering hazard did not apply, and all five checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. After the merge the three trust-boundary trees were compared again against accepted main and all three remained identical, and the harness pin still appeared exactly twice in the workflow on the merged tip, so the squash carried nothing across the boundary. Capacity at the time of merge, counted from `ScratchOrgInfo` rather than the figure note 103 established as unreliable, stood at three orgs created in the current Salesforce day with none active.

Ledger: zero findings and two informational notes. Note 104 records that the response schema declares the operator status with an enum of the seven statuses and that the enum is not enforced at serialization time. The serializer strips unknown properties, which was verified thoroughly, but it does not validate enum membership, and a summary carrying an invented status was served with HTTP 200. The guarantee that only the seven statuses can reach an operator therefore rests on the store's mapper rather than on the schema, and the mapper is sound today, assigning exactly those seven values and throwing in three places for any row it cannot map, so no real ledger state can produce anything else. ADR 0049 claims only that the schema strips any extra property, which is accurate, so this is an observation rather than a documentation inaccuracy. It is recorded because the enum reads like a guard and is not one, so a second producer of these summaries, or a mapper branch that bypasses those throws, would not be caught by the schema. Note 105 is a verifier hygiene note. Listing all local orgs authenticates as every org this machine holds a connection for, including the dedicated CI user, and those logins land in the same login history used to prove that exactly one credentialed login occurred during an Apex job. On this run the extra CI user login fell forty-seven minutes after the window and was easy to attribute, the only login inside the window being the CI user from a hosted runner address, but a run verified closer to one of the verifier's own status checks would produce a login inside the window that looks like an unexplained second authentication. The practice adopted is to query a named alias directly rather than list all orgs while a protected run is in flight, and to state the attribution of every login in the window rather than filter by address. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84 and 86 remain informational. This advances note 34 by giving the reconciler's verdicts a reader, without closing it, because the route is deliberately unmounted and there is still no operator interface, no pagination, no retention or deletion policy, no scheduled reconciliation, no privileged retry, no disconnect or rebind and no publish CLI. ADR 0048's rule that a reconciliation records what was found and never acts on it survives into the view, which is the property this reviewer most wanted carried forward, because the operator is shown a finding and told that judgement is required rather than offered a button. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion claim is made.

## 2026-10-27: PR #93 mounts the OIDC administrator publication surface (ADR 0050); FINDING 1 raised and closed, notes 106 and 107, PASS at `2a11db4`

Two commits on accepted main `0cc5cbf`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, gave the reviewed but deliberately unmounted routes an external identity and a single composition that reaches them. The first head `39ff2c1` was held with one finding and the second head `2a11db4` closed it. Twenty-six files changed, 1,473 insertions and 117 deletions, comprising a new `tenant/oidc-login.ts`, a new `publication-surface.ts`, a new `runtime-config.ts`, a query-executor adapter in `database.ts`, the real wiring in `app.ts` and `main.ts`, the first HTTP contract published in `docs/openapi.yaml` for the administrator surface, ADR 0050, amendments to ADRs 0041, 0042, 0044, 0047 and 0049, and three new test files. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were identical on both sides at both heads, the harness pin appeared twice, and no Salesforce metadata, permission, namespace or sharing file changed. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the fixed head: root 278 of 278, service unit 352 of 352, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, the production dependency audit reporting no known vulnerability, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration at 7 of 7 with zero skips, which the builder had correctly declared it could not run and had asked the verifier to supply. Two hundred and eleven independent assertions were driven across three probe files against the compiled modules, that real database and a real generated RSA key, and two hundred and six passed, the five failures being five instances of one informational note.

This unit crossed a boundary that every previous unit had left alone, so the dependency change was reviewed before any install was run. Every changed lockfile integrity hash was compared against the npm registry and all five matched exactly: `fastify@5.12.7`, the newly added `jose@6.2.12`, `fast-uri@3.1.9`, `fast-uri@4.2.3` and the development-only `brace-expansion@5.0.12`. The bumps were not cosmetic. Fastify 5.12.6 patched GHSA-389g-gv7q-r4x8, a header validation bypass affecting versions from 5.12.2 up to 5.12.6, so the previous exact pin of 5.12.3 had been exposed to it. The two `fast-uri` moves land precisely on the first patched versions for four advisories published on 23 October. The new direct dependency is published by the library's own author, carries no advisory in any 6.x range, and introduced exactly one package into the tree with no overrides block. A newer patch, 6.2.13, exists and nothing requires it.

Because `http-security.ts` was left unchanged while Fastify moved underneath it, the note 94 logging guard was the single property this reviewer trusted least, and ADR 0050 was right to say the guard is rerun rather than assumed compatible. A 5.12.7 instance still carries exactly one `fastify.logController` symbol with `disableRequestLogging` as an own data property, and the guard still refuses an app with no controller, one constructed with the flag false, one left at its default, and a proxied app. Shape is not behaviour, so the behaviour was measured as well: with a captured log stream an unhardened 5.12.7 app emitted two automatic request log lines for one request and the hardened app emitted none. The flag the guard inspects is still the flag Fastify honours.

The OIDC module is the most carefully built security code reviewed in this repository so far, and fifty assertions against a real RSA key all passed. A correctly signed current token verifies and returns only an actor identifier, shaped as `oidc:` plus a forty-three character base64url digest that discloses neither issuer nor subject. Everything forged collapsed to one fixed error: a wrong issuer, the multitenant common issuer substituted for the configured one, a wrong audience, an expired token, a token whose issued-at time exceeded the five minute maximum age, a future-dated token, a missing expiry, issued-at or subject claim, an empty, non-string, over-long or control-character subject, a token signed by a different RSA key, an unsigned token claiming no algorithm, an HS256 token presented with a valid key identifier, a PS256 token, a two-segment token and an empty token. Standard semantics are preserved where they should be, so an audience array containing the exact audience is accepted. Configuration is held to exact HTTPS, refusing an http issuer, an issuer carrying a port, a query or credentials, an http key-set URI, an empty audience and an audience containing a space. The actor derivation is stable, issuer-separated and unambiguous: the same issuer and subject always derive the same value, the exported provisioning helper derives it identically, the same subject under a different issuer derives a different value, and the NUL delimiter cannot be smuggled into the concatenation because an issuer containing NUL cannot pass the exact-href check at all. The derived actor is forty-eight characters and fits the existing route actor pattern, so the ADR's claim that it fits the existing actor key is verifiable rather than asserted.

The composition fails closed on everything that was removed from it. Removing each of the eight capabilities in turn refuses the whole surface, as do an extra unexpected capability, a proxied capability set, an accessor-backed capability whose getter was confirmed never to run, a symbol-keyed capability, a session store missing revoke, an attempt store missing its operator listing, a connection store missing its refresh lock, a proxied session store, a non-function verifier and a non-string origin. An external origin that is http or carries a port, a path or a trailing slash is refused. The hardened-logging check runs before any route is registered, and on a refused host all five paths return not-found across both methods, so no route survives the refusal. Without the surface none of the five paths is routed and the health probe still answers. Thirty-three of thirty-three boundary attacks across the four protected routes were refused with no disclosure, covering a wrong origin, a missing origin, a missing, malformed, duplicated or unknown session cookie, and a missing, wrong or foreign CSRF token, with a revoked session refused on all four and a tenant selector in the query string refused. A forged tenant, actor and expected-org header triple changed the response not at all. Method discipline holds on the real application, and the mounted attempt summary still carries exactly the six fields and sets no-store, so note 104's boundary survives mounting and no second producer of operator statuses was introduced. All fifteen runtime settings are required and naming the setting, and the overall publication timeout is constrained to be no lower than the per-request timeout, which is a real invariant rather than two independent bounds. Separately, and found by accident while testing, `readConfig` refuses a disabled database TLS setting when the environment is production.

The finding was a seam defect rather than a flaw in either module, and it is the reason this unit took two heads. The OIDC verifier signals every rejection by throwing, while the login route classified its catch by type and gave the authentication status only to its own input-error class, so every other error became a service-unavailable response. Seven outcomes driven through the real application showed six distinct refusals reported as an outage: no authorization header, a malformed token, a wrong audience, an expired token, a token older than five minutes, and a correctly verified token whose derived actor had no tenant binding, which is precisely the authorization decision ADR 0050 singles out. Only the provisioned success returned its no-content status. The route's authentication branch was therefore unreachable in production while `docs/openapi.yaml`, added by this very change, declared it, so the implementation contradicted its own published contract. The mechanism was confirmed rather than inferred, because a verifier that returned a malformed value instead of throwing did produce the authentication status. Nothing was bypassed, no session was issued, the body was a fixed string and every rejected login cleared the cookie, so this was a correctness and contract defect rather than a security hole; it was held because the fix changes observable status codes on a security boundary, because monitoring and client retry behaviour depend on the distinction, and because it was far cheaper to settle before the surface was first exposed. It was also invisible to the test suite by construction, since the identity tests called the verifier directly and the composition tests injected a stub verifier that always succeeded, so the one seam the unit exists to create had no coverage at all.

The fix at the second head is narrow and was verified from both directions. A single classifier accepts three types only, being the route's input error, the OIDC error, and the session error carrying exactly the absent-session code, and everything else keeps the unavailable status. All nine refusal classes now return the generic authentication status with the cookie cleared. The inverse risk received equal attention, because a classifier that was too generous would hide a real outage behind an authentication response and be worse than the original defect: ten unexpected failure shapes still return the generic unavailable status disclosing no internal detail, including a generic error, a thrown string, a thrown null, a type error, a storage failure, a conflict, an input failure, a raw connection-refused driver error, and a genuinely unreachable database on a dead port rather than a simulated one. The classifier matches by type and not by message, proven with an imposter error whose message and name were set to impersonate the OIDC error and which still received the unavailable status. Of the four session failure codes exactly one maps to authentication. That narrowness is sound at the source, because the absent-session code is raised from exactly one site and only when the insert against the administrator bindings returns no rows, while a query failure in the same method is caught separately as a storage failure and a broken row count becomes a conflict, so the one code the route now trusts cannot be produced by an infrastructure fault and the authentication status cannot mask an outage. The same actor returns the authentication status before provisioning and succeeds after it, with nothing else changed. The new composition test injects a rejection through the real application factory and asserts the status and exact body, so the seam now carries regression cover, and the ADR records the boundary with its retry and monitoring rationale, leaving route, document and ADR in agreement.

Four defects in the verifier's own probes are disclosed, all found and corrected before either verdict. A valid-token login assertion failed because the probe never provisioned the derived actor, which was misread for a moment as a verification failure; chasing it rather than relaxing the assertion is what produced the finding's sixth case, so the standing rule paid for itself. A non-canonical secret test was vacuous because the mutation appended a padding character to a string that already ended in one, making it the identity, which was confirmed explicitly before being replaced with four genuinely non-canonical forms that are all refused. A third failure was a knock-on of the first. The fourth appeared during re-verification, where a harness verifier sliced the authorization header itself and so bypassed the production bearer parser, making a lowercase scheme appear to succeed; retested against the real verifier, that case and ten other header shapes all return the authentication status, which also reduced note 107 to an interoperability remark. The common cause of the first and fourth is a stub standing in for the module under test and quietly skipping the check being tested.

The protected run was required rather than optional, and the superseded run deserves a note of its own. When the head changed, run 40474148493 was cancelled at the old head and the supersede check reported success, which is ADR 0030's workflow firing live for the second time this reviewer has verified: it cancelled a zero-step run at the older sha without consuming an org. The approved replacement, hosted run 40482971958 attempt 1, passed at the exact fixed head with no retry consumed, the pre-authentication budget recheck returning an initial allowance with no prior attempt against this head and UTC day, and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, a fifteenth consecutive unchanged reading. The exact head was confirmed five times, by the preflight commit check, twice by the recheck step against both the checked-out tree and the live API, by the post-test stale rejection, and by the gate job's policy sha. Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__40482971958-1__2a11db49e4d7__725ec71d23d9`, which the Dev Hub independently showed as the only org created in the Salesforce day, created at 10:00:01 by the dedicated CI user, Deleted with a null error code, with exactly one matching `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 10:01:31 and zero active scratch org records. The fallback cleanup step is absent, correctly, because it is skipped when the primary cleanup succeeds, and the final step logged out of the Dev Hub. The credential search covered the usual auth URL, token, key, secret and identifier patterns and was extended to this unit's own vocabulary of session secret, credential keys, OIDC settings, key-set URI, derived actor prefix, JWT prefix and CSRF, with zero matches across all of them and forty-two masked redactions. One case-insensitive pattern returned a non-zero count and was accounted for rather than dismissed: `authorization` matched eight times and every match was the identical `actions/checkout` git extraheader line with its value masked, once per checkout step and once per cleanup.

Across the Apex job window there was exactly one login org-wide, the CI user from a hosted runner address, with no verifier authentication inside the window. Only one other login existed in the surrounding hour and it was the verifier's own capacity check two minutes before the job began. That is cleaner than the previous unit's run and deliberately so, because note 105 had recorded that listing all local orgs authenticates as every locally held connection including the CI user and pollutes exactly this evidence; querying named aliases only produced a login history with no spurious entry to explain away, so the note has already paid for itself.

Five forty-character hex strings appeared and all five were mapped: the reviewed head 124 times, the harness pin 24, the `actions/checkout` pin 20, the `actions/setup-node` pin 16, and a single Azure runner image provisioner commit six times. That last commit, `21505ad2`, was new to this log and was chased before anything was passed, which is what the standing rule exists for. It is the provisioner at version 20261021.618, newer than both commits seen in earlier runs and replacing them, and all three jobs ran that one provisioner with image 20261012.334.1, so this run was homogeneous unlike the previous unit's run where two provisioner versions were served minutes apart. That remains consistent with a fleet serving several versions concurrently; all three jobs simply drew the same one. Five shas is within the five or six range a previous entry told future readers to expect.

`PASS: PR #93 merged as 73efc61`, with squash tree `864febf` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 10:37 by the repository account. It was the only open pull request, so the strict-protection ordering hazard did not apply, and all six checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. After the merge the three trust-boundary trees were compared again against accepted main and all three remained identical, and the harness pin still appeared exactly twice on the merged tip, so the squash carried nothing across the boundary.

Ledger: one finding, raised at the first head and closed at the second, and two informational notes. Note 106 records that a malformed, as opposed to missing, OIDC issuer, audience or key-set URI is fail-closed but anonymous, because the configuration reader checks only presence while the exact-HTTPS and audience checks live in the verifier's own decoder, and the entry point prints a message only for a configuration error. This was proven by running the compiled service: a missing issuer names the setting while an http issuer prints a bare startup failure, and every other setting names itself. Startup is prevented in all cases, so this is diagnosability rather than security, and moving those three checks into the configuration reader would settle it cheaply. Note 107 records that the bearer parser requires the literal capitalised scheme while the relevant standard makes the scheme name case-insensitive, which is harmless for a first-party browser client and is now refused with the correct status, leaving only an interoperability remark for whenever a second client is written; it also notes that a key-set fetch failure is indistinguishable from an invalid token, which is correct for the response body and overlaps note 106 for diagnosis. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 104, 105, 106 and 107 remain informational. This advances note 34 through external identity and mounting, without closing it, because there is still no operator interface, no self-service actor and tenant provisioning, no pagination, no retention or deletion policy, no scheduled reconciliation, no privileged retry, no disconnect or rebind and no publish CLI, and the pinned ODK Validate Java and jar assets are not yet present in a service runtime image.
