## 2026-10-27: PR #95 adds the trusted Salesforce-identical Apex exemption (ADR 0051); 0 findings, note 108, PASS at `b9aac49`

Two commits on accepted main `04b4b1b`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, changed the verification policy itself rather than the product. Commit `afc78d2` implemented the new decision and became the immutable policy pin, and head `b9aac49` differs from it in exactly two lines, both the `ref:` values in `salesforce-verify.yml`, which now resolve to that pin. Seven files changed, 534 insertions and 40 deletions, comprising the workflow, the pinned classifier `scripts/apex-pr-policy.mjs` and its tests, the inline workflow tests, ADR 0051 and the `docs/ci.md` and `docs/verification.md` amendments. This is the first unit in the project to deliberately modify the two trees every other unit is required to leave byte-identical, so it was reviewed as a new trust boundary rather than as an ordinary change. The verifier also recorded a bias risk at the top of the review, because the change implements a throughput recommendation the verifier itself had made, and therefore attacked the design rather than confirming it. Local evidence reproduced exactly at the head: root 282 of 282, the focused policy and workflow suites 46 of 46, scaffold, Prettier and `git diff --check` clean. Ninety-four independent adversarial assertions were driven at the classifier as it exists at the pin, and ninety-three passed; the single failure was a synthetic input shape shown to be unreachable and defended twice over.

The design question that decides whether the exemption is sound was not on the builder's thirteen-point list, and the verifier added it. The exemption asserts that when the `salesforce` tree is byte-identical to accepted main a hosted Apex run carries no new information, and that holds only if `salesforce/` is the complete set of pull-request-controlled inputs to the Apex verification. It is, and by construction rather than by discipline: the apex job's reviewed checkout specifies `sparse-checkout: salesforce`, so only that tree from the pull request is ever materialised on the runner, the harness itself arrives from the pinned trusted checkout, and `verify-salesforce.mjs` resolves `KUSANYA_SALESFORCE_DIR` and reads only beneath it. The policy job's reviewed checkout is sparse on the same path, which was confirmed in the live classify log. The exemption is therefore exactly coextensive with the only pull-request-controlled bytes the Apex job can observe, and the sixteen consecutive identical readings are corroboration rather than the argument. ADR 0051's revisit trigger of moving Salesforce source outside `salesforce/` is precisely the right one, and moving or widening that sparse checkout is the same event.

One apparent weakness was investigated and cleared rather than reported. Pinning trusted policy to a commit that is not an ancestor of `main` looks unsound, but the superseded pin `1d0edc18` is also not an ancestor of `main`, so pinning to a pull-request commit is the established pattern this log has already accepted in earlier units, and GitHub keeps pull-request commits reachable. The `scripts` tree is identical between the implementation commit and the head, at `bd671830`, so the reviewed code is the code the pin executes, and the live log confirmed the trusted checkout reporting the implementation commit by name.

The adversarial probe established a baseline first, proving that an ordinary application pull request with an identical Salesforce tree genuinely reaches the new decision, so that none of the refusals was vacuous. Every hostile variation then fell back to full Apex. On identity: a fork head repository, a fork base repository, a missing head repository, a stale head, a stale checkout, a base commit behind accepted main, accepted main moving under the pull request, a nondefault base branch, a base ref disagreeing with the event, a closed pull request, an already merged pull request, a missing merged flag, an empty diff, a malformed repository identity, a missing default branch, a short head identifier and an uppercase head identifier. On events: manual dispatch always forces full Apex and says so, as do a push event, a missing event name and a target-event name. On the trust boundary: changes to the workflow directory and to the scripts directory, both as exact paths and as nested files, a change under the Salesforce directory, replacement of each of those three directories by a file, and renames out of and into the Salesforce directory as well as out of the scripts directory, which matter because a rename hides in the previous filename. On the Salesforce root entry: a differing tree identifier, a missing entry on either side, and replacement by a regular file, an executable file, a symbolic link, a submodule or a directory with an unexpected mode, plus a malformed identifier.

The attack the verifier most wanted to land concerns contradictory evidence, because the classifier consumes API JSON rather than Git objects directly. Trees were supplied whose root Salesforce entry carried an identical identifier on both sides while the file list simultaneously proved an added Apex class underneath it, a state Git's Merkle structure makes impossible but a hostile or faulty API response could claim. The classifier refuses, so the file list wins over the tree. Also refused: a file absent from the head tree, a file identifier disagreeing with the tree, an added file already present on accepted main, a removed file still present on the head, a file count above or below the reported diff, a duplicated entry, an unknown status, a rename without a previous filename, a previous filename on a non-rename and a non-array file list. On tree completeness: a truncated tree on either side, a missing truncation flag, a malformed tree identifier, a non-array payload, a duplicate entry path, an unknown entry type and a non-string mode, together with absolute, traversing, current-directory, backslash, empty, control-character and trailing-slash paths in the file list.

Nothing can request the exemption. The pull request body, title and labels were set to the exemption's own name and a forged request field and label were added to the input, and the decision and label were unchanged because both are computed. Reading the workflow confirms this at the source: the only pull-request-derived values anywhere are the pull request number and head identifier supplied by GitHub, never pull request content, and the dispatch inputs exist only for the manual path that always forces full Apex. The exempt result exposes exactly seven decision fields and nothing else. The documentation exemption remains exact and separate, still excluding the four critical documents and the decisions directory, emitting no Salesforce tree identity, which the gate now independently requires, and prose whose bytes are not verified text no longer qualifies as documentation but falls through to the Salesforce comparison rather than being waved through.

The gate was read as carefully as the classifier. It rechecks, unconditionally and before the decision switch, the live head, the live base branch against the default branch, the live base commit against the policy's reported base, and the live accepted-main reference including that its object type is a commit and its identifier matches. The apex job's condition is an allowlist on full Apex, so an exempt decision skips Apex without that condition needing to change, and the gate requires a skipped Apex job for both exemptions. The classifier additionally rereads the pull request and the default-branch reference at the end of collection and refuses if either moved, so there is a guard against change in flight on both sides. The exempt gate message prints all three identities together with the words that it is not phase-gate or C10 Apex evidence, and the ADR, `docs/ci.md` and `docs/verification.md` all state that neither exemption is Apex, coverage, namespace, phase-gate or C10 evidence and that manual dispatch remains the reviewed route to real exact-head evidence.

The builder declared that actionlint and ShellCheck were unavailable locally and required verifier reproduction, so both were installed, at versions 1.7.12 and 0.11.0, and run across all three workflow files at the reviewed head with no findings. A clean lint result is worthless if the integration silently did nothing, so it was proven live: on a copy of the workflow a quoted command substitution was replaced with an unquoted one, actionlint reported the corresponding ShellCheck diagnostic and exited nonzero, while the real workflow produces nothing. The repository's own inline workflow tests deserve mention because they extract the real Bash from the YAML and execute it, so they exercise the actual gate script rather than a transcription of it, which is why the new gate coverage is trustworthy.

The unit could not exempt itself, and this was established from the live run rather than by inference. The classify job's budget step executed, and that step is conditional on a full Apex decision; correspondingly the Apex job waited for environment approval with zero steps rather than reporting itself skipped. After approval the gate's own environment recorded a full Apex decision with an empty Salesforce tree identity and a populated accepted-main identity, which also confirmed the one behaviour that could not be checked locally, namely that the new base identifier output is populated on the full Apex path. The three new gate assertions ran against live API state and passed.

Hosted run 40494290943 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed, the pre-authentication budget recheck returning an initial allowance, and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, a sixteenth consecutive unchanged reading. Both credentialed jobs checked out the trusted policy at the new pin, confirming that the reviewed code is the executing code. Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__40494290943-1__b9aac49ac4cb__467b4edbd640`, which the Dev Hub independently showed created at 11:26:37 by the dedicated CI user, Deleted with a null error code, with a matching `deleteScratchOrg` audit entry at 11:28:05 and zero active scratch org records. The fallback cleanup step is absent, correctly, and the final step logged out. Exactly one login occurred org-wide in the surrounding period, the CI user from a hosted runner address inside the Apex window, with no verifier authentication anywhere nearby because the verifier kept to note 105's discipline of querying named aliases rather than listing all local orgs. The credential search returned zero matches across the usual patterns and the preceding unit's secrets vocabulary, with forty-two masked redactions, and the single non-zero case-insensitive pattern was accounted for as the checkout action's own masked git extraheader line.

Six forty-character hex strings appeared and all six were mapped, and one was new in kind rather than merely new in value. The reviewed head appeared 124 times, the new harness pin 24, the checkout action pin 20, the setup-node pin 16, the Azure runner image provisioner 6, and accepted main twice. That last one has never appeared in a run log before and was chased rather than assumed; both occurrences are the gate echoing its accepted-main environment value, which is public repository history and not a credential, and it appears because this change introduced that output. The standing expectation therefore changes and is recorded here for future readers: a full Apex run now shows six identifiers including accepted main, the harness pin value has changed, and the old pin should never appear in a future run log, so seeing it again would mean the workflow was reverted and is a finding rather than a curiosity.

`PASS: PR #95 merged as 899a8a0`, with squash tree `e99c748` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 11:47. It was the only open pull request and all five checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. This merge moves the verification baseline, so the new accepted values are recorded here: `.github` becomes `4a15bb9d7502e75af73fcec73de87c7cdfad4b59`, `scripts` becomes `bd671830a6b751f51fe95d822c7d4aed115b9f74`, `salesforce` remains `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d`, and the harness pin is `afc78d2d8e0d597ae8b8d42c1dd069a0a0dee18e` appearing exactly twice in the workflow with the old pin absent from it. Every earlier entry in this log quoting the previous two tree identifiers is now stale by design, and a future reviewer should read the current values from the repository rather than from this log.

Ledger: zero findings and one informational note. Note 108 records that a classification failure fails the policy job rather than degrading to a real Apex run. When evidence collection throws, for example on a transient API error, the command-line fallback emits a result with no accepted-main identifier, and the workflow's extraction of that field exits nonzero, which under strict shell settings fails the classify step, the policy job and therefore the required gate. This was confirmed by replaying the workflow's exact extraction against the real fallback output with the actual `jq` binary. The behaviour is strictly safer than the previous fall-through to real Apex and cannot grant an exemption, and ADR 0051 already states it exactly, so the note records only the operator consequence: a transient API error now presents as a failed required check whose remedy is a workflow re-run, and because the Apex job never starts the retry budget is untouched and a re-run is cheap. The one probe assertion that did not pass was the verifier's own and is unreachable, since passing a proxy that throws on every property read makes the classifier throw rather than return a full Apex decision; production never sees such a value, the only production call site sits inside a guarded block whose outer fallback was verified empirically to emit a full Apex decision for missing environment and malformed arguments, and the workflow independently asserts the schema version and an allowlisted decision, so two outer layers already fail closed. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 104, 105, 106, 107 and 108 remain informational. This unit is verification policy only and advances nothing in note 34, and it claims no C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state or Phase 1 completion.

One consequence is deliberately left open. The exemption is now available to ordinary application pull requests, so the next unit touching none of the three guarded trees should classify the new decision, skip Apex, consume no scratch org and require no protected approval. That first live firing is owed proof and will be verified specifically, in the same way ADR 0030's supersede workflow was, rather than assumed to work in production because it works in tests. Until that is recorded here the mechanism is reviewed but not proven in the field. Note also that a documentation-only pull request, including this log's own, still takes the older documentation exemption because that check runs first, so such a run is not evidence for the new path.

## 2026-10-27: PR #97 adds tenant operator access and attempt pagination (ADR 0052), and discharges ADR 0051's owed first live firing; 0 findings, notes 109 to 112, PASS at `e64acc0`

Two commits on accepted main `d1df18e`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, delivered the first deliberately larger unit and the first unit to run under the new Salesforce-identical exemption. Twenty files changed, 1,544 insertions and 79 deletions, comprising a new static operator interface at `GET /admin`, a new self-service provisioning route at `POST /api/admin/administrators`, keyset pagination added to the attempt listing and store, the shared OIDC identity capability that both login and provisioning now draw from, the composition and entry-point wiring, ADR 0052, the OpenAPI and documentation updates, and unit plus PostgreSQL integration coverage. `.github`, `scripts` and `salesforce` were identical to accepted main on both sides, the harness pin appeared twice, and no package manifest, lockfile, Salesforce metadata, permission or namespace file changed. Local evidence reproduced exactly as declared: root 282 of 282, service unit 364 of 364, the containerised target also 364 of 364, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration 7 of 7 with zero skips. One hundred and thirty-one independent adversarial assertions were driven against the compiled modules, that real database and a real generated RSA key, and all one hundred and thirty-one passed after two defects in the verifier's own probe were corrected.

This unit also answered a process question. Bill had asked whether the work could proceed in larger modules to shorten the overall timeline, and this reviewer had proposed a sizing rule: bundle freely where a unit adds code behind boundaries already verified, and keep a unit small only where it introduces or changes one. This unit was built to that rule, carrying a single new trust boundary in provisioning while the operator interface and pagination sat behind boundaries verified in PRs #81, #91 and #93, and adding no migration. The rule held: the review was no harder than a small unit's, because the new boundary was one place to attack rather than three.

The owed proof from PR #95 is discharged, and it was verified specifically rather than assumed from passing tests. Hosted run 40509218137 attempt 1 completed in twenty-nine seconds with no approval wait and recorded, in the gate's own environment, the decision `salesforce-identical-exemption`, a policy sha equal to the reviewed head, a policy base sha equal to accepted main, and a Salesforce tree sha of `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d`, which this reviewer had independently confirmed identical on both commits with `git ls-tree`. Apex was skipped, the budget output was empty because the budget step is conditional on full Apex, and the gate printed all three identities together with the statement that this is not phase-gate or C10 Apex evidence. The three live rechecks of base branch, base commit and the accepted-main reference all ran and passed, and the trusted policy executed at the reviewed pin `afc78d2`.

The two negatives were established independently of the workflow log, because they are the substance of the claim. No deployment approval was ever requested: the pending-deployments endpoint returned zero, and the Apex job reached a skipped conclusion with zero steps and a null runner, so no credentialed environment was entered and Bill was never asked. No scratch org was created: the Dev Hub showed zero orgs tagged with this run, zero active scratch orgs, and zero logins of any kind in the surrounding period, so the Dev Hub was never contacted at all. The exemption mechanism is therefore proven in the field on all three of its paths, full Apex at PR #95, documentation at PR #96 and Salesforce-identical here. Against the previous unit's seventeen-minute run with a human approval and a scratch org, this consumed nothing, so the throughput claim behind ADR 0051 is now evidenced rather than argued.

Provisioning was the single new boundary and it holds by construction rather than by convention. The request body is parsed to a plain record and must carry exactly one key, `subject`; every other value handed to the serializable provisioning operation is read from the live session context, namely tenant, Salesforce org, authorization origin and namespace, so no code path allows a request to name any of them. The actor derivation is equally structural: the identity factory returns one frozen object whose subject mapper closes over the same validated issuer that the login verifier uses, composition checks that object has exactly the two expected function keys, and provisioning receives that same object, so no other issuer is available to the route. The mapping was proved to agree with login end to end rather than by inspection: an administrator enrolled a subject, the stored actor equalled the exported derivation for that issuer and subject, the binding was the enroller's own tenant, the provider subject appeared nowhere in the row, and the enrolled subject then logged in successfully and listed its tenant's attempts.

Every attempt to smuggle a tuple field was refused with a fixed non-disclosing body: a body carrying an actor identifier, tenant, Salesforce org, authorization origin, namespace, CSRF token or a second subject; a missing, absent, non-object, array or null body; a prototype-pollution key; and a subject that was non-string, empty, longer than five hundred and twelve characters, control-character bearing or null. Forged tenant, actor, org and namespace headers were ignored entirely, with the enrolment still landing in the enroller's tenant and the other tenant's binding count unchanged. The HTTP boundary held across a wrong and a missing origin, a missing, malformed, duplicated and unknown session cookie, a revoked session, a missing, wrong and foreign CSRF token, a query string, and every non-POST method. Immutability survived: re-enrolling the same subject into the same tenant was idempotent, and one tenant could not capture another tenant's administrator, whose binding and subsequent login both remained intact, so ADR 0043's invariant was not reopened by the new route.

Pagination was attacked at the page boundary, which is where keyset schemes fail. The cursor carries no authority by construction, because the authorization join is unchanged and repeated on every page while the cursor only adds a strictly-less-than restriction on the row pair, so it can narrow but never widen and cannot reach another tenant. With one hundred and two rows for one tenant and one for another, the first page returned exactly one hundred rows with an eighty-six character cursor, the second returned the remaining two with a null continuation, and the union was one hundred and two distinct attempts with no repeat and no omission, the other tenant's row never appearing. The decisive case was forcing all one hundred and two rows to an identical microsecond timestamp: both pages still worked, the union was still one hundred and two distinct rows, and pagination still terminated. That holds because the key is the pair of creation timestamp and attempt identifier with a UUID tiebreak, and because the cursor carries PostgreSQL's exact microsecond value through `to_char` rather than a millisecond JavaScript timestamp, so rows sharing a millisecond cannot be skipped or repeated. The store additionally enforces its own monotonicity, throwing if any row is not strictly below the previous one, so a mis-ordered result fails closed rather than duplicating.

Cross-tenant cursor replay was refused in substance rather than by error: one tenant replaying the other's cursor received a successful response containing zero rows and none of the other tenant's attempts, and still saw exactly its own single row on a normal request, while a cursor hand-dated to 2099 returned that same single row. Ten malformed cursors were each refused with a fixed four-hundred response, covering a flipped character, truncation, over-length, a non-base64url character, an empty value, standard base64 padding in place of base64url, a non-version-four identifier, a millisecond timestamp, two delimiters and an impossible calendar date, as were an extra parameter alongside a valid cursor, a repeated cursor parameter, an unknown parameter and a prototype key. Rows changing under pagination behaved correctly, with a row inserted after the first page absent from the second, which is the intended keyset property, and a deletion mid-pagination neither erroring nor duplicating. Every item still carried exactly the six fields, the body carried only the attempts array and the continuation, and no page disclosed a digest, failure code, request count, actor, tenant, reconciliation status or any artifact or organization identifier.

The operator interface was examined from the outside rather than from its source, because a hash-pinned content security policy is worthless if the hash and the served bytes can drift. They cannot, since the policy is computed over the same module-level constants that are interpolated into the document, and this was confirmed by extracting the served script and style elements from the response and computing their digests independently, both of which matched the header exactly. The policy denies everything by default and allows only same-origin connections and form actions, forbids framing and images, and contains no unsafe-inline, unsafe-eval, unsafe-hashes, wildcard, data or blob source. The response also carries a no-referrer policy, content-type options, both cross-origin policies at same-origin, and a permissions policy disabling camera, microphone, geolocation, display capture, payment and USB. Mechanical searches found no unsafe DOM sink, no evaluation primitive, no inline event handler, no browser storage of any kind, no cookie access, no external asset and no style attribute, the last mattering because style attributes would have required unsafe-hashes. Every value reaches the document through text content only, and fetches are same-origin, no-store and refuse redirects. Credential handling is careful: the access token enters a password field with autocomplete disabled, is read into a local variable, the field is cleared immediately, and the token is sent only in an authorization header to the same-origin login route, while the CSRF value lives in a closure and never enters storage or a URL. On an uncertain publication outcome the shell states that human judgement is required and offers no retry control, which carries ADR 0048's boundary through to the browser.

Composition remains fail-closed across all eight capabilities, and additionally refuses an identity object missing either function, carrying an extra key, proxied, or with a non-function mapper, a session store missing the provisioning operation, and an extra top-level capability. An unhardened host is refused and no route survives on it, including the operator shell, and without the surface none of the new paths is routed.

The second commit deletes twenty-three test lines, which always warrants inspection, and the reason proved legitimate. The deleted test read the OpenAPI document through a path outside the service directory, which is not present in the container build context, so it would have failed the declared container target; the containerised run now passes three hundred and sixty-four of three hundred and sixty-four, which confirms the deletion fixed a real problem rather than concealing one. The lost assertions were nonetheless the document's least-data guard, so every one of them was executed by hand and all passed: the document declares the three paths, requires both response fields, pins the cursor pattern, accepts only a subject for provisioning, exposes exactly seven operator statuses, and mentions no sensitive attempt column.

Two defects in the verifier's own probe are disclosed. A seventeen-character form version identifier was constructed where the store correctly requires fifteen or eighteen, so seeding threw before any pagination assertion ran, which is the standing lesson about proving a fixture can reach the intended state. More instructively, a trap already recorded was tripped again in a new disguise: verifying that a victim administrator could still log in after a failed cross-tenant capture attempt silently replaced that administrator's session, so three later cursor-isolation assertions failed with an authentication status and briefly resembled tenant-isolation defects. Any login displaces a session, not only a revocation, and the standing note has been strengthened to require re-issuing and asserting the replacement before any section reuses a session the probe itself may have displaced.

`PASS: PR #97 merged as 1af7767`, with squash tree `8fbc4dd` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 14:01. It was the only open pull request, and all six checks were green or skipped with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. After the merge the three trust-boundary trees were compared again against accepted main and all three remained identical, with the harness pin still appearing exactly twice. No scratch org was consumed and no human approval was requested at any point in this unit, which is the first time that has been true of a source change.

Ledger: zero findings and four informational notes. Note 109 narrows a claim in ADR 0052, which states that a subject already bound elsewhere returns the same fixed unavailable response as any storage refusal, exposing neither actor existence nor the other tenant. The second half is exactly right and was confirmed, since the body is a fixed string and nothing identifies the other tenant, but the first half is slightly stronger than the behaviour, because a fresh enrolment returns no-content while a cross-tenant conflict returns unavailable, so an administrator who already knows a subject identifier can learn the single bit that the subject is bound somewhere else, though not where. The oracle is weak, requiring a live session and prior knowledge of the subject and being confounded by genuine storage failures, and no truthful alternative status exists, since any response distinguishing enrolled from refused leaks the same bit and reporting success for a refusal would be worse; the remedy is to narrow the wording or to adopt an invitation design. Note 110 records that this unit creates the first route able to add an actor to a tenant and that enrolment is permanent, since bindings are immutable, there is no deprovisioning route, retention and deletion remain outstanding, and there is no role model, so every enrolled actor is itself a full administrator. Each property is deliberate and separately declared, but together they mean the blast radius of one compromised administrator session is now larger and irreversible, which is worth recording so that a deprovisioning capability is understood as a security control rather than a convenience. Note 111 records that the deleted contract assertions were not re-homed, so nothing automated now prevents the document from gaining a sensitive column or losing the cursor pattern, and that the repository root suite runs from the root where the document is available and the container build context does not apply, making a small root-level contract test the natural home. Note 112 records that the presentation route's record guard lost its prototype check with no code comment and no mention in the ADR, that the removal is necessary and safe because Fastify's parsed query object does not carry the ordinary object prototype, which was confirmed directly and is in fact the mechanism behind the refusal of any query string recorded in the PR #91 entry, and that one sentence of comment would prevent a future reader from restoring the check and silently breaking pagination. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 104, 105, 106, 107, 108, 109, 110, 111 and 112 remain informational. Note 34 is materially advanced and still not closed, since the operator interface, self-service provisioning and pagination now exist while initial tenant bootstrap, a role model, the browser-redirect OIDC client, retention and deletion, scheduled reconciliation, privileged retry, disconnect and rebind, the publish CLI and the validator assets in a service image all remain outstanding. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state, namespaced-behaviour or Phase 1 completion claim is made, and the change correctly claimed none.

## 2026-10-28: PR #99 adds administrator bootstrap and deprovisioning (ADR 0053); 0 findings, notes 109, 111 and 112 closed, notes 113 and 114 raised, PASS at `01dcdd7`

One commit `01dcdd7` on accepted main `9a34cb0`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, closed both ends of the administrator lifecycle that the previous unit had left open. Twenty-three files changed, 1,230 insertions and 68 deletions, comprising a server-only stdin bootstrap command, migration 0008 adding lifecycle state and a lifecycle audit table, one-winner bootstrap and atomic deprovisioning in the session store, an authenticated peer-removal route, the operator shell's removal control, the restored root-level OpenAPI tripwire, ADR 0053 and the documentation updates. Local evidence reproduced exactly as declared: root 283 of 283, service unit 371 of 371, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration 7 of 7 with zero skips. One hundred and nineteen independent adversarial assertions were driven against the compiled modules and that real database, and all one hundred and nineteen passed after two defects in the verifier's own probe were corrected.

This is the first unit to pass with a changed `scripts/` tree, so the trust-boundary argument deserves recording rather than assuming. The change is exactly one added file and nothing else, `scripts/openapi-contract.test.mjs`, with no modification or deletion anywhere under `scripts/`, which was confirmed tree against tree rather than commit against commit so that nothing could slip in through the squash. The file cannot reach the hosted run, and that is structural rather than a judgement: the credentialed job executes the trusted harness checked out at the fixed pin, which predates the file, while the reviewed checkout is restricted by sparse checkout to `salesforce` alone, so the pull request's `scripts/` directory is never materialised on the runner at all. The file itself imports only Node built-ins, reads the OpenAPI document and asserts, writing nothing and making no network call. `.github` and `salesforce` were identical to accepted main and the harness pin appeared exactly twice. The handover claimed packages were unchanged; `service/package.json` did gain one line, but it is a new npm script rather than a dependency and no lockfile moved, so the substance of the claim held.

Note 111 is closed, and the new tripwire was proved to bite rather than taken on trust, because a vacuous guard would be worse than none in the very place a guard was just restored. The document and the test were copied into an isolated tree and the document mutated five ways: adding a sensitive field to the attempt summary, adding an eighth operator status, removing a status, renaming the delete operation, and merely mentioning a forbidden column inside the attempts path section. All five made the test fail while the unmutated document passed. The restored guard is stronger than the one deleted in the previous unit, because it anchors field assertions to exact indentation, counts the statuses at exactly seven and checks the summary and path sections separately, and it lives in the repository root suite where the document is available and the service container build context that forced the original deletion does not apply.

Bootstrap is server-only by construction. The module's only export takes an async iterable, and standard input is supplied solely by its private entry point, so there is no HTTP route and no argument-vector tuple; the word bootstrap appears nowhere in the composition root, the provisioning routes, the application factory or the service entry point, and every plausible bootstrap path and every unrouted method on the administrators path returned not-found. Input is bounded at sixteen kilobytes before parsing, decoded as strict text, and must be a plain object carrying exactly five keys. The actor is derived through the same exported helper the login path uses, so a non-HTTPS issuer refuses the whole operation. Refused without leaving a binding, tenant row or audit row behind: oversized input, non-JSON, empty input, an array, a string, null, a missing key, an extra key, a non-string, empty, over-long or control-character subject, a malformed tenant or organization identifier, an insecure authorization origin and a bad namespace prefix.

One winner was proved three ways. Replaying the identical document is refused, a second subject cannot be bootstrapped into a tenant that already holds a binding, and the same actor cannot be bootstrapped into a second tenant. Under twelve concurrent bootstraps of one fresh tenant exactly one succeeded, exactly one binding and exactly one audit row existed afterwards, and every loser reported a refusal rather than a storage error, so the ordered advisory locks and the unique-violation mapping both work rather than leaking a raw driver error. The binding and its audit commit together because they are one statement, and the schema makes a dishonest audit unrepresentable: a bootstrap row whose performer differs from its target is refused by a check constraint, an unknown action is refused, and a cross-tenant row is refused by the composite foreign keys. Those keys were confirmed well founded rather than assumed, since a unique constraint over actor and tenant exists from an earlier migration, so each audit row's performer and target are genuinely pinned to bindings in the same tenant.

Deprovisioning's guarantees hold, and the ones that matter hold in the database rather than in the caller. A removal marks the binding inactive with a timestamp, deletes every session of the target in the same statement and appends the audit, all atomically; migration 0008's check constraint pairs the two columns, and both incoherent combinations were refused when attempted directly in SQL. Revocation is immediate and defended twice over: the removed peer's session was refused on all five protected routes and the peer could not log in again, and separately a binding was marked inactive while its session row was deliberately left intact, after which authentication still refused it because that path now rechecks the active flag. Session issuance refuses an inactive actor as well, giving four independent enforcement points. The tombstone is immutable, since the removed peer could not be re-enrolled into the same tenant, because provisioning's conflict clause now requires an active row, and could not be bootstrapped into another tenant, because bootstrap refuses any actor already bound at all; the row is retained rather than deleted, which preserves the audit and publication foreign keys, and ADR 0053 records deleting bindings as a rejected alternative for exactly that reason.

The refusals are correct and uniform. Self-removal is refused whether or not a peer exists, removing another tenant's administrator is refused, removing the last administrator is refused, and at least one active administrator remained across every sequence driven. An unknown subject is refused with the same fixed message, so removal discloses nothing beyond what the caller already knows about their own tenant. Reciprocal concurrent removals produced exactly one winner, one remaining active administrator and exactly one deprovision audit, with the loser receiving the fixed unavailable message; the mechanism is visible in the statement, where the tenant advisory lock serialises the two, the active bindings are locked for update in deterministic actor order so the pair cannot deadlock, and the loser then fails the requirement that more than one active administrator exist. The route mirrors the enrolment route faithfully, checking origin, then session, then the session-derived token, and only then the query and body; the ordering was proved rather than read, because a hostile body carrying actor and tenant selectors returned an authentication failure with no session and a token failure with a session but no token, so request data is never parsed before the caller is authorised. The performer and tenant come from the live session and the body carries only a subject, with extra actor, tenant, performer or target keys all refused along with malformed subjects, a prototype key, an absent body and a query string, and the intended target stayed active through every refusal.

Note 109 is closed exactly as asked, since ADR 0052 now states that the route exposes no actor or tenant detail and never identifies the other tenant, and adds plainly that a caller can still distinguish successful enrolment from the generic unavailable outcome and that the route does not claim to hide that one bit. Note 112 is answered with a comment naming the real reason that the record guard cannot require an ordinary object prototype. The operator shell gained a removal control that reuses the existing subject field, sends the delete with its token header, clears the field and adds no browser state; because the inline script changed, the property most likely to break silently was re-verified from outside, and the policy's script and style digests still match the hashes of the bytes actually served, with storage, unsafe sinks and unsafe policy directives all still at zero.

Rotation while the administrator is still available works, which was confirmed by having a peer remove the original, so the gap is narrow and specific to the unavailable or compromised sole administrator; it matters because the preceding unit's note established deprovisioning as a security control, and this is the one case where that control cannot be applied, so a sentence in the consequences would let an operator plan for it by enrolling a second administrator at bootstrap time. Note 114 records that SOQL date literals are evaluated in the querying user's timezone, which for this Dev Hub is `America/Los_Angeles`, so a today predicate silently excludes any event between midnight and seven in the morning UTC that belongs to the previous Pacific day, and is not even stable across a single verification: the Pacific day rolled over between two of this review's own queries, so a cleanup audit query returned zero rows and could have been read as a missing audit. Verifications that compare against a run's UTC timestamps should use explicit UTC datetime bounds, and capacity counts should name the Salesforce day they refer to rather than reporting a bare number, since the failure cuts both ways between a false alarm about cleanup and a false zero that misjudges quota. Counting that way, the previous Salesforce day used three of six including this run's org with all three deleted, and the current day stood at none of six created and none of three active. ## 2026-10-28: PR #101 adds tenant administrator roles with live re-scoping (ADR 0054); 0 findings, note 113 answered, PASS at `ddc151e`

One commit `ddc151e` on accepted main `1f73db0`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, replaced the single-role model that every preceding administrator unit had assumed. Thirty-two files changed, 1,424 insertions and 127 deletions, comprising migration 0009 with a closed three-role set and a role audit table, a new centralized capability module, role enforcement added to every privileged route including the still-unmounted Salesforce authorization pair, same-tenant peer role changes with self-change and last-full-administrator protection, explicit enrolment roles, operator-shell role controls, a strengthened contract tripwire, ADR 0054 and the documentation updates. Local evidence reproduced exactly as declared: root 283 of 283, service unit 378 of 378, the containerised target also 378 of 378, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration 7 of 7 with zero skips. One hundred and twenty-three independent adversarial assertions were driven against the compiled modules, that real database and the real application factory, and all one hundred and twenty-three passed after three defects in the verifier's own probe were corrected. This was the unit the verifier recommended in the preceding relay, and every one of the seven properties named there is enforced.

The property the relay said would be pushed hardest was live re-scoping on demotion, and it holds structurally rather than by diligence. Authentication selects the role from the binding on every call, joins the tenant row, requires the session unexpired and the binding active, and passes the value through a closed-set decoder, so a stale role has nowhere to hide. That was confirmed from both ends: the session cookie is an opaque forty-three character token, and the session row holds exactly a digest, actor, expiry and creation timestamp with no role column at all. Driven through the real application with no re-login anywhere, an administrator holding a live session enrolled a peer successfully, was demoted to reviewer by a second administrator, and the same live session was then denied immediately while creating no binding; that demoted session retained exactly its new capability, keeping publication history and losing publish. The reverse was equally immediate, a reviewer who could not publish gaining publish on the same session once promoted. The role was then changed directly in PostgreSQL, bypassing the route entirely, and the very next request honoured it in both directions. ADR 0054's alternatives section rejects placing the role in the token or cookie because an old credential would retain stale authority after demotion, which is the correct reason, and the implementation earns it.

The capability matrix was verified exhaustively rather than sampled. Every route registration in the service was enumerated instead of trusting the summary: there are seven privileged routes and exactly seven capability checks, one each, while three session routes and the static shell are deliberately role-independent because the role gates capabilities rather than authentication, and the two health probes are unauthenticated. All fifteen role-by-route cells behaved as specified, with an administrator allowed everywhere, a publisher denied all three lifecycle operations but allowed publication and history, and a reviewer allowed only history. The publish cell returned an unprocessable-entity status for permitted roles, which is the useful detail, because it proves the capability gate passed and a later check refused rather than an allowed cell quietly failing for an unrelated reason. Every denied cell left storage untouched and each role could still bootstrap a CSRF token. The denial is deliberately indistinguishable from a bad CSRF token, both returning the identical fixed response, and the check is folded into the same condition as the token comparison so it runs before any body parsing.

The still-unmounted Salesforce authorization routes gained the same enforcement, which is the right order, since a check added before mounting cannot be forgotten at mounting time. Both remain unmounted in the real application, returning not-found, so they were registered on a separate hardened instance with stores that throw if touched. A publisher and a reviewer were each denied the authorization start with the fixed response and neither store was reached, while an administrator passed the capability gate and proceeded further. The callback route carries the same requirement, which matters because the callback completes the authorization flow.

No role can be taken from request data. A reviewer attempted elevation through every channel a request offers, namely forged role headers, a role in the query string, a role in the publish body and a role cookie beside the session, and none elevated anything while the stored role stayed unchanged. Self-elevation is structurally impossible, because the role-change statement requires performer and target to differ and requires the performer to hold the full administrator role, so an administrator cannot change their own role at all and no lower role can change anyone's; since the only granter already holds the top role, the rule that nobody may grant a role they lack is satisfied by construction. A context carrying an unknown role cannot even be decoded. The change route's body must be exactly a subject and a role, and a missing role, missing subject, unknown role, extra key, actor key, prototype key or any query string is refused with the target unchanged after every refusal. Enrolment cannot be used to change a role either, since re-enrolling an existing actor at a different role is refused while the same role is idempotent, so role changes must pass through the dedicated operation.

The last full administrator cannot be lost, and the guard is specific rather than blunt. The sole administrator can be neither demoted nor removed; with two administrators one may be demoted, leaving exactly one; and promotion back is still permitted, which was checked deliberately as a positive control proving the guard blocks demotion rather than freezing every role change. One detail deserves particular mention because leaving it alone was the subtle mistake this review was hunting. The deprovisioning guard previously required more than one active binding, which was equivalent to more than one administrator while every binding carried full authority. It now requires more than one administrator when the target is one, so a tenant can no longer be reduced to reviewers only. That condition was correctly strengthened rather than inherited. Concurrency holds on both racing shapes: reciprocal concurrent demotions left one administrator with exactly one winner, and a demotion racing a removal also left one administrator. The losing demotion returned the authorization refusal rather than the storage refusal, which is an emergent consequence of live re-scoping worth recording, because the loser had already been demoted by the time its request was authorised and so failed at the capability gate rather than the database guard.

Migration 0009's backfill is honest and the absence of a default was verified rather than accepted. The column is added nullable, every existing row is set to the administrator role, and the column then becomes not-null with a closed check constraint while no default is left behind, which `information_schema` confirmed by reporting the column not nullable with a null default. An insert omitting the role is therefore refused by the schema, so no future code path can silently create an administrator, and the claim that the backfill leaves no persistent privilege default is accurate. The backfill was also proved to cover pre-existing data rather than only fresh inserts, by applying migrations only through 0008, inserting a legacy binding with no role, then applying 0009 and finding that row an administrator. The role audit table makes a dishonest record unrepresentable, which raw SQL confirmed by refusing an enrolment row carrying a previous role, a change row with no previous role, a change row whose roles are equal, an unknown action, an unknown role value and a cross-tenant row, so every audit row records a real transition and the composite foreign keys pin performer and target to bindings in the same tenant.

The `scripts/` tree changed for the third time, again by exactly one file, and again the change was verified tree against tree so that nothing could enter through the squash. The modification to the contract tripwire is purely additive, now also requiring the patch operation and the three-role enum to appear exactly twice, with no assertion removed. The strengthened assertions were validated the same way the original ones were, because a strengthened guard that does not bite is worse than an honest weak one: four mutations produced four failures, covering removal of the patch operation, dropping a role from the enum, adding a fourth role and renaming a role throughout, while the unmutated document passes. The boundary argument is unchanged and still holds, since the credentialed job executes the harness at the fixed pin which does not contain this file, and the reviewed checkout is restricted by sparse checkout to the Salesforce directory alone, so the pull request's scripts directory is never materialised on the runner.

Note 113 is answered, and in the form the preceding relay asked for, namely a sentence in the consequences rather than new code. ADR 0054 records that the one-shot bootstrap command cannot recover an existing tenant and that deployment and customer onboarding guidance must enrol a second full administrator promptly, stating explicitly that this answers the note operationally without adding an unauthenticated recovery path, and it ties the self-change prohibition to the same concern so that a single full administrator cannot remove their own recovery authority. ADR 0053 is amended to record that its single-role statement is superseded and to enumerate what remains of note 34.

The protected run was required rather than optional, and the classifier again refused the exemption this project added earlier, choosing full Apex because the scripts tree changed even though the Salesforce tree was byte-identical, for the third time. Hosted run 40659312858 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, an eighteenth consecutive unchanged reading, with the trusted policy executing at the reviewed pin in all four credentialed checkouts. Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__40659312858-1__ddc151e29622__0117e32d6d25`, and the Dev Hub was queried with explicit UTC datetime bounds rather than date literals, which is the method note 114 established after the preceding unit tripped over the Pacific day boundary. That returned exactly one org created since nine o'clock, Deleted with a null error code, exactly one matching delete entry at 09:07:26 by the dedicated CI user, and zero active scratch org records, with the current Salesforce day standing at one of six created and none of three active. Across the Apex window there was exactly one login org-wide, the CI user from a hosted runner address, and no verifier authentication appears anywhere in the period because every Dev Hub query in this review addressed a named alias.

The credential search returned zero matches across the usual patterns and was extended to this unit's own vocabulary, since roles are a new kind of identity detail; the publisher and reviewer role names and the role audit table name each returned zero, so no role value or audit detail reached the log, and there were forty-two masked redactions. Two case-insensitive patterns returned a non-zero count and both were accounted for rather than dismissed: the administrator role name matched four times and every match was the commit subject line echoed by `git checkout` rather than a role value, and the authorization pattern matched eight times as the checkout action's own masked git extraheader line. Six forty-character hex strings appeared and all six were mapped to the reviewed head, the harness pin, the checkout and setup-node pins, the unchanged runner image provisioner and accepted main, with no new identifier.

Three defects in the verifier's own probe are disclosed. The most instructive was a design error rather than a typo: the role-by-route matrix operated on another role's principal as its deletion target, so the administrator's pass through the matrix successfully removed the reviewer, and because deprovisioning revokes sessions immediately, five later reviewer assertions failed with an authentication status and briefly resembled a session defect. Rewritten so each iteration owns a disposable target, the matrix is clean. The irony is that the correct behaviour verified in the preceding unit is what broke this probe. The second defect was guessing the OAuth configuration shape and supplying fields the interface does not declare, so registration threw before any assertion ran, which is the standing trap about reading the contract before writing the fake. The third was a muddled assertion labelled as a demotion refusal whose body actually attempted a promotion, which is correctly permitted; it was rewritten as a positive control that promotion back is allowed, which is the more useful property.

It was the only open pull request and all five checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. This merge moves the verification baseline for the third time, so the new accepted values are recorded here: `.github` remains `4a15bb9d7502e75af73fcec73de87c7cdfad4b59`, `salesforce` remains `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d`, and `scripts` becomes `e4d2ae32b659c805ff10bfd0e8886e4413d43fe1`, differing from the previous baseline by exactly the one modified contract test, with the harness pin unchanged and still appearing exactly twice.

Ledger: zero findings, note 113 answered and no new notes. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 104, 105, 106, 107, 108, 110 and 114 remain informational. Note 110's concern is materially reduced rather than closed, because the blast radius of one stolen session now depends on its role, so a compromised publisher or reviewer session can no longer enrol, remove or authorize Salesforce, while a compromised administrator session still can and additions remain irreversible. ## 2026-10-28: PR #103 adds one-time tenant operator invitations (ADR 0055); 0 findings, NOTE 109 CLOSED, notes 115 and 116, PASS at `0268e9b`

One commit `0268e9b` on accepted main `1374f39`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, replaced direct browser enrolment with an invitation the invited party must accept. Twenty-six files changed, 2,379 insertions and 355 deletions, comprising migration 0010 with an invitation table, three composite foreign keys, seven check constraints and an immutability trigger, a new invitation store using 256-bit tokens of which only the digest is stored, administrator-only issue and revoke routes, acceptance folded into the trusted OIDC login path, the removal of the direct enrolment route, narrowed per-adapter production facades, a strengthened contract tripwire, ADR 0055 and the documentation updates. Local evidence: root 283 of 283, service unit 386 of 386, the containerised target also 386 of 386, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and `git diff --check` clean, and disposable PostgreSQL 17.11 integration 8 of 8 with zero skips. Ninety-six independent adversarial assertions were driven against the compiled modules, that real database and the real application factory, and all ninety-six passed after two defects in the verifier's own probe were corrected. This was the unit the verifier recommended, and it closes the note it was recommended to close.

Note 109 is closed, and the closure was measured rather than inferred. The note recorded that direct enrolment returned success for a fresh subject and a generic unavailable response for a subject already bound in another tenant, giving the inviting administrator one bit about existence elsewhere. Issuing an invitation for a brand-new subject and for a subject bound in a second tenant now both return the same created status, so the two are indistinguishable to the inviter. The refusal has moved to acceptance, which only the invited subject can attempt, and that subject learns only that their own acceptance failed, which concerns their own tenancy rather than anyone else's. An administrator who observes that an invitation was never accepted cannot distinguish a subject bound elsewhere from a person who never acted. The oracle is therefore removed by design rather than by wording, which is what the preceding relay asked for.

The token is never at rest. It is a 256-bit value returned exactly once, and only its SHA-256 hexadecimal digest is persisted, which was confirmed by dumping every table in the schema and finding the raw token in none of them while the stored digest matched the issued token exactly. The issue response carries exactly three documented fields with a no-store header and discloses no tenant, actor or role. An unaccepted invitation confers nothing: no binding, no session, and the invited subject cannot log in without the token.

Acceptance was tested end to end through the real application rather than at the store, because that is where a particular class of bug would hide. The invitation store computes its own session digest and CSRF derivation rather than importing the session store's, so the two must agree or a session created by acceptance would be unusable. The agreement was verified behaviourally: the session returned by acceptance authenticates on a protected route and the CSRF token returned alongside it is accepted on a protected route. The binding carries the invitation's stored role rather than anything from the request, which was confirmed by accepting a reviewer invitation while asking for the administrator role in the body and finding the stored role to be reviewer. Consumption, the binding, the enrolment audit and the first session all land together because they are one statement.

Every lifecycle case the relay named is refused with the invitation left unrewritten. Replay of a consumed invitation is refused and creates no second binding or audit. A different subject presenting the token is refused, the invitation stays pending, no binding is made for the impostor, and the intended subject can still accept afterwards; that property is enforced twice, in the lookup's target predicate and in the database's terminal-state constraint requiring the recorded acceptor to equal the target. A random token is refused. An expired invitation is refused and creates no binding. A revoked invitation cannot be accepted, and revocation updates the row rather than deleting it, recording the revoker so the trail survives. Eight concurrent acceptances produced exactly one winner, exactly one binding, exactly one enrolment audit and exactly one session. Revocation is also deliberately non-disclosing in a way worth recording approvingly: revoking an unknown identifier returns the same success as revoking a real one, and revoking an already accepted invitation changes nothing, so an administrator cannot probe which identifiers exist.

Authority is correct and one property exceeded what the relay asked for. Only an administrator may issue or revoke, with a publisher and a reviewer each refused on both. An actor already bound in another tenant cannot accept and its original binding is untouched. Beyond that, an invitation does not outlive its issuer's authority, because acceptance joins the issuing administrator's binding and requires it to be still active and still an administrator. That was tested by issuing an invitation, promoting a peer, having the peer demote the issuer and then attempting acceptance, which was refused with no binding created and the invitation left pending. This matters because revoking an administrator now implicitly neutralises the invitations they left behind, closing a route by which authority could otherwise leak past a removal.

The migration puts the invariants where they cannot be bypassed, and raw SQL confirmed it. A before-update trigger refused every attempt to rewrite the invitation identifier, token digest, tenant, issuer, target, role, issue time and expiry, and refused to rewrite a terminal state once recorded. The check constraints made unrepresentable an acceptor who is not the target, acceptance after expiry, a revocation with no revoker, an unknown role, an expiry before issue, a cross-tenant issuer, a non-hexadecimal token digest, and a row that is both accepted and revoked. Three composite foreign keys pin issuer, acceptor and revoker to bindings in the same tenant.

The removed route is gone and unreachable rather than merely unrouted. The enrolment method on the administrators path returns not-found and creates nothing while the role change and deprovisioning methods still work. Composition now builds four separate frozen facades, each bound only to the methods its adapter needs, and the legacy provisioning helper appears in none of them; that the surface no longer needs it at all was proved by mounting with a session store omitting the helper entirely, which succeeded. Removing the invitation store or its configuration refuses the whole surface, and the operator shell never posts to the administrators path. The helper remains on the class for tests, which the handover declares and which is acceptable precisely because no mounted adapter can reach it.

Acceptance accepts no selector. The login body must be exactly the invitation token, and a tenant selector, an actor selector, a role selector, a missing token, a non-string token, a short token and a prototype key are each refused with the invitation left pending, after which a clean acceptance succeeds, proving the refusals concerned the selectors rather than the token. Every invitation failure returns the identical fixed authentication message, disclosing no tenant, actor or role.

The `scripts/` tree changed for the fourth time, and this time the handover did not declare it, which became note 115. In substance the change is one file and good: the contract tripwire now actively asserts that the removed enrolment method stays removed, reduces the role-enum count on that path, and adds assertions for the invitation path, its three response fields and the login body's required token, each with its own non-disclosure check, with no assertion removed. The new assertions were validated by breaking the document four ways, covering restoration of an enrolment method, renaming the invitation method, renaming the token field and relaxing the login body's required list; all four failed the test while the unmutated document passes, so the route removal is locked in by an automated guard. The boundary argument is unchanged, since the credentialed job executes the harness at the fixed pin which does not contain this file and the reviewed checkout is restricted by sparse checkout to the Salesforce directory alone.

The declared integration figure had to be reproduced rather than accepted at first reading. The suite initially returned seven of eight, with the administrator-session test failing on a provisioning conflict from bootstrap. That was the verifier's own residue, because the probe had left bindings in the same database and bootstrap correctly refuses a tenant that already holds one. On a clean PostgreSQL 17.11 container the suite passes eight of eight with zero skips, including the new invitation test, so the builder's figure is accurate. Checking the environment before the code resolved it in one step.

The protected run was required, and the classifier again refused the exemption this project added earlier, choosing full Apex because the scripts tree changed even though the Salesforce tree was byte-identical, for the fourth time. Hosted run 40684072381 attempt 1 passed at the exact head with no retry consumed and reported `101 passed; 1428/1473 executable lines (96.95%)`, a nineteenth consecutive unchanged reading, with the trusted policy executing at the reviewed pin. Cleanup deleted the one owned org tagged `kusanya-ci-v1__40684072381-1__0268e9b9fa06__e857cf6b7be8`, which the Dev Hub confirmed with explicit UTC bounds as created at 13:25:00, Deleted with a null error code, with one matching delete entry at 13:26:15 by the dedicated CI user and zero active scratch org records. The credential search returned zero matches across the usual patterns and, because this unit introduces a bearer-style token, specifically across the invitation vocabulary of token field, identifier, digest column and configuration variable, all of which returned zero; a search for anything shaped like the token, a forty-three character base64url word, returned only a GitHub deprecation notice slug. Twenty-one masked redactions were present and the single non-zero case-insensitive pattern was the checkout action's own masked extraheader line.

Two observations about run evidence are recorded for future readers because both initially resembled anomalies. Exactly one login occurred org-wide inside the Apex window, the CI user from a hosted runner address, but a third login appeared timestamped nearly five hours after the run finished, which looked future-dated. It was not: the current time was later still, and that entry was the verifier's own Dev Hub query thirty-six seconds old, the stale impression arising because the approval wait had been long. The lesson, which extends note 114 rather than adding a note, is to establish the current time explicitly before attributing logins. Separately, every forty-character hex count came back at roughly half its usual value, which is also not an anomaly: this run's archive contained only the three combined job logs rather than the per-step files, so each line appears once instead of twice. The set of six identifiers was identical to previous runs, and a future reader should compare the set rather than the multiplicities, since those depend on which files the archive happens to contain.

Two defects in the verifier's own probe are disclosed. The instructive one was an attempt to test expiry by rewriting the expiry column on a live invitation, which the immutability trigger refused, stopping the probe with an unhandled error. That is the guard working, and it is stronger than the method assumed, since not even direct SQL can extend an invitation; the test was rebuilt to insert an already-expired row at creation time, which satisfies the expiry-after-issue constraint while still being past, and an explicit assertion that direct SQL cannot extend an expiry was added. The second was ordinary, a session variable scoped to one block and used in three later ones.

It was the only open pull request and all five checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. This merge moves the verification baseline for the fourth time, so the new accepted values are recorded here: `.github` remains `4a15bb9d7502e75af73fcec73de87c7cdfad4b59`, `salesforce` remains `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d`, and `scripts` becomes `a2412e3faf42bb48391a882718e3d770ec6328c4`, differing from the previous baseline by exactly the one modified contract test, with the harness pin unchanged and still appearing exactly twice.
