Ledger: zero findings, note 109 closed and two informational notes. Note 115 records that the handover did not declare the `scripts/` change and stated that no verification-harness change was made; the latter is literally true because the contract tripwire is not the harness, but the scripts tree is one of the three trust-boundary trees this review compares every unit and it is the tree whose change forces the protected run, and the two preceding units both declared theirs explicitly. The substance was good and the change improves the guard; the ask is only that any change under the workflow, scripts or Salesforce trees be named in the handover, because the protocol treats the declaration as the thing to check the repository against and an undeclared boundary change inverts that. Note 116 records that the invitation store duplicates the session store's digest and CSRF helpers rather than sharing them; both currently produce identical output, verified behaviourally by using the returned session and token on protected routes, so there is no defect today, but the correctness of acceptance rests on three literals matching across two files, namely hexadecimal encoding, the HMAC domain separator and base64url output, with nothing enforcing the match and a quiet failure mode if they diverge, so a shared helper or an equality test would pin it cheaply. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 104, 105, 106, 107, 108, 110, 114, 115 and 116 remain informational. Note 110 is further reduced because enrolment now requires the invited party to act, so a stolen administrator session can create pending invitations but cannot by itself add an administrator, and those pending invitations die with the stolen actor's authority; the handover states this accurately as reduced rather than closed. Note 34 is advanced again and still not closed, the invitation strand being closed and the remaining strands being invitation delivery and listing, attempt retention and deletion, scheduled reconciliation, privileged retry, disconnect and rebind, the publish CLI and the ODK Validate assets in the service runtime image. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state, namespaced-behaviour or Phase 1 completion claim is made, and the change correctly claimed none.

## 2026-10-28: PR #105 adds audited publication-attempt retention (ADR 0056); FINDING 1 raised and CLOSED, notes 117 to 120, HOLD at `3181cc8` then PASS at `fd34c44`

This is the first unit in the project's history where no text was relayed by hand between builder and verifier, and it is recorded first because it changes how every later entry should be read. Immediately before this unit the verifier wrote and the owner merged PR #106 as `19583f7`, a twenty-two line change to `docs/verification.md` making the pull request the transport: the builder writes its verification request into the pull request body, the verifier reads it there and replies in pull request comments, and the owner's whole instruction to the verifier becomes a pull request number. The verifier authored that change and therefore did not independently verify it, which is stated plainly here because the log is the record; it was posted for the builder to review, and the builder's agreement is recorded in this unit's behaviour rather than in a separate verdict. Two actions were deliberately excluded and remain with the owner: approving a protected Salesforce run, which is the only human gate on credentialed execution against the Dev Hub, and merging. The change also states that neither agent may approve a run, merge, or run the verifier inside the pipeline it verifies. PR #106 took the trusted Salesforce-identical exemption, because `docs/verification.md` is one of the four critical documents excluded from the ordinary-documentation exemption and so cannot earn the cheap path, and because the Salesforce tree was byte-identical to accepted main.

The protocol worked on its first use, and that is recorded as evidence rather than intention. The builder placed a complete re-verification request in the pull request body, correctly omitting the pull request number, title, branch and head commit, which the verifier took from the pull request itself; a head sha written into prose is stale the moment the builder pushes again, which is exactly what happened here. The body carried the trust-boundary declaration the new protocol requires, and that declaration was accurate in every line, which took one command to confirm rather than a conversation. The verdict for both the hold and the pass was posted as a pull request comment and is the artefact of record.

The unit itself is an operational retention boundary: a tenant-configurable retention window between thirty and three thousand six hundred and fifty days defaulting to ninety, a selector-free administrator route, a store operation bound to one transaction and capped at one hundred attempts, and migration 0011, which places eligibility, age, tenant scope and actor authority in a before-delete trigger on the attempts table with a surviving per-attempt audit row. Eighteen files changed, 1,307 insertions and 25 deletions, across migration 0011, the store operation, the new route, the surface and operator-interface mounting, a dedicated publication-retention capability, ADR 0056, six documentation files and five test files. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state, namespaced-behaviour or Phase 1 completion claim was made, and the change correctly claimed none.

The first review, at head `3181cc8`, returned a hold with one finding out of 128 assertions, of which 124 passed. The design was the strongest reviewed to that point, and the finding was worth holding precisely because the trigger is the thing the unit's whole claim rests on. The eligibility expression appeared twice, once in the trigger guard and once in the audit check, written as a disjunction permitting a succeeded or failed attempt with no reconciliation status, or a partial attempt whose status is committed or artifacts missing. When the outcome is partial and the reconciliation status is null, the second disjunct is null rather than false, because a null compared against a membership list yields null. Both guards then failed open, for different reasons. The trigger's guard raises when the negation of eligibility holds, and the negation of null is null, which does not satisfy a conditional, so the deletion proceeded; seeding a genuine partial attempt through the store's own completion path, ageing it four hundred days and deleting it as a legitimate active administrator of its own tenant removed the row. The audit check is violated only by false, and null passes, so the trigger's own insert succeeded and the audit table ended up holding the exact shape that check exists to forbid, namely a partial outcome with a null reconciliation status. Exactly one of the eight reachable states was affected, established by driving all eight through the trigger. The route was never a vector, because its candidate selection expresses eligibility in a filter clause and a filter clause discards null, which is also why the finding was reachable only by paths other than the route, and is the reason for putting the policy in a trigger in the first place.

The fix is in two parts and both were measured. The substantive part adds an explicit non-null test ahead of the membership test in both the audit check and the trigger guard, which removes the three-valued result without relying on short-circuit evaluation: when the status is null the non-null test is false, and false conjoined with null is false in SQL whichever operand the planner evaluates first, so the branch cannot produce null regardless of evaluation order. The second part wraps the trigger's whole disjunction in a true test, which converts any residual null to false and therefore makes the guard raise. Measured in isolation, the only inputs that still produce a null before wrapping are those with a null outcome, which the attempts table forbids today because the column is not-null with a value check, so this part is defence in depth against a future schema change rather than a live necessity.

Re-verification at head `fd34c44` returned a pass with zero findings and 88 of 88 independent adversarial assertions passing. All eight reachable states were driven through the raw trigger as a legitimate active administrator of the attempt's own tenant at four hundred days of age: started, indeterminate, unreconciled partial and unlinked are each refused with a check violation and the row survives, while succeeded, failed, partial committed and partial artifacts missing are each deleted. The unreconciled partial row that was deleted at the earlier head is now refused, which is the finding closed at the site where it was demonstrated. The audit check was tested directly against eleven shapes and accepts only the four intended ones, rejecting the partial with null status it previously accepted, along with partial unlinked, partial indeterminate, succeeded committed, failed unlinked, started with null, and a null outcome, which is refused by the not-null column before the check is reached.

The most important check was whether the test shipped alongside the fix actually catches the regression, because a test added with a fix can pass for reasons unrelated to it. Reverting both parts of the fix, rebuilding and running the integration suite against a clean PostgreSQL 17 produced eight passed and one failed, with the retention test failing on a missing expected rejection where a check violation was expected, at the precise assertion that exercises the unreconciled partial. So the guard is pinned. A narrower mutation was then run separately and is the origin of note 117: removing only the true wrapper, keeping the non-null tests, left all nine integration tests passing, so that half of the fix is guarded by nothing.

The surrounding boundary was re-exercised rather than assumed to be unaffected by a local fix. Actor authority at the trigger refuses an unset actor setting, an empty actor, a publisher of the tenant, a reviewer of the tenant, a deprovisioned administrator, an administrator of another tenant and an unknown actor, and refuses a two hundred character actor at a string-length violation before the guard is reached; a second active administrator of the same tenant is permitted, so the authority is tenant-level by design rather than actor-ownership. Tenant scoping holds in both directions. The age boundary at the default window refuses one day and eighty-nine days and permits ninety-one and four hundred. The tenant window rejects twenty-nine and three thousand six hundred and fifty-one and accepts thirty and three thousand six hundred and fifty, and it is live policy rather than a value captured at insert, proved by widening a tenant to the maximum so that a four-hundred-day-old attempt became protected and then narrowing it so the same row became deletable. The surviving audit row records the truth: its terminal timestamp equals the coalesce of reconciliation and completion times to the microsecond, its retention days equal the tenant's current value, and its performing actor is the administrator who deleted the row rather than the actor who published the attempt. Atomicity holds, with a pre-existing audit row for the same tenant and attempt making the deletion fail on the unique constraint while the attempt survives and no duplicate audit row is left behind. The actor setting is transaction-local, confirmed by setting it in one transaction, committing, and finding a later transaction on the same pooled connection refused. On the route path, with only protected rows old enough the operation deletes nothing and all four protected states survive; with one hundred and five eligible rows the first pass deletes exactly one hundred with the row count falling by exactly one hundred measured immediately before the call, the second deletes the remaining five and the third deletes nothing, with an audit row surviving every deletion; and the store refuses a publisher context and a reviewer context, and deletes nothing for an administrator-role context carrying a mismatched tenant and organisation while leaving an eligible row in place.

The trust boundary was re-read because accepted main had moved twice in the same hour. Against accepted main `19583f7`, the workflow tree remains `4a15bb9d7502e75af73fcec73de87c7cdfad4b59`, the scripts tree remains `a2412e3faf42bb48391a882718e3d770ec6328c4` and the Salesforce tree remains `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d`, so all three trust-boundary trees are byte-identical to accepted main and the baseline does not move with this unit. The harness pin is unchanged and appears exactly twice in the verification workflow, at lines 54 and 162. No manifest, lockfile, permission set, profile or namespace change. Because the reviewed head is a merge commit, what the merge carried was checked separately: its parents are the branch commit and accepted main, and its diff against the branch commit is exactly PR #106's twenty-two line document change and nothing else, so the complete change since the first review is three files, namely the migration fix, the integration test that pins it, and that document. The threat model is one of the four critical documents, so it was checked mechanically rather than by eye: normalising table padding, all thirteen existing rows survive byte-identical and exactly one row is added, for erasure of uncertain or cross-tenant publication evidence, and no control was weakened or removed.

Run evidence was bound to the exact head rather than taken from the citation in the body. Every check run at `fd34c44` is green, the three required contexts pass, and the scratch org job is skipped holding zero steps. The gate printed that Apex did not run because the trusted Salesforce tree `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` is identical to accepted main `19583f7bcb336de14ef9166b07eef49f003b1e73` at reviewed head `fd34c44d0d295cefcb9dcaa226521ce139623e79`, and that this is not phase-gate or C10 Apex evidence. This is the third live firing of the ADR 0051 Salesforce-identical path, and the ADR 0030 supersede workflow also fired, cancelling a superseded zero-step run. Seven distinct forty-character hexadecimal identifiers appear in the gate logs and all seven are accounted for: the reviewed head, accepted main, the Salesforce tree, the harness pin, the two pinned action commits, and the hosted runner image build commit `21505ad2541d5be6135ed84fc33697146cf75b8e`, which is not a git object in this repository and is printed by the runner image provisioner in every job's setup. The verifier first guessed that identifier was the ephemeral pull request merge reference, which was wrong, and this baseline had already recorded runner image provisioner commits as an expected class, so it was a re-derivation rather than a discovery. Credential hygiene returned zero matches across both runs for force URLs, refresh tokens, access tokens and private key headers. The Dev Hub was queried with explicit UTC bounds per note 114 rather than inferring absence from the skip: five scratch orgs exist with a creation date on or after the twenty-seventh, all with deleted status, the most recent created at 13:25:00 while the gate ran at 20:01:36, and zero records are active.

Local evidence: service unit 393 of 393, disposable PostgreSQL 17 integration 9 of 9 with zero skips, root 283 of 283, and lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and whitespace checks all clean. Every figure the builder declared holds. One difference from the hosted run is recorded rather than glossed: the hosted root suite reports two hundred and eighty passed with three skipped where the local run reports two hundred and eighty-three passed with none, because those three tests are guarded as requiring the real Windows command interpreter and skip on a Linux runner. The local run therefore covered three assertions the hosted run cannot, and the hosted run covered nothing the local run did not, so the two are complementary rather than revealing a gap.

One defect in the verifier's own probe is disclosed, and it is the third of its kind in three units, so it is recorded as a habit rather than an accident. The probe's rollback section deliberately plants a colliding audit row to prove that a collision rolls the whole deletion back, and the section left that row in place; the next section's batch then selected the poisoned attempt, hit the collision and threw, which on first reading resembled a route defect. A different section had also left rows that were legitimately eligible, which made a hardcoded expectation wrong for a second reason. The section was rebuilt to drain and measure the live state immediately before any batch assertion rather than assuming what earlier sections left behind, and everything was re-run on a fresh container. Diagnosing it rather than relaxing the assertion produced note 119.

`PASS: PR #105 merged as 86e3362`, with squash tree `2e5ec52` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 20:22. All checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. The baseline does not move with this unit, because no trust-boundary tree changed.

Ledger: one finding raised and closed, and four informational notes. Note 117 records that the true wrapper in the trigger guard is pinned by no test, demonstrated by removing only the wrapper and finding all nine integration tests still passing; the wrapper is correct and should be kept, but it is unreachable today only because the attempts table's outcome column is not-null with a value check, so if that column ever became nullable the wrapper would become load-bearing with nothing to notice its removal. Note 118 records that eligibility is now written in three places with two different null strategies, the audit check and the trigger guard using an explicit non-null test while the route's candidate selection relies on a filter clause discarding null, which is sound and is why the route was never a vector; all three agree today and nothing pins them to each other, which is the same shape as note 116. Note 119 records that the retention batch is all-or-nothing, so a single attempt whose audit row already exists would roll back the whole hundred-row statement and wedge retention for that tenant until the colliding row were removed; reachability was then checked rather than assumed, and the attempt identifier is generated inside the store with a random identifier and is never caller-supplied, so the state cannot arise through the application, the note being recorded because the blast radius is the tenant rather than the row. Note 120 records that a truncation by the database owner bypasses the row trigger entirely and writes no audit, measured by removing a protected started attempt with no audit row created; this sits inside the gap the builder declared, that append-only behaviour is a write-path guarantee rather than tamper evidence against a privileged database owner, and is recorded as a measurement of that boundary rather than as a finding. Note 39 remains open. Notes 75, 80, 81, 82, 84, 86, 104 to 108, 110, 114 to 120 remain informational. Note 34 is advanced again and still not closed, the retention and deletion strand now being closed and the remaining strands being invitation delivery and listing, scheduled reconciliation, privileged retry, disconnect and rebind, the publish CLI and the ODK Validate assets in the service runtime image.

## 2026-10-28: PR #109 fixes the concurrent administrator bootstrap race (issue #108); 0 findings, notes 121 and 122, PASS at `19fc5d0`

This entry begins with how the defect was found, because the route to it is the most useful thing in it. PR #107, the verification log for PR #105, changed one documentation file by thirty lines and its required `Service and container tests` check failed on run 40763381496 with `2 !== 1`. The assertion was the one requiring exactly one winner when two actors bootstrap the same tenant concurrently. Two had won. A documentation-only change cannot cause that, so the failure was a pre-existing defect in accepted `main` that the test had never fired on before.

The defect was in `AdministratorSessionStore.bootstrap`, introduced with ADR 0053 in PR #99 and verified by this verifier at the time without the race ever occurring. One statement took transaction-scoped advisory locks on the tenant and the actor in materialised common table expressions, then in the same statement read `NOT EXISTS` over the bindings for that tenant or actor. **The lock cannot protect that read.** The query runs on the pool with no explicit transaction, so it is a single implicit transaction in `READ COMMITTED`, and PostgreSQL fixes the statement snapshot when the statement begins executing, which is before the common table expression acquires the lock. The second caller therefore blocks on the lock and, on resuming, evaluates its guard against a snapshot that predates the winner's commit. It sees no binding and inserts one.

That mechanism was measured rather than reasoned. Session A held an advisory lock while session B issued a single statement that takes the same lock and then reads, exactly the shape `bootstrap` used. B blocked. A then inserted the row and committed. When B resumed, the read inside that same statement saw zero rows. The same shape with the lock in its own statement saw one row, which is what an explicit transaction would give, because `READ COMMITTED` takes a new snapshot per statement.

Whether the race occurs depends entirely on whether the connection pool already holds a spare live connection, and that dependency runs the wrong way for safety. With one live connection the second caller must open another, which takes long enough that the first has committed, and the race never fires: zero of twenty-five rounds produced two winners. With two or more live connections both statements start together and **both succeed in twenty-five of twenty-five rounds**. The integration suite runs against a nearly cold pool, and the real suite was run eight times on clean containers for this review, passing nine of nine every time. A long-running service always has a warm pool. So the condition under which the test passes is the condition that does not occur in production, and the defect had been latent and invisible since PR #99.

What a lost race leaves behind is two self-bootstrapped administrators in a tenant documented to admit exactly one, each having written its own `bootstrap` lifecycle audit naming itself as both performer and target, neither invited by the other, which is the gate PR #103 added for every administrator after the first. Nothing in the schema caught it. The bindings table keys on the actor and uniquely constrains the actor and tenant pair, and correctly has no unique constraint on the tenant alone, because a tenant now holds many bindings. The `NOT EXISTS` read was the only enforcement and there was no second line of defence.

Severity was assessed as should fix rather than blocker, and the reason is reachability. `bootstrap` has exactly one caller in the whole service, the server-only one-shot command documented in the service runbook, which reads its input tuple from standard input. It is reachable from no HTTP route, so this was never remotely triggerable and never an authentication bypass. It needs an operator to run that command twice concurrently, which a retrying wrapper or an impatient second invocation could do. The reasons to fix it anyway were that the invariant is load-bearing for the invitation model, and that a required check had begun failing intermittently on every pull request, which trains a team to re-run checks instead of reading them. The verifier recorded the whole analysis, with a fix direction it had proved, as issue #108, and explicitly declined to ask for a re-run of PR #107 to obtain a green check.

The fix in PR #109 is one commit, eight files, 234 insertions and 28 deletions. Migration `0012_bootstrap_uniqueness` adds a partial unique index permitting one `bootstrap` audit per tenant, and `bootstrap` drops the advisory locks rather than leaving misleading code in place. A losing concurrent statement now fails on the index with SQLSTATE `23505`, which `bootstrap` already mapped to its fixed conflict, so no error-handling change was needed. Because every common table expression is in one statement, the losing statement's tenant and binding writes roll back with its audit insert, so an index on the audit table transitively protects the bindings table that has no such constraint. The migration first refuses to run at all if any tenant already holds more than one historical `bootstrap` audit, failing closed without deleting, rewriting or choosing between security audit records, and the service runbook gains the preflight query an operator must run before deploying it.

The review ran every race on a pool with a stated number of connections already established, since that is the condition under which the defect appears. Bootstrap against one tenant with two, three, five and ten competing actors each gave exactly one winner, one binding and one `bootstrap` audit across twelve rounds per width, against two winners in twenty-five of twenty-five before the fix. The only refusal code observed at any width was the fixed conflict, with no storage error, so the index introduces neither deadlocks nor spurious failures even at ten-way contention. The removed actor lock was also not load-bearing: one actor bootstrapping two different tenants concurrently gave exactly one winner and one binding across fifteen warm rounds, held by the primary key on the actor.

The most important question was whether the defect generalised, because the same advisory-lock pattern appears at ten other sites across the session and invitation stores. It does not, and the reason is worth recording because it is not the locks. `deprovision` and `changeRole` read with `SELECT ... FOR UPDATE`, and in `READ COMMITTED` PostgreSQL re-evaluates a locked row after the lock wait, so those reads do see the winner's commit. `provision` is backed by the primary key on the actor. Invitation acceptance is backed by the invitation row's terminal-state check constraint. **`bootstrap` was the one invariant whose write had neither a constraint nor a row lock behind it**, which is precisely why it was the one that broke. The advisory locks at the remaining sites are redundant rather than wrong.

That conclusion was not taken on reasoning either. All four of those invariants were re-run warm: one actor provisioned into two tenants at once, two administrators cross-removed at once, both administrators demoted at once, and eight concurrent invitation acceptances. Every one holds, so no conclusion from PRs #99, #101 or #103 changes. This is note 122, recorded because those earlier verifications did not control pool warmth and so may never have raced.

Three independent mutations established that the new tests are not vacuous, and one of them found something. Changing the index from unique to non-unique makes the race test fail with `2 !== 1`, confirming the builder's own mutation evidence. Reintroducing an advisory lock into `bootstrap` makes the unit tripwire fail, so the assertion that the locks stay removed bites. But removing the migration's duplicate-refusal block entirely, while keeping the unique index, leaves **all ten integration tests and all three hundred and ninety-three unit tests passing**, which is note 121.

Migration 0012 was exercised against a database damaged on purpose rather than taken on trust. Stopping the migration at 0011, seeding two `bootstrap` audits for one tenant and then migrating produced a `MigrationError`; both audit rows survived byte for byte including identifiers and timestamps rather than merely in count; 0012 did not mark itself applied, leaving eleven migration rows; and the index was not created. After removing the duplicate as an operator would, the migration applied and the index existed. The builder's own version of this test isolates better than the verifier's did, running the whole scenario in a throwaway schema through `search_path` and dropping it afterwards.

The index is correctly scoped, which was checked rather than assumed. Exactly one place in the service writes a `bootstrap` audit, inside `bootstrap` itself, so the index cannot wrongly block another path. Three `deprovision` audits coexist in one tenant, confirming the predicate is partial. The definition read back from the catalogue is unique and predicated on the bootstrap action. A tenant whose founder has been deprovisioned still refuses a fresh bootstrap with the fixed conflict, so no previous behaviour changed.

The trust boundary did not move. Against accepted main `86e3362`, the workflow, scripts and Salesforce trees are all byte-identical, the harness pin appears exactly twice, and there is no manifest, lockfile, permission set, profile or namespace change. The declaration in the pull request body is accurate in every line. The threat model is one of the four critical documents, so it was checked mechanically: all fourteen table rows are byte-identical with none added or removed, and the change is prose only, recording that ADR 0053 now enforces one bootstrap per tenant with a partial unique audit index.

The ADR 0053 amendment deserves specific credit, because it is the artefact most able to carry a wrong claim forward. It states plainly that the original advisory-lock serialisation was not a concurrency boundary, explains the snapshot mechanism that defeated it, and records that the index supersedes that lock discipline and remains enforced if the query is later reorganised. An ADR that quietly rewrote its own reasoning would have been worse than the defect.

One claim could not be seen from the repository and was therefore verified directly: the pull request and the ADR state that a read-only inventory of `cobitech-edge` found no running or stopped Kusanya containers, so there was no deployed database to query for historical damage. A read-only container listing on that host returned fifteen containers, none of them Kusanya. Nothing was touched. The claim is accurate, and the runbook's mandatory preflight for any database introduced or restored later is the right conclusion from it.

Run evidence was bound to the exact head. Every check run at `19fc5d0` is green, all three required contexts pass, and the scratch org job is skipped holding zero steps. The gate printed that Apex did not run because the trusted Salesforce tree `878014c43b675f457148da406c33b72fc377d05d` is identical to accepted main `86e336251954c23f656363fa790b3f91328bb1aa` at reviewed head `19fc5d0e418ce4515829cd8b777d8de82f057cdc`, and that this is not phase-gate or C10 Apex evidence. This is the fourth live firing of the ADR 0051 Salesforce-identical path. Seven distinct forty-character hexadecimal identifiers appear and all are accounted for: the reviewed head, accepted main, the Salesforce tree, the harness pin, the two pinned action commits, and the hosted runner image build commit, which is not a git object in this repository. Credential hygiene returned zero matches. The Dev Hub, queried with explicit UTC bounds, holds no scratch org created since 13:30 while the gate ran at 20:47, so nothing was created by this run. That the required check which failed on PR #107 passes here is itself part of the evidence.

Local evidence: service unit 393 of 393, disposable PostgreSQL 17 integration 10 of 10 with zero skips, root 283 of 283, and lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and whitespace checks all clean. Every figure the builder declared holds. The hosted root suite again reports two hundred and eighty passed with three skipped against the local two hundred and eighty-three with none, which is the three tests guarded as requiring the real Windows command interpreter.

Two defects in the verifier's own work are disclosed. While building the issue #108 reproduction, a Salesforce org stem was widened from fifteen to eighteen characters to satisfy a check constraint tripped in a hand-written insert. An eighteen-character Salesforce identifier carries a checksum suffix, so every randomly generated one fails input validation, and the experiment silently became vacuous: every call returned an input refusal and the winner count was always zero, which initially read as the defect having disappeared. The fifteen-character stem had been correct all along because the store normalises it, so the fix was the error. Printing the rejection reason instead of interpreting the zero resolved it. Separately, the first reproduction left a deliberately planted colliding audit row in place, which wedged a later section, the same residue habit disclosed in the PR #105 entry.

`PASS: PR #109 merged as fa61989`, with squash tree `29c3e829` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 21:05. All checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. The baseline does not move with this unit, because no trust-boundary tree changed.

Ledger: zero findings and two informational notes. Note 121 records that the migration's duplicate-refusal block is pinned by no test and that its message cannot reach an operator. Removing the block leaves every test passing, because the unique index build fails on a duplicated key anyway, the migration transaction rolls back, and all three observable assertions still hold; and separately `applyMigrations` catches every error and throws a fixed migration failure with no cause, so the wording about duplicate bootstrap audits requiring operator review is discarded before anyone sees it. The block is worth keeping, because it fails earlier and more cheaply than an index build and states the intent in SQL where the next reader of the migration will find it, and the runbook preflight is the real operator path; but the diagnostic value its wording implies is not delivered, so it should either be pinned by a test distinguishing the two SQLSTATEs or accepted as unpinned intent. Note 122 records that the verifier's own earlier concurrency verifications did not control pool warmth, so the concurrency assertions reported as passing in PRs #99, #101 and #103 may not have raced at all; all four have now been re-run warm and every one holds, so no conclusion changes, but earlier log entries should be read with that caveat and controlling pool warmth is now standard for any concurrency claim. The builder's own test is stricter than the verifier's probe was, asserting two distinct backend process identifiers before racing, which turns the precondition into a verified fact rather than a hope, and the verifier has adopted it. Note 39 remains open. Notes 34, 39, 117, 118, 119, 120, 121 and 122 remain informational or open, with notes 118, 119 and 120 unaffected by this change. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state, namespaced-behaviour or Phase 1 completion claim is made, and the change correctly claimed none.

## 2026-10-29: PR #111 adds the tenant-bound publish CLI (ADR 0057); 0 findings, notes 123 to 125, PASS at `cb7b61f`

One commit `cb7b61f` on accepted main `bb9549c`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, adds the command-line and continuous-integration publication boundary required by brief C3 defect 16 and the Phase 1 CLI deliverable in C11. Eight files changed, 1,370 insertions and 21 deletions, comprising a 699-line bounded standard-input client of the already-mounted login, CSRF, durable publication and logout routes, a 466-line unit suite of eleven CLI-focused cases, ADR 0057, and architecture, roadmap, threat-model and service runbook changes. The unit deliberately claims no C10 acceptance test and no Phase 1 completion. Local evidence: service unit 404 of 404, root 283 of 283, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and whitespace clean, every figure the handover declared.

The design decision that makes the unit small is that the CLI is a client of the existing HTTPS composition rather than a second path into the data. Its entire input is one exact JSON document on standard input carrying only the canonical HTTPS service origin, one short-lived OIDC access token for the Kusanya API, and one Salesforce Form Version ID. There is no tenant, organisation, namespace, refresh token or database input, so the service remains the only component that resolves the actor, current role, tenant binding, namespace and encrypted Salesforce connection. The CLI imports the route path constants from the server modules rather than restating them, which means those cannot drift. ADR 0057 records why an argument, a URL and a dedicated environment variable were each rejected in favour of standard input, and why an automatic retry on an uncertain publication was rejected outright.

The check this unit most needed was not in the pull request, and supplying it was the main work of the review. Every test the builder wrote drives the CLI through an injected transport. That proves the logic but cannot prove the strict parser accepts what the real server actually sends, and a client of this kind usually fails in exactly that gap: one `Set-Cookie` attribute in a different order, one unexpected field, and the tool never works in production while every unit test passes. So the **shipped binary** was driven with its own global `fetch` over **real TLS** against the **real mounted application** and a real PostgreSQL 17. The composed application was served on `https://localhost` using a self-signed certificate generated by `openssl` outside the repository, port 443 being required because the CLI refuses an explicit port and bindable on this host without elevation, and a genuinely minted OIDC token was piped into `dist/src/publish-definition.js`.

That single run settled several claims at once. It exited 1, wrote one complete bounded JSON document to standard output and nothing to standard error, and reported `sessionCleanup: confirmed`. The real login, the real CSRF exchange, the real publication route and the real logout all round-tripped. The real publisher's diagnostics survived the strict parser. The database afterwards held zero `administrator_sessions` rows, so the logout genuinely revoked, and exactly one `failed` attempt with failure code `TENANT_PUBLICATION_NOT_CONNECTED`. The access token appeared nowhere in the output. The ADR's claim that a result other than published is still written as one bounded document with a non-zero exit code is therefore measured rather than read. The cookie contract was also compared directly against the server's own serialisation and matches byte for byte, so the client is not dead on arrival.

With the real session and publication routes mounted on a hardened host, every outcome branch was driven through the actual handler rather than a canned body. A successful publication yields `published` with the digest, the already-committed flag and the request count intact and cleanup confirmed. A rejected definition yields `rejected` with parsed diagnostics. A 409 after a recorded partial yields `partial`, a 409 with no partial recorded yields `unresolved`, and a publisher that throws yields `unresolved`; all three direct the operator to reconcile. The real publisher against an unconnected tenant yields `rejected` with its real diagnostic. No outcome anywhere in the matrix contains the word retry, and every uncertain case asks only for reconciliation or for inspection of publication history.

One structural property is worth recording in the unit's favour, and it was found by attempting to use it. The `publisher` field on the publication route dependencies is documented as a test seam, and `registerPublicationSurface` enforces an exact ten-key dependency set, so a publisher cannot be injected through the production composition at all: passing it to `createApp` is refused with `PUBLICATION_SURFACE_CONFIGURATION`. That is the per-adapter composition guard from PR #103 doing precisely its job, and it is why the outcome matrix above had to be driven by registering routes directly.

Credential discipline was measured on the real round trip rather than argued. Exactly four requests are made, in order, and the bearer token appears in exactly one of them, the login, and in no request body. The publish body is exactly the form version identifier and nothing else. No request carries a tenant, organisation or namespace. The returned document contains neither the access token, nor the session cookie value, nor the CSRF token.

The input surface is closed. Extra `tenantId`, `expectedSalesforceOrgId`, `namespacePrefix` and `refreshToken` keys are each refused, as are a non-HTTPS origin, an origin with a path, embedded credentials or an explicit port, a token that is not JWT-shaped, and null-prototype or array inputs. Standard input refuses oversized documents, duplicate keys, trailing data, bare values, empty input, deep nesting and malformed UTF-8. The compiled executable refuses any positional argument and refuses empty input, in each case emitting only the fixed failure message with an empty standard output and no echo of the credential.

Twenty-two hostile transport cases all land on a safe outcome, including an unknown extra property, a duplicate key, a declared content length above the cap, a seventy kilobyte streamed body with no declared length, a missing or wrong content type, malformed UTF-8 in the body, an unmapped status and a server error carrying a plausible success body. Each becomes `transport_unresolved` asking only for inspection. Both deadline paths are enforced and were measured to the millisecond: a login transport that never settles is refused after 10001 milliseconds, and a CSRF body that stalls after its headers arrive is refused after 10003 milliseconds. The second matters more than the first, because it proves the deadline remains armed while the response body is being read rather than only until headers arrive. Seven malformed session cookie variants are refused, including a missing `Secure`, a missing `HttpOnly`, `SameSite=None`, an out-of-range maximum age and a different cookie name. Cleanup cannot hide a known outcome: with logout throwing, returning a server error, or returning the wrong success status, the publication stays `published` and cleanup is reported `unconfirmed`. The declared CSRF gap behaves exactly as declared, with a CSRF failure after a successful login refusing the whole run and attempting neither a publication nor a logout, the server-side session being left to expire under the bounded lifetime that the cookie validation pins between sixty and eighty-six thousand four hundred seconds.

The builder's own mutation evidence is accurate: removing the abort call from the deadline fails the test named for bounding a transport that ignores abort, with exactly the reported `false !== true`.

The trust boundary did not move. All three trust-boundary trees are byte-identical to accepted main and the harness pin appears exactly twice. The manifest change was declared and its substance verified: `service/package.json` adds exactly one line, the named script `publish-definition`, which is not a lifecycle hook such as `preinstall`, `postinstall` or `prepare` and is therefore inert unless deliberately invoked; both lockfiles, both dependency blocks and the Salesforce project manifest are untouched. This is the class of change note 115 asked to be declared, and it was declared with its reason. The threat model is one of the four critical documents, so it was checked mechanically: all fourteen existing rows are byte-identical with exactly one row added, for command-line credential leakage and unsafe publication retry, and no control was weakened.

Run evidence was bound to the exact head. All three required contexts pass and the scratch org job is skipped holding zero steps. The gate printed that Apex did not run because the trusted Salesforce tree is identical to accepted main `bb9549c` at reviewed head `cb7b61f`, the fifth live firing of the ADR 0051 Salesforce-identical path. Seven distinct forty-character hexadecimal identifiers appear and all are accounted for. Credential hygiene returned zero matches. The Dev Hub, queried with explicit UTC bounds, holds no scratch org created since 21:00 on the twenty-eighth while the gate ran at 05:48 on the twenty-ninth, so nothing was created by this run.

`PASS: PR #111 merged as 53ad92e`, with squash tree `0e75bb7` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 09:03. All checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. The baseline does not move with this unit.

Two defects in the verifier's own work are disclosed. A scripted edit to a probe reported success while its replacement had silently matched nothing, so a later section ran with an undefined variable; the success message had been printed without asserting that the replacement took effect, which is the trap the verifier's own standing notes warn about, and the remedy was to use an editing tool that fails loudly. Separately, one probe section attempted to clean up attempt rows between rounds with a direct delete, which migration 0011's retention trigger correctly refused with a check violation; that was the PR #105 guard working against the verifier, and the cleanup was removed rather than worked around.

Ledger: zero findings and three informational notes. Note 123 records that the deadline timer is unreferenced, so it cannot keep the process alive by itself. Both halves were measured: with any live handle the deadline fires correctly, and with a transport holding no handles the process exits with code zero and no output, which for a continuous-integration publish step is the worst available failure mode. It is not reachable through the shipped command, because the entry point calls the client with no transport argument and therefore uses global fetch, which always holds a socket or name-resolution handle, and there is no argument or standard-input path by which a transport could be supplied; so this is a note rather than a finding. Setting a failing exit code at the start of the entry point and clearing it only on a confirmed published result would make a silent exit a failure by default. Note 124 records that the same class of failure reports in two different shapes depending on when it happens: an authentication refusal at login produces only the fixed failure message with empty standard output, confirmed with the real binary and a corrupted token signature, while an authentication refusal on the publication request produces structured output with a non-zero exit code; both are correct and the pre-result behaviour is declared deliberately, but an operator whose workload credential has just rotated sees the same message as one whose service is unreachable, and a distinct exit code for a pre-result failure would separate them without disclosing anything. Note 125 records that the client imports its route paths from the server, which is good and prevents drift, but states the session cookie contract as a literal regular expression requiring an exact attribute order; it matches the server today, which was verified, but nothing pins the two together and a future change to attribute order would break the client with only the fixed failure message to go on, which is the same shape as notes 116 and 118. Note 39 remains open. Notes 34, 39, 117 to 125 remain open or informational. Note 34 is advanced again and still not closed, the publish-CLI strand now being closed and the remaining strands being invitation delivery and listing, scheduled reconciliation and retention, unlinked Salesforce File cleanup, privileged retry, disconnect and rebind, and the ODK Validate runtime assets in the service image. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state, namespaced-behaviour or Phase 1 completion claim is made, and the change correctly claimed none; C10 test 12 and the seven supplied forms through a real continuous-integration workload identity remain to be exercised.

## 2026-10-29: PR #113 hardens the publish CLI executable contract (ADR 0058); 0 findings, notes 123 to 125 CLOSED, notes 126 and 127, PASS at `c1dafd4`

One commit `c1dafd4` on accepted main `09ade40`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, acts on all three notes raised against PR #111. Twelve files changed, 747 insertions and 55 deletions, comprising a fail-closed executable exit contract, a new shared administrator session cookie module used by both the server and the client, a 388-line PostgreSQL integration test that launches the compiled command through real TLS against the real mounted application, a test-stage-only OpenSSL installation in the service image, ADR 0058, and architecture, roadmap, licence-inventory and runbook changes. The unit claims no C10 acceptance test. Local evidence: service unit 408 of 408, root 283 of 283, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and whitespace clean, every figure the handover declared. The verifier's own integration run was stronger than the builder's: the builder had no local database and skipped ten cases, while this review ran 11 of 11 with zero skips against real PostgreSQL 17, including the new command-line test.

Note 123 is closed and the closure is unusually satisfying, because the repository now contains a test that reproduces the exact failure the note described. The note recorded that the deadline timer was unreferenced, so it could not keep the process alive by itself, and that a transport holding no live handle would therefore let the process exit with code zero and no output, which for a continuous-integration publish step is the worst available failure mode. The executable now sets its exit code to two before it parses input or starts any asynchronous work, and only one complete structured result may replace that default. Removing that single line and running the builder's focused test produces `0 !== 2` with an actual of zero and an expected of two, so the silent green exit is reproduced by the test and defeated by the fix.

Note 124 is closed, and the whole contract was proven through the shipped binary rather than through the library. The note recorded that the same class of failure reported in two different shapes depending on when it happened, giving an operator no way to distinguish a rotated credential from an unreachable service. Exit codes are now zero for a published result with confirmed cleanup, one for a bounded structured non-success written to standard output, and two for any failure before a safe result. All three were measured by running the compiled command with its own global fetch over real TLS against the real composed application and a real database. A genuine published result returned exit zero, which had not been demonstrated anywhere in this project before, with the database then holding one succeeded attempt carrying the expected digest and request count and zero live sessions, so the real logout had revoked. A real rejection through the production surface returned exit one with the document still written to standard output. A corrupted token signature, an unreachable origin, absent standard input, a positional argument and malformed input each returned exit two with the fixed message and an empty standard output.

Note 125 is closed. The session cookie now has one shared contract owning the name, value shape, lifetime bounds, serialiser and parser, imported by both the browser route and the command-line client. Fifty-three independent assertions hold. All one hundred and twenty permutations of the five security attributes are accepted, which is precisely the coupling the note described, so a server-side reorder can no longer break the client. Twenty-seven drift cases are refused: a missing secure, HTTP-only, path or maximum-age attribute; the strict and none same-site values; a narrowed path; a lowercase attribute name; a duplicated attribute; two maximum-age attributes; an additional priority attribute; a domain attribute, which the host prefix forbids; lifetimes below sixty or above eighty-six thousand four hundred seconds; negative and non-numeric lifetimes; wrong value lengths and non-base64url values; a different cookie name; a missing host prefix; two cookies joined into one header value; and attribute separators without spaces. The serialiser refuses to emit anything weaker, and the single permitted empty form is the clearing cookie, which the parser then correctly refuses to read back as a session. Both boundary lifetimes round-trip. The review also established that the session cookie has exactly one emitter: the OAuth routes emit a cookie twice, which was chased, and both emissions are that module's own authorization state binding rather than the administrator session.

The container change was the part of the declaration the review trusted least, so it was measured by building both targets rather than by reading the stage graph. The base Node image carries no OpenSSL command-line tool at all and no OpenSSL library package. The test target carries OpenSSL 3.0.22 at the expected path. The default runtime target carries no command-line tool, no path entry and no package, and a filesystem-wide search of that image for certificate and key files returned nothing. The runtime application directory contains only the compiled sources, the production dependencies and the package manifest, and the compiled directory contains only sources, so no test code ships either. The declaration is therefore accurate: the runtime stage starts from a fresh base and copies three paths from the build stage, none of which carry the installed package. The licence inventory's new row was also confirmed from the installed package's own copyright file rather than from memory, which reports the Apache 2.0 licence that the row states, and all fourteen existing licence rows are byte-identical once table padding is normalised.

Three independent mutations established that the new tests are not vacuous. Removing the initial failing exit state fails the focused no-live-handle test. Renaming the cookie in the shared module alone fails twenty-one unit tests. Dropping the secure attribute from the serialiser fails the new real-TLS integration test, which is the most valuable of the three because it proves that test detects a genuine security drift between server and client across a real wire rather than passing because it never looks.

The integration test's technique is better than the one the verifier built for PR #111 and is recorded here so the better method is the one that survives. Rather than binding the privileged port 443, the test runs a local CONNECT proxy on an ephemeral port and points the child process's proxy environment at it, passing the flag that makes Node honour those variables. The client still sees an origin with no explicit port, so its own origin validation passes, and nothing requires a privileged bind or risks a port collision. Certificates are generated into a temporary directory and removed recursively in a finally block, so no key material persists, and a search of the hosted logs specifically for certificate and private-key headers found none.

The trust boundary did not move. All three trust-boundary trees are byte-identical to accepted main, the harness pin appears exactly twice, and every npm manifest and both lockfiles are unchanged, which was verified by path rather than inferred. The only manifest-adjacent change is the service container definition, declared with its reason and verified above.

Run evidence was bound to the exact head. All three required contexts pass and the scratch org job is skipped holding zero steps. The gate printed that Apex did not run because the trusted Salesforce tree is identical to accepted main `09ade40` at reviewed head `c1dafd4`, the sixth live firing of the ADR 0051 Salesforce-identical path. Seven distinct forty-character hexadecimal identifiers appear and all are accounted for. Credential hygiene returned zero matches, including a search for certificate material added for this unit. Hosted continuous integration ran the unit suite at 408 of 408 twice and the integration suite at 11 of 11 with zero skips twice, once on the host and once inside the container, and both container builds completed. The Dev Hub, queried with explicit UTC bounds, holds no scratch org created at any point on the twenty-ninth.

ADR 0058 is accurate against the code on every point tested, and it states plainly that review found the three gaps and that review had to construct the missing end-to-end probe outside the repository. Recording that in the decision record rather than presenting the work as self-originated is the right call and is noted approvingly.

`PASS: PR #113 merged as 0bbfe60`, with squash tree `8752b67` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 11:26. All checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. The baseline does not move with this unit.

Ledger: zero findings, three notes closed and two informational notes raised. Note 126 records that the shared contract covers emission and response-cookie parsing but not request-cookie reading: six modules still declare the cookie name themselves and five also carry their own value pattern, namely the three publication route modules, the invitation and provisioning route modules and the OAuth routes. Those modules only read the request cookie header, so a divergence would fail closed with an authentication refusal rather than admitting anything, and the divergence was then tested rather than assumed by renaming the cookie in the shared module alone, which failed twenty-one unit tests loudly. That makes it a maintenance wart rather than a latent hazard, and it corrects the verifier's own earlier phrasing: notes 116, 118 and 125 each asserted that nothing pinned a duplicated literal without that claim having been tested, and here the tests did pin it, so notes 116 and 118 deserve the same mutation before their wording is trusted. Note 127 records that the integration test's OpenSSL override environment variable is undocumented, defaulting to the Git for Windows path on Windows and to the command on the path elsewhere, which is the right shape but appears in neither the runbook nor the licence note; the cost is low because the failure is self-diagnosing, reporting a spawn error that names the missing path, so a one-line runbook mention would close it. Note 39 remains open. Notes 34, 39, 117 to 120, 126 and 127 remain open or informational. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state, namespaced-behaviour or Phase 1 completion claim is made, and the change correctly claimed none; C10 test 12 and the seven supplied forms through a selected real workload identity remain outstanding.

## 2026-10-29: PR #115 shares the administrator session cookie readers (ADR 0058 amendment); 0 findings, notes 126 and 127 CLOSED, note 128, PASS at `acb9d78`

Two commits on accepted main `fb78032`, authored and committed by Bill Owiti, fold every administrator request-cookie reader into the single shared contract introduced by PR #113 and document the integration test's OpenSSL override. Twelve files changed, 111 insertions and 93 deletions, a net gain of eighteen lines for the removal of seven duplicated readers. The unit claims no C10 acceptance test and no phase gate. Local evidence: service unit 409 of 409, root 283 of 283, lint, typecheck, Prettier, scaffold and whitespace clean, every figure the handover declared. The verifier's integration run was again stronger than the builder's, who had no local database available: 11 of 11 with zero skips against real PostgreSQL 17.

A refactor's only real question is whether behaviour moved, and reading a diff cannot answer it. So the real composed application was built twice, once from accepted main in a separate git worktree and once from the reviewed head, and an identical matrix was run against both: nine endpoints that read the administrator session cookie, across twenty-one Cookie-header variants, comparing status code and response body. The variants covered an absent header, a genuine live session, a well-shaped but unknown value, lengths of forty-two, forty-four, one hundred, five hundred and twelve and five hundred and thirteen, illegal characters, an empty value, duplication with identical and with differing values, three malformed syntaxes, an unrelated cookie alone and in both orders, whitespace around the name, the Salesforce OAuth binding cookie alone, and both cookies together. **On all seven mounted endpoints the responses are byte-identical between accepted main and the reviewed head**, one hundred and forty-seven comparisons once the per-run CSRF token is normalised. The refactor is therefore invisible at the production boundary, which is the claim this unit needed.

The matrix was also partly vacuous, and that is recorded because the omission would have inflated the result. The two Salesforce OAuth endpoints returned not-found for all twenty-one variants in both builds, because the route registration function is exported but never called anywhere in the service sources, which was verified by search. That is ADR 0041 working as designed, and it means forty-two of the one hundred and eighty-nine rows compared nothing about the code this unit changed. Reporting one hundred and eighty-nine clean comparisons would have been wrong. Those routes were therefore registered directly on a hardened host from each build and the same variants replayed, which is where the one behaviour difference lives and is recorded as note 128.

Note 126 is closed. The inventory claim is exact: searching the service sources for the cookie name and for the forty-three-character value pattern finds each declared in exactly one place, the shared cookie module. All three publication route modules, the session, invitation and provisioning routes, and both Salesforce OAuth boundaries now import the shared reader, with a route-local wrapper preserving each endpoint's existing generic error shape, which the differential matrix confirms byte for byte. The shared reader is also stricter than the seven it replaces: the old readers validated syntax and duplicates but left the value shape to each caller, whereas the shared one rejects an invalid opaque value inside the reader and rejects a non-string header explicitly rather than relying on a type error. The declared mutation evidence is accurate to the number: renaming the cookie centrally while leaving the clients unchanged yields four hundred and nine tests with three hundred and sixty-eight passing and forty-one failing, exactly the figure declared.

Note 127 is closed. The service runbook now documents the OpenSSL override environment variable, the path behaviour and the Windows fallback, and the wording matches the integration test's own default precisely: the override first, then the Git for Windows path on Windows, otherwise the command on the path.

The handling of the first head is recorded approvingly. That head failed the required scaffold and lint check, which was confirmed independently from its own check run, and the cause was the repository formatting check. The builder did not re-run the failed check. A second commit fixed the formatting, and that commit was verified to be formatting only: two line-wrapping changes in one file with identical semantics and nothing else in the diff. Declaring the failure in the handover rather than hoping it would pass unnoticed is the discipline this project asked for after issue #108, where the temptation was to retry a red check instead of reading it.

The trust boundary did not move. All three trust-boundary trees are byte-identical to accepted main, the harness pin appears exactly twice, and every npm manifest, both lockfiles and the service container definition are unchanged, verified by path. Run evidence was bound to the exact head: all three required contexts pass, the scratch org job is skipped holding zero steps, and the supersede workflow fired for the replaced head. The gate printed that Apex did not run because the trusted Salesforce tree is identical to accepted main `fb78032` at reviewed head `acb9d78`, the seventh live firing of the ADR 0051 Salesforce-identical path. Seven distinct forty-character hexadecimal identifiers appear and all are accounted for. Credential hygiene returned zero matches, including certificate and private-key headers. The Dev Hub, queried with explicit UTC bounds, holds no scratch org created at any point on the twenty-ninth.

`PASS: PR #115 merged as 1d449fa`, with squash tree `108081a` identical to the verified head tree, merged at 13:05. All checks were green with the three required contexts satisfied and a clean merge state. The baseline does not move with this unit.

One defect in the verifier's own work is disclosed. The OAuth comparison first crashed in both builds because the OAuth configuration shape was guessed rather than read, inventing three fields the real interface does not declare while omitting the two it requires. Both builds failed identically, which resembled symmetry but was two vacuous runs; reading the interface fixed it. That is the standing trap about reading a contract before writing a fake, and it is at least the third time it has been tripped. Separately, the first count of failures under the rename mutation was eighty-three, which was the verifier's own grep counting each failure twice, from the inline report and from the summary, plus the summary header; the reporter's own total of forty-one is correct and matches what the builder declared, and the correction was made before reporting rather than raised as a discrepancy.

Ledger: zero findings, two notes closed and one informational note raised. Note 128 records that the Salesforce OAuth routes previously validated the administrator cookie against a forty-three to five-hundred-and-twelve character pattern while the shared reader requires exactly forty-three, so sixteen of thirty responses on those two endpoints change from an authentication refusal to a request-rejected refusal. It is not a finding for four measured reasons: those routes are unmounted and the production surface returns not-found for both paths in both builds; both outcomes are refusals, so nothing is admitted that was not admitted before; the accepted set only narrowed, and the session store issues exactly forty-three characters, so no legitimate session can be rejected; and no oracle is created, because the caller already knows the shape of the cookie it sent. What is worth recording is the claim rather than the code: the handover states that the unit closes the notes without changing product behaviour, which is slightly too strong, whereas the ADR amendment's narrower statement that the changes do not alter the CLI, route authority or acceptance-test claims is accurate, and route authority is indeed untouched. Whoever later mounts the Salesforce OAuth surface should read this note first, because at that point the difference becomes reachable and someone must decide whether a malformed cookie deserves a four hundred or a four hundred and one. Note 39 remains open. Notes 34, 39, 117 to 120 and 128 remain open or informational. No C10 acceptance test, publish-ready state, namespaced-behaviour or Phase 1 completion claim is made, and the change correctly claimed none; C10 test 12 and the seven supplied forms through a selected real workload identity remain outstanding.
